# RFC 2616 (HTTP/1.1, June 1999) -- Normative Facts Catalog [OBSOLETE: superseded by RFC 9110/9112]

_Generated from local RFC source via FABLE extraction + double independent Opus verification. Every quote is verbatim from the fetched RFC text and was confirmed against the source by two independent verifiers (default-refute). Rows flagged **VERIFY** had a verifier disagreement and need a manual source check._

### 1 - Introduction, Requirements keywords, Terminology, Overall Operation

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `1-1` | informative | All capitalized requirement keywords in RFC 2616 carry RFC 2119 semantics. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The key words "MUST", "MUST NOT", "REQUIRED", "SHALL", "SHALL NOT", "SHOULD", "SHOULD NOT", "RECOMMENDED", "MAY", and "OPTIONAL" in this document are to be interpreted as described in RFC 2119 | Interpret every keyword in the conformance checklist per RFC 2119; MUST rows are hard gates, SHOULD rows need documented justification to skip. |
| `1-2` | informative | Failing any single MUST/REQUIRED level requirement makes the implementation non-compliant. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An implementation is not compliant if it fails to satisfy one or more of the MUST or REQUIRED level requirements for the protocols it implements. | Track every client-scoped MUST/REQUIRED row as blocking; a single unmet one means the http1 package cannot claim HTTP/1.1 compliance. |
| `1-3` | informative | Satisfying all MUST/REQUIRED plus all SHOULD requirements earns the label 'unconditionally compliant'. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An implementation that satisfies all the MUST or REQUIRED level and all the SHOULD level requirements for its protocols is said to be "unconditionally compliant" | Audit SHOULD-level rows separately; only if all pass may docs claim unconditional compliance. |
| `1-4` | informative | Satisfying all MUSTs but not all SHOULDs yields only 'conditionally compliant' status. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | one that satisfies all the MUST level requirements but not all the SHOULD level requirements for its protocols is said to be "conditionally compliant." | If any SHOULD is deliberately skipped (e.g., no caching), document the claim level as conditionally compliant. |
| `1-5` | informative | Every HTTP message must be a structured octet sequence matching the section 4 generic-message syntax. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The basic unit of HTTP communication, consisting of a structured sequence of octets matching the syntax defined in section 4 and transmitted via the connection. | Both the request serializer and the response parser must conform to the section 4 message grammar (start-line, headers, CRLF, body). |
| `1-6` | informative | An entity is structured as entity-header fields (metainformation) plus an entity-body (content), per section 7. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An entity consists of metainformation in the form of entity-header fields and content in the form of an entity-body, as described in section 7. | Model request/response payloads as entity-headers + entity-body; keep entity-header handling distinct from general/request/response headers. |
| `1-7` | informative | 'Client' means the program that establishes connections in order to send requests. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A program that establishes connections for the purpose of sending requests. | This library is the 'client' role in every later rule; all client-scoped requirements in the RFC bind poseidon-http-client. |
| `1-8` | informative | 'User agent' means the client that originates (rather than forwards) a request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The client which initiates a request. These are often browsers, editors, spiders (web-traversing robots), or other end user tools. | The library also fills the user-agent role (it originates requests, never forwards), so user-agent-scoped rules bind it too. |
| `1-9` | informative | Client/server designations describe the role played on a particular connection, not the program overall. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | our use of these terms refers only to the role being performed by the program for a particular connection, rather than to the program's capabilities in general. | Apply role-scoped requirements per connection: on every connection the library opens it is bound by client rules regardless of anything else the host program does. |
| `1-10` | MUST | A proxy must implement both the client-side and server-side requirements of the specification. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST implement both the client and server requirements of this specification. | No client obligation; relevant only if the library is ever embedded in a proxy, in which case its client half must be fully conformant. |
| `1-11` | informative | Proxy requirements apply to transparent and non-transparent proxies alike unless a rule says otherwise. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Except where either transparent or non-transparent behavior is explicitly stated, the HTTP proxy requirements apply to both types of proxies. | No client action; when reading later proxy-scoped rows, do not narrow them to one proxy type. |
| `1-12` | informative | A gateway presents itself as the origin server; the requesting client may be unable to tell the difference. <br>_(gateway [not-client])_ | a gateway receives requests as if it were the origin server for the requested resource; the requesting client may not be aware that it is communicating with a gateway. | No special client handling: the client must work identically whether the peer is an origin server or a gateway. |
| `1-13` | informative | Once active, a tunnel is a blind relay and not a party to the HTTP communication. <br>_(tunnel [not-client])_ | Once active, a tunnel is not considered a party to the HTTP communication, though the tunnel may have been initiated by an HTTP request. | No client obligation here; matters later for CONNECT semantics — after tunnel establishment, bytes are opaque to HTTP. |
| `1-14` | informative | Any client or server is permitted to include a cache, but a server acting as a tunnel cannot use one. <br>_(client,server,cache,tunnel [CLIENT])_ | Any client or server may include a cache, though a cache cannot be used by a server that is acting as a tunnel. | Caching is optional for the client — poseidon-http-client having no cache is conformant; if a cache is ever added, section 13 rules attach. |
| `1-15` | informative | A response is 'cacheable' only if a cache is allowed to store it for answering subsequent requests. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A response is cacheable if a cache is allowed to store a copy of the response message for use in answering subsequent requests. | No obligation for a cache-less client; becomes load-bearing only if response caching is added. |
| `1-16` | informative | All messages flow from upstream to downstream; the terms define message-flow direction. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Upstream and downstream describe the flow of a message: all messages flow from upstream to downstream. | Use this vocabulary when interpreting later rules (e.g., Via, Upgrade): for responses the server is upstream of the client. |
| `1-17` | informative | 'Inbound' means traveling toward the origin server; 'outbound' means traveling toward the user agent. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | "inbound" means "traveling toward the origin server", and "outbound" means "traveling toward the user agent" | Requests the client sends are inbound; responses it receives are outbound — needed to read chain-scoped rules correctly. |
| `1-18` | informative | A client's request consists of a request method, URI, and protocol version, followed by a MIME-like message (headers, optional body). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client sends a request to the server in the form of a request method, URI, and protocol version, followed by a MIME-like message | Request serializer must emit method + request-URI + HTTP-version request line, then header fields and optional body — verify http1 writer produces exactly this shape. |
| `1-19` | informative | A response consists of a status line carrying protocol version and a status code, followed by a MIME-like message (headers, optional body). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server responds with a status line, including the message's protocol version and a success or error code, followed by a MIME-like message | Response parser must accept status-line (version + code) then headers and optional entity-body; it must parse the version and code fields, not just skip the line. |
| `1-20` | informative | HTTP communication options differ in scope: some bind only the nearest non-tunnel hop, some only chain end-points, some every connection in the chain. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | some HTTP communication options may apply only to the connection with the nearest, non-tunnel neighbor, only to the end-points of the chain, or to all connections along the chain. | Client must distinguish hop-by-hop options (e.g., Connection, TE) from end-to-end ones when emitting headers and interpreting responses. |
| `1-21` | informative | The default HTTP port is TCP 80; other ports are permitted. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The default port is TCP 80 [19], but other ports can be used. | When a URL omits the port, the client dialer must default to port 80 (http scheme); explicit ports must be honored. |
| `1-22` | informative | HTTP presumes only a reliable transport; any protocol providing such guarantees may carry it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP only presumes a reliable transport; any protocol that provides such guarantees can be used | Client must run HTTP/1.1 over a reliable, ordered byte stream (TCP or TLS-over-TCP); no obligation to support unreliable transports. |
| `1-23` | informative | In HTTP/1.1 a single connection may carry one or more request/response exchanges, and connections may close for a variety of reasons. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In HTTP/1.1, a connection may be used for one or more request/response exchanges, although connections may be closed for a variety of reasons | Client must support reusing a connection for multiple exchanges (persistence is the HTTP/1.1 default) and tolerate connection close at any point per section 8.1. |

### 2 - RFC 2616 Section 2 — ABNF notation and basic grammar rules conformance facts

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `2-1` | informative | Grammar rule names are written bare (without angle brackets) and separated from their definition by the '=' character. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | The name of a rule is simply the name itself (without any enclosing "<" and ">") and is separated from its definition by the equal "=" character. | Spec-reading notation only; no direct code check. |
| `2-2` | informative | Quoted literal text in the grammar is case-insensitive unless a rule states otherwise. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Quotation marks surround literal text. Unless stated otherwise, the text is case-insensitive. | Parser must match grammar literals case-insensitively (e.g. 'chunked', 'Keep-Alive') except where another section declares case-sensitivity. |
| `2-3` | informative | A bar '\|' between grammar elements denotes alternatives. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Elements separated by a bar ("\|") are alternatives, e.g., "yes \| no" will accept yes or no. | Notation only; applied when implementing each concrete grammar rule. |
| `2-4` | informative | Elements enclosed in parentheses are treated as a single grouped element. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Elements enclosed in parentheses are treated as a single element. | Notation only; no direct code check. |
| `2-5` | informative | '*' denotes repetition: <n>*<m>element means at least n and at most m occurrences, with defaults 0 and infinity. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | The full form is "<n>*<m>element" indicating at least <n> and at most <m> occurrences of element. | Notation only; honor min/max occurrence counts when implementing each rule. |
| `2-6` | informative | Square brackets enclose optional elements, equivalent to *1(...). <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Square brackets enclose optional elements; "[foo bar]" is equivalent to "*1(foo bar)". | Notation only; no direct code check. |
| `2-7` | informative | N(element) means exactly N occurrences of the element (e.g. 2DIGIT is a two-digit number). <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Specific repetition: "<n>(element)" is equivalent to "<n>*<n>(element)"; that is, exactly <n> occurrences of (element). | Notation only; enforce exact counts where rules use it (e.g. HTTP-date parsing). |
| `2-8` | OPTIONAL | The '#' construct defines lists whose elements are separated by one or more commas and optional linear white space. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The full form is "<n>#<m>element" indicating at least <n> and at most <m> elements, each separated by one or more commas (",") and OPTIONAL linear white space (LWS). | List-valued header parser must split on commas and tolerate arbitrary LWS around elements; emitter separates elements with ','. |
| `2-9` | informative | Null (empty) elements are permitted in #-lists but do not contribute to the count of elements present. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Wherever this construct is used, null elements are allowed, but do not contribute to the count of elements present. | When parsing list headers, skip empty elements ('elem,,elem' counts as two) instead of erroring or producing empty entries. |
| `2-10` | MUST | Where a list rule requires at least one element, at least one non-null element must actually be present. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Therefore, where at least one element is required, at least one non-null element MUST be present. | Treat a 1#-required header containing only null elements as invalid/absent; never emit a required list with only empty elements. |
| `2-11` | informative | A semicolon set off to the right of rule text begins a grammar comment running to end of line. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | A semi-colon, set off some distance to the right of rule text, starts a comment that continues to the end of line. | Spec-reading notation only; no code check. |
| `2-12` | informative | Implied *LWS: linear white space may appear between any two adjacent words or between words and separators without changing a field's interpretation. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Except where noted otherwise, linear white space (LWS) can be included between any two adjacent words (token or quoted-string), and between adjacent words and separators, without changing the interpretation of a field. | Field-value parser must tolerate LWS between tokens, quoted-strings and separators everywhere the grammar does not forbid it. |
| `2-13` | MUST | At least one delimiter (LWS and/or a separator) must separate any two tokens, otherwise they would parse as a single token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | At least one delimiter (LWS and/or separators) MUST exist between any two tokens | Emitter must never concatenate two tokens without a delimiter; tokenizer treats an unbroken run of token characters as one token. |
| `2-14` | informative | Protocol characters are drawn from the US-ASCII coded character set (ANSI X3.4-1986). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The US-ASCII coded character set is defined by ANSI X3.4-1986 [21]. | Decode/encode start-line and header protocol elements as US-ASCII; character classes below are byte-value based. |
| `2-15` | informative | OCTET is any 8-bit sequence of data. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | OCTET = <any 8-bit sequence of data> | Treat entity-body content as arbitrary opaque 8-bit octets. |
| `2-16` | informative | CHAR is any US-ASCII character, octets 0-127. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | CHAR = <any US-ASCII character (octets 0 - 127)> | CHAR class = bytes 0x00-0x7F; underlies token and quoted-pair definitions. |
| `2-17` | informative | UPALPHA is any US-ASCII uppercase letter A through Z. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | UPALPHA = <any US-ASCII uppercase letter "A".."Z"> | Lexer character class: 0x41-0x5A. |
| `2-18` | informative | LOALPHA is any US-ASCII lowercase letter a through z. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | LOALPHA = <any US-ASCII lowercase letter "a".."z"> | Lexer character class: 0x61-0x7A. |
| `2-19` | informative | ALPHA is UPALPHA or LOALPHA. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | ALPHA = UPALPHA \| LOALPHA | Lexer character class: A-Z plus a-z. |
| `2-20` | informative | DIGIT is any US-ASCII digit 0 through 9. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | DIGIT = <any US-ASCII digit "0".."9"> | Lexer character class: 0x30-0x39; used by status codes, Content-Length, versions. |
| `2-21` | informative | CTL is any US-ASCII control character (octets 0-31) or DEL (127). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | CTL = <any US-ASCII control character (octets 0 - 31) and DEL (127)> | Classify bytes 0x00-0x1F and 0x7F as CTL; exclude them from token and TEXT. |
| `2-22` | informative | CR is the US-ASCII carriage return character (13). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | CR = <US-ASCII CR, carriage return (13)> | Constant 0x0D in line-terminator handling. |
| `2-23` | informative | LF is the US-ASCII linefeed character (10). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | LF = <US-ASCII LF, linefeed (10)> | Constant 0x0A in line-terminator handling. |
| `2-24` | informative | SP is the US-ASCII space character (32). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | SP = <US-ASCII SP, space (32)> | Constant 0x20; single SP separates request-line/status-line parts and is folding-equivalent whitespace. |
| `2-25` | informative | HT is the US-ASCII horizontal-tab character (9). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HT = <US-ASCII HT, horizontal-tab (9)> | Constant 0x09; valid LWS/folding character alongside SP. |
| `2-26` | informative | <"> is the US-ASCII double-quote mark (34). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | <"> = <US-ASCII double-quote mark (34)> | Constant 0x22; delimits quoted-string. |
| `2-27` | informative | The sequence CR LF is the end-of-line marker for every protocol element except the entity-body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 defines the sequence CR LF as the end-of-line marker for all protocol elements except the entity-body | Terminate request-line, status-line and each header field on CRLF when emitting and parsing (appendix 19.3 covers tolerant bare-LF acceptance). |
| `2-28` | informative | The end-of-line marker inside an entity-body is defined by the associated media type, not by HTTP framing. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The end-of-line marker within an entity-body is defined by its associated media type, as described in section 3.7. | Never apply CRLF line logic to body octets; frame the body solely by length/transfer-coding and pass bytes through unmodified. |
| `2-29` | informative | CRLF is the two-character sequence CR followed by LF. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | CRLF = CR LF | Emit exactly 0x0D 0x0A as the line terminator for all protocol elements. |
| `2-30` | informative | Header field values may be folded onto multiple lines when each continuation line begins with a space or horizontal tab. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 header field values can be folded onto multiple lines if the continuation line begins with a space or horizontal tab. | Response header parser must recognize a line starting with SP/HT as a continuation of the previous field value (obs-fold) and unfold it. |
| `2-31` | informative | All linear white space, including folding, has the same semantics as a single SP. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All linear white space, including folding, has the same semantics as SP. | Treat any LWS run (including CRLF+SP/HT folds) in a field value as semantically equivalent to one space. |
| `2-32` | MAY | A recipient may replace any linear white space with a single SP before interpreting or forwarding the field value. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient MAY replace any linear white space with a single SP before interpreting the field value or forwarding the message downstream. | Collapsing LWS to one SP during header normalization is permitted (not required); verify doing so never changes parsed semantics. |
| `2-33` | informative | LWS is an optional CRLF followed by one or more SP or HT characters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | LWS = [CRLF] 1*( SP \| HT ) | Lexer rule for linear white space: optional CRLF then at least one SP/HT; CRLF alone (not followed by SP/HT) is a line terminator, not LWS. |
| `2-34` | MAY | Words of *TEXT may contain characters from character sets other than ISO-8859-1 only when encoded per RFC 2047. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Words of *TEXT MAY contain characters from character sets other than ISO-8859-1 [22] only when encoded according to the rules of RFC 2047 [14]. | If the client emits non-ISO-8859-1 text in header fields it must RFC 2047-encode it; parser should tolerate RFC 2047 encoded-words in TEXT. |
| `2-35` | informative | TEXT is any octet except CTLs but including LWS, used only for descriptive field content not interpreted by the message parser. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | TEXT = <any OCTET except CTLs, but including LWS> | Accept any non-CTL octet (plus LWS) in descriptive field values; do not attempt structural parsing of TEXT content. |
| `2-36` | informative | A CRLF may appear within TEXT only as part of a header field continuation, and the folding LWS is expected to be replaced with a single SP before interpretation. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A CRLF is allowed in the definition of TEXT only as part of a header field continuation. | Reject bare CRLF inside a field value unless followed by SP/HT (folding); replace the folding LWS with one SP before interpreting the value. |
| `2-37` | informative | HEX comprises the uppercase and lowercase hexadecimal letters A-F/a-f plus DIGIT. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HEX = "A" \| "B" \| "C" \| "D" \| "E" \| "F" \| "a" \| "b" \| "c" \| "d" \| "e" \| "f" \| DIGIT | Parse hexadecimal protocol elements (e.g. chunk-size lines) accepting both uppercase and lowercase hex digits. |
| `2-38` | MUST | Separator characters must be enclosed in a quoted string to be used within a parameter value. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | These special characters MUST be in a quoted string to be used within a parameter value (as defined in section 3.6). | When emitting parameter values containing separator characters, wrap them in quoted-string form; never emit them bare. |
| `2-39` | informative | A token is one or more CHARs excluding CTLs and separators. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | token = 1*<any CHAR except CTLs or separators> | Token lexer: 1+ bytes from 0x00-0x7F minus CTLs minus the separator set; validate emitted methods, header names, and coding names against it. |
| `2-40` | informative | The separator characters are ( ) < > @ , ; : backslash double-quote / [ ] ? = { } SP and HT. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | separators = "(" \| ")" \| "<" \| ">" \| "@" \| "," \| ";" \| ":" \| "\" \| <"> \| "/" \| "[" \| "]" \| "?" \| "=" \| "{" \| "}" \| SP \| HT | Encode this exact 19-character separator set in the tokenizer; these bytes terminate tokens and are invalid inside token values. |
| `2-41` | informative | Parenthesized comments are allowed only in fields whose grammar includes 'comment'; in all other fields parentheses are ordinary field-value characters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Comments are only allowed in fields containing "comment" as part of their field value definition. In all other fields, parentheses are considered part of the field value. | Treat '(' as comment-start only when parsing comment-bearing fields (e.g. Via, Server, User-Agent); elsewhere keep parentheses as literal data. |
| `2-42` | informative | A comment is parenthesized text composed of ctext, quoted-pairs, and nested comments. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | comment = "(" *( ctext \| quoted-pair \| comment ) ")" | Comment parser must support nesting and backslash quoted-pair escapes, matching balanced parentheses. |
| `2-43` | informative | ctext is any TEXT excluding the '(' and ')' characters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | ctext = <any TEXT excluding "(" and ")"> | Inside comments, bare parentheses are structural; any other non-CTL octet is comment content. |
| `2-44` | informative | A string of text quoted with double-quote marks is parsed as a single word. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A string of text is parsed as a single word if it is quoted using double-quote marks. | Tokenizer must treat a double-quoted string in a field value as one word, not split it on internal separators or LWS. |
| `2-45` | informative | quoted-string is a double-quote-delimited sequence of qdtext or quoted-pair characters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | quoted-string = ( <"> *(qdtext \| quoted-pair ) <"> ) | Implement quoted-string parse/emit: opening and closing 0x22, body of qdtext or backslash-escaped pairs. |
| `2-46` | informative | qdtext is any TEXT except the double-quote mark. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | qdtext = <any TEXT except <">> | Inside quoted-string, an unescaped 0x22 terminates the string; all other non-CTL octets (and folding LWS) are literal content. |
| `2-47` | MAY | The backslash may be used as a single-character quoting mechanism only within quoted-string and comment constructs. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The backslash character ("\") MAY be used as a single-character quoting mechanism only within quoted-string and comment constructs. | Interpret backslash escapes only inside quoted-string/comment; treat '\' as an ordinary character elsewhere; use it when emitting embedded quotes. |
| `2-48` | informative | quoted-pair is a backslash followed by any CHAR, representing that character literally. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | quoted-pair = "\" CHAR | Within quoted-string/comment, consume '\' plus the next CHAR (octet 0-127) as a single escaped literal character. |

### 3.1-3.4 - Protocol Parameters: HTTP Version, URIs, Date/Time Formats, Character Sets

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `3.1-3.4-1` | informative | The HTTP version in a message's first line follows the grammar HTTP "/" 1*DIGIT "." 1*DIGIT. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP-Version = "HTTP" "/" 1*DIGIT "." 1*DIGIT | Emit exactly 'HTTP/1.1' in request lines; parse status-line versions as HTTP/ digits . digits, rejecting anything else. |
| `3.1-3.4-2` | MUST | Major and minor version numbers must be treated as two separate integers, not a decimal fraction. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the major and minor numbers MUST be treated as separate integers | Parse major and minor into separate ints; compare numerically per component (HTTP/2.4 < HTTP/2.13). |
| `3.1-3.4-3` | MAY | Each version component may exceed a single digit. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | each MAY be incremented higher than a single digit | Version parser must accept multi-digit components (e.g. HTTP/1.22, HTTP/12.3) without overflow or truncation. |
| `3.1-3.4-4` | MUST | Recipients must ignore leading zeros in version numbers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Leading zeros MUST be ignored by recipients and MUST NOT be sent. | When parsing a response status line, treat HTTP/01.01 as HTTP/1.1 (numeric parse discards leading zeros). |
| `3.1-3.4-5` | MUST NOT | Senders must not emit leading zeros in version numbers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Leading zeros MUST be ignored by recipients and MUST NOT be sent. | Serializer must never write leading zeros in the request-line HTTP-Version. |
| `3.1-3.4-6` | MUST | Any application sending a message labeled HTTP/1.1 must be at least conditionally compliant with RFC 2616. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An application that sends a request or response message that includes HTTP-Version of "HTTP/1.1" MUST be at least conditionally compliant with this specification. | Sending 'HTTP/1.1' asserts compliance: the client must implement all applicable MUSTs of the spec before advertising 1.1. |
| `3.1-3.4-7` | SHOULD | Compliant applications should label their messages HTTP/1.1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Applications that are at least conditionally compliant with this specification SHOULD use an HTTP-Version of "HTTP/1.1" in their messages | Default the request-line version to HTTP/1.1. |
| `3.1-3.4-8` | MUST | Messages that are not compatible with HTTP/1.0 must be labeled HTTP/1.1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | and MUST do so for any message that is not compatible with HTTP/1.0 | Any request using 1.1-only mechanisms (chunked body, Expect: 100-continue, etc.) must carry version HTTP/1.1. |
| `3.1-3.4-9` | informative | An application's HTTP version is the highest version for which it is at least conditionally compliant. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The HTTP version of an application is the highest HTTP version for which the application is at least conditionally compliant. | Advertise only the highest version the client actually implements; do not send a higher version number. |
| `3.1-3.4-10` | MUST NOT | A proxy/gateway must not forward a message with a version indicator greater than its own actual version. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | a proxy/gateway MUST NOT send a message with a version indicator which is greater than its actual version | No client obligation; applies only if a proxy/gateway mode is ever implemented. |
| `3.1-3.4-11` | MUST | On receiving a higher-version request, a proxy/gateway must downgrade it, respond with an error, or switch to tunnel behavior. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | the proxy/gateway MUST either downgrade the request version, or respond with an error, or switch to tunnel behavior | No client obligation; client should merely tolerate a downgraded (e.g. HTTP/1.0) response version from intermediaries. |
| `3.1-3.4-12` | MUST | Caching proxies must upgrade forwarded requests to the highest version they support. <br>_(proxy,cache [not-client])_ | caching proxies MUST, gateways MAY, and tunnels MUST NOT upgrade the request to the highest version they support | No client obligation. |
| `3.1-3.4-13` | MAY | Gateways may upgrade forwarded requests to the highest version they support. <br>_(gateway [not-client])_ | caching proxies MUST, gateways MAY, and tunnels MUST NOT upgrade the request to the highest version they support | No client obligation. |
| `3.1-3.4-14` | MUST NOT | Tunnels must not upgrade the request version. <br>_(tunnel [not-client])_ | caching proxies MUST, gateways MAY, and tunnels MUST NOT upgrade the request to the highest version they support | No client obligation. |
| `3.1-3.4-15` | MUST | A proxy/gateway response to an upgraded request must be in the same major version as the request. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | The proxy/gateway's response to that request MUST be in the same major version as the request. | No client obligation; client can rely on receiving a major version matching what it sent. |
| `3.1-3.4-16` | informative | Absolute URIs are distinguished from relative ones by beginning with a scheme name followed by a colon. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | absolute URIs always begin with a scheme name followed by a colon | URI classifier: treat input as absolute iff it starts with scheme ':' per RFC 2396; otherwise resolve relative to a base. |
| `3.1-3.4-17` | informative | HTTP adopts the URI grammar productions (URI-reference, absoluteURI, relativeURI, port, host, abs_path, rel_path, authority) from RFC 2396. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This specification adopts the definitions of "URI-reference", "absoluteURI", "relativeURI", "port", "host","abs_path", "rel_path", and "authority" from that specification. | URI parsing/serialization in the client must conform to RFC 2396 productions for these components. |
| `3.1-3.4-18` | informative | HTTP places no a priori limit on URI length. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The HTTP protocol does not place any a priori limit on the length of a URI. | Do not impose an arbitrary hard cap on URI length when emitting requests or parsing URIs (e.g. Location values). |
| `3.1-3.4-19` | MUST | Servers must be able to handle the URI of any resource they serve. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers MUST be able to handle the URI of any resource they serve | Server-side only; no client action. |
| `3.1-3.4-20` | SHOULD | Servers should handle URIs of unbounded length if they provide GET-based forms that could generate them. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | SHOULD be able to handle URIs of unbounded length if they provide GET-based forms that could generate such URIs | Server-side only; no client action. |
| `3.1-3.4-21` | SHOULD | A server should return 414 (Request-URI Too Long) if a URI exceeds what it can handle. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server SHOULD return 414 (Request-URI Too Long) status if a URI is longer than the server can handle | Binds the server; client only needs to surface a 414 status like any other response. |
| `3.1-3.4-22` | informative | http URLs follow the grammar "http:" "//" host [":" port] [abs_path ["?" query]]. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | http_URL = "http:" "//" host [ ":" port ] [ abs_path [ "?" query ]] | URL parser must accept http URLs per this production: optional port, optional abs_path, optional query. |
| `3.1-3.4-23` | informative | An empty or absent port in an http URL means port 80. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the port is empty or not given, port 80 is assumed. | Dial TCP port 80 when the http URL omits the port or gives an empty port (e.g. 'host:'). |
| `3.1-3.4-24` | SHOULD | IP addresses in URLs should be avoided whenever possible. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | The use of IP addresses in URLs SHOULD be avoided whenever possible | Advisory on URL authoring; client code must still accept IP-literal URLs supplied by callers, nothing to enforce. |
| `3.1-3.4-25` | MUST | When the URL has no abs_path, the Request-URI must be given as "/". <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the abs_path is not present in the URL, it MUST be given as "/" when used as a Request-URI for a resource | Request serializer: for URLs like http://host, emit 'GET / HTTP/1.1' — never an empty Request-URI. |
| `3.1-3.4-26` | MAY | A proxy receiving a non-fully-qualified host name may append its own domain. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | If a proxy receives a host name which is not a fully qualified domain name, it MAY add its domain to the host name it received. | Proxy-only; no client action. |
| `3.1-3.4-27` | MUST NOT | A proxy receiving a fully qualified domain name must not change the host name. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | If a proxy receives a fully qualified domain name, the proxy MUST NOT change the host name. | Proxy-only; no client action. |
| `3.1-3.4-28` | SHOULD | A client comparing URIs should use case-sensitive octet-by-octet comparison of the entire URIs, subject to the listed exceptions. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client SHOULD use a case-sensitive octet-by-octet comparison of the entire URIs | Any URI-equality logic (cache keys, redirect loop detection, connection reuse keys) compares raw octets case-sensitively except the four exceptions below. |
| `3.1-3.4-29` | informative | For comparison, an empty or absent port equals the default port for that URI-reference. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A port that is empty or not given is equivalent to the default port for that URI-reference | Normalize absent/empty port to the scheme default (80 for http) before URI comparison. |
| `3.1-3.4-30` | MUST | Host name comparisons must be case-insensitive. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Comparisons of host names MUST be case-insensitive | Case-fold host names before comparing URIs (ABC.com == abc.com). |
| `3.1-3.4-31` | MUST | Scheme name comparisons must be case-insensitive. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Comparisons of scheme names MUST be case-insensitive | Case-fold the scheme before comparing URIs (HTTP:// == http://). |
| `3.1-3.4-32` | informative | For comparison, an empty abs_path equals an abs_path of "/". <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | An empty abs_path is equivalent to an abs_path of "/". | Normalize empty path to '/' before URI comparison. |
| `3.1-3.4-33` | informative | Characters outside the RFC 2396 reserved and unsafe sets are equivalent to their %HEX HEX encodings for comparison. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Characters other than those in the "reserved" and "unsafe" sets (see RFC 2396 [42]) are equivalent to their ""%" HEX HEX" encoding. | URI comparison decodes percent-escapes of unreserved characters (%7E == ~) before octet compare. |
| `3.1-3.4-34` | MUST | Clients and servers that parse dates must accept all three historical date formats (RFC 1123, RFC 850, asctime). <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 clients and servers that parse the date value MUST accept all three formats (for compatibility with HTTP/1.0) | Date parser (Date, Expires, Last-Modified, Retry-After, etc.) must accept rfc1123-date, rfc850-date, and asctime-date. |
| `3.1-3.4-35` | MUST | When generating HTTP-date values in header fields, only the RFC 1123 format may be used. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | they MUST only generate the RFC 1123 format for representing HTTP-date values in header fields | Any date the client emits (e.g. If-Modified-Since, Date) must be formatted as 'Sun, 06 Nov 1994 08:49:37 GMT'. |
| `3.1-3.4-36` | MUST | All HTTP date/time stamps must be expressed in GMT, without exception. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All HTTP date/time stamps MUST be represented in Greenwich Mean Time (GMT), without exception. | Emit dates converted to GMT (UTC) with literal 'GMT' zone; interpret all parsed dates as GMT. |
| `3.1-3.4-37` | MUST | GMT must be assumed as the time zone when reading the asctime date format. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MUST be assumed when reading the asctime format | asctime parser attaches GMT to the parsed time even though the format carries no zone indicator. |
| `3.1-3.4-38` | informative | HTTP-date is case sensitive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP-date is case sensitive | Match day/month names and 'GMT' exactly as in the grammar; emit exact-case tokens ('Sun', not 'SUN' or 'sun'). |
| `3.1-3.4-39` | MUST NOT | HTTP-date must not include LWS beyond the SP explicitly present in the grammar. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MUST NOT include additional LWS beyond that specifically included as SP in the grammar | Date serializer uses exactly one SP where the grammar says SP, never tabs or extra spaces; strict parsers may reject padded dates. |
| `3.1-3.4-40` | informative | HTTP-date is one of rfc1123-date, rfc850-date, or asctime-date. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP-date = rfc1123-date \| rfc850-date \| asctime-date | Date parser tries all three alternatives; nothing else is a valid HTTP-date. |
| `3.1-3.4-41` | informative | RFC 1123 date grammar: wkday "," SP date1 SP time SP "GMT". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | rfc1123-date = wkday "," SP date1 SP time SP "GMT" | Implement this exact production for both parsing and (exclusively) generation. |
| `3.1-3.4-42` | informative | RFC 850 date grammar: weekday "," SP date2 SP time SP "GMT". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | rfc850-date = weekday "," SP date2 SP time SP "GMT" | Parser accepts full weekday names and two-digit years per this production (parse-only, never generate). |
| `3.1-3.4-43` | informative | asctime date grammar: wkday SP date3 SP time SP 4DIGIT. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | asctime-date = wkday SP date3 SP time SP 4DIGIT | Parser accepts asctime form with trailing 4-digit year and no zone (parse-only, never generate). |
| `3.1-3.4-44` | informative | date1 grammar (RFC 1123): 2DIGIT SP month SP 4DIGIT (day month year). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | date1 = 2DIGIT SP month SP 4DIGIT | RFC 1123 parser/serializer: two-digit day, month token, four-digit year, single spaces. |
| `3.1-3.4-45` | informative | date2 grammar (RFC 850): 2DIGIT "-" month "-" 2DIGIT (day-month-year). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | date2 = 2DIGIT "-" month "-" 2DIGIT | RFC 850 parser: hyphen-separated day-month-2-digit-year (e.g. 02-Jun-82). |
| `3.1-3.4-46` | informative | date3 grammar (asctime): month SP (2DIGIT \| (SP 1DIGIT)) — day may be space-padded. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | date3 = month SP ( 2DIGIT \| ( SP 1DIGIT )) | asctime parser accepts single-digit days padded with a leading space ('Jun 2'). |
| `3.1-3.4-47` | informative | time grammar: 2DIGIT ":" 2DIGIT ":" 2DIGIT, range 00:00:00-23:59:59. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | time = 2DIGIT ":" 2DIGIT ":" 2DIGIT | Parse/emit HH:MM:SS with exactly two digits per component. |
| `3.1-3.4-48` | informative | wkday tokens are the three-letter day abbreviations Mon-Sun. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | wkday = "Mon" \| "Tue" \| "Wed" \| "Thu" \| "Fri" \| "Sat" \| "Sun" | Recognize exactly these seven case-sensitive tokens in rfc1123/asctime dates; emit them in generated dates. |
| `3.1-3.4-49` | informative | weekday tokens are the full day names Monday-Sunday (used only in rfc850-date). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | weekday = "Monday" \| "Tuesday" \| "Wednesday" \| "Thursday" \| "Friday" \| "Saturday" \| "Sunday" | Recognize full day names case-sensitively when parsing RFC 850 dates. |
| `3.1-3.4-50` | informative | month tokens are the twelve three-letter abbreviations Jan-Dec. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | month = "Jan" \| "Feb" \| "Mar" \| "Apr" \| "May" \| "Jun" \| "Jul" \| "Aug" \| "Sep" \| "Oct" \| "Nov" \| "Dec" | Recognize/emit exactly these twelve case-sensitive month tokens. |
| `3.1-3.4-51` | informative | The date format requirements apply only to usage within the protocol stream, not to user presentation or logging. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Clients and servers are not required to use these formats for user presentation, request logging, etc. | Enforce format MUSTs only on wire bytes; logs/metrics may format dates freely. |
| `3.1-3.4-52` | informative | Delta-seconds values are a decimal integer number of seconds: 1*DIGIT. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | delta-seconds = 1*DIGIT | Parse delta-seconds header values (e.g. Retry-After, Age, max-age) as one-or-more decimal digits only. |
| `3.1-3.4-53` | MUST | A MIME character set name's definition must fully specify the octet-to-character mapping; external profiling information is not permitted. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | the definition associated with a MIME character set name MUST fully specify the mapping to be performed from octets to characters | Binds charset definitions/registry, not client code; no implementation action for a client that does not define charsets. |
| `3.1-3.4-54` | informative | HTTP character sets are identified by case-insensitive tokens from the IANA Character Set registry. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP character sets are identified by case-insensitive tokens. | Compare charset names case-insensitively wherever the client matches them (e.g. Content-Type charset parameter). |
| `3.1-3.4-55` | informative | The charset value is grammatically an HTTP token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | charset = token | Parse charset values with the generic token production; reject separators/CTLs in charset names. |
| `3.1-3.4-56` | MUST | A charset token predefined in the IANA registry must denote the charset the registry defines. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | any token that has a predefined value within the IANA Character Set registry [19] MUST represent the character set defined by that registry | If the client interprets charset tokens at all, IANA-registered names must map to their registry-defined encodings, never a private meaning. |
| `3.1-3.4-57` | SHOULD | Applications should restrict their character-set usage to IANA-registered charsets. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Applications SHOULD limit their use of character sets to those defined by the IANA registry. | When the client emits charset names (Accept-Charset, Content-Type of request bodies), use only IANA-registered tokens. |
| `3.1-3.4-58` | MAY | Senders may include an explicit charset parameter even when the charset is ISO-8859-1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Senders wishing to defeat this behavior MAY include a charset parameter even when the charset is ISO-8859-1 | Client may (optionally) append ';charset=...' to Content-Type on request bodies even for ISO-8859-1. |
| `3.1-3.4-59` | SHOULD | Senders should include the charset parameter when it is known not to confuse the recipient. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | SHOULD do so when it is known that it will not confuse the recipient | Prefer labeling request-body Content-Type with an explicit charset when targeting HTTP/1.1 servers. |
| `3.1-3.4-60` | MUST | HTTP/1.1 recipients must respect the charset label provided by the sender. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 recipients MUST respect the charset label provided by the sender | When the client decodes/interprets a response body, honor the Content-Type charset parameter; never silently override it. |
| `3.1-3.4-61` | MUST | User agents that guess charsets must prefer the content-type field's charset (if supported) over the recipient's preference when initially displaying a document. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | user agents that have a provision to "guess" a charset MUST use the charset from the content-type field if they support that charset | Only applicable if the client ever adds charset sniffing: the sniffer must yield to an explicit, supported Content-Type charset label. |

### 3.5-3.6 - Content Codings, Transfer Codings, Chunked Transfer Coding

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `3.5-3.6-1` | informative | A content-coding value is syntactically a single HTTP token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | content-coding = token | Parse Accept-Encoding/Content-Encoding values with the token grammar; reject non-token content-coding names when emitting. |
| `3.5-3.6-2` | informative | Content-coding values must be compared case-insensitively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All content-coding values are case-insensitive. | Match Content-Encoding response values (e.g. GZIP, GZip) case-insensitively when selecting a decoder. |
| `3.5-3.6-3` | informative | Content-coding values appear in the Accept-Encoding and Content-Encoding header fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 uses content-coding values in the Accept-Encoding (section 14.3) and Content-Encoding (section 14.11) header fields. | Wire content-coding handling to exactly these two headers: emit Accept-Encoding on requests, interpret Content-Encoding on responses. |
| `3.5-3.6-4` | informative | The "gzip" content-coding is the RFC 1952 gzip file format (LZ77 with 32-bit CRC). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An encoding format produced by the file compression program "gzip" (GNU zip) as described in RFC 1952 [25]. | If the client decodes Content-Encoding: gzip, use an RFC 1952 gzip decoder (magic bytes + CRC32), not raw deflate. |
| `3.5-3.6-5` | informative | The "compress" content-coding is the UNIX compress adaptive LZW format. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The encoding format produced by the common UNIX file compression program "compress". This format is an adaptive Lempel-Ziv-Welch coding (LZW). | Only advertise "compress" in Accept-Encoding if an LZW decoder is actually implemented; otherwise never offer it. |
| `3.5-3.6-6` | SHOULD | Applications should treat "x-gzip" and "x-compress" as equivalent to "gzip" and "compress". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | applications SHOULD consider "x-gzip" and "x-compress" to be equivalent to "gzip" and "compress" respectively. | In the Content-Encoding decoder lookup, alias x-gzip→gzip and x-compress→compress (case-insensitively). |
| `3.5-3.6-7` | informative | The "deflate" content-coding is the zlib format (RFC 1950) containing deflate-compressed data (RFC 1951). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "zlib" format defined in RFC 1950 [31] in combination with the "deflate" compression mechanism described in RFC 1951 [29]. | If the client decodes Content-Encoding: deflate, expect a zlib wrapper (RFC 1950), not bare RFC 1951 deflate (real servers send both — consider tolerating raw deflate as a fallback). |
| `3.5-3.6-8` | SHOULD NOT | The "identity" content-coding means no transformation; it is used only in Accept-Encoding and should not appear in Content-Encoding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This content-coding is used only in the Accept-Encoding header, and SHOULD NOT be used in the Content-Encoding header. | Never emit Content-Encoding: identity on request bodies; "identity" is valid in the client's Accept-Encoding; tolerate a server that violates the SHOULD NOT by treating it as no transformation. |
| `3.5-3.6-9` | SHOULD | New content-coding value tokens should be registered with IANA. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | New content-coding value tokens SHOULD be registered | Registration duty for spec authors, not client code; no client-side check — client only needs to skip decoding for unrecognized codings and surface them to the caller. |
| `3.5-3.6-10` | SHOULD | Specifications of new content-coding algorithms should be publicly available and adequate for independent implementation. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | specifications of the content coding algorithms needed to implement a new value SHOULD be publicly available and adequate for independent implementation | Binds registrants of new codings only; nothing to implement in the client. |
| `3.5-3.6-11` | informative | A transfer-coding is either the literal "chunked" or a transfer-extension: a token followed by optional semicolon-separated parameters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | transfer-coding = "chunked" \| transfer-extension transfer-extension = token *( ";" parameter ) | Parse each Transfer-Encoding/TE list element as token *( ";" parameter ); "chunked" is a special-cased token. |
| `3.5-3.6-12` | informative | Transfer-coding parameters are attribute=value pairs where attribute is a token and value is a token or quoted-string. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | parameter = attribute "=" value attribute = token value = token \| quoted-string | Transfer-coding parameter parser must accept both token and quoted-string values (with quoted-string unescaping) after "=". |
| `3.5-3.6-13` | informative | Transfer-coding values must be compared case-insensitively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All transfer-coding values are case-insensitive. | Recognize Transfer-Encoding: Chunked / CHUNKED etc. case-insensitively when deciding response framing. |
| `3.5-3.6-14` | informative | Transfer-coding values appear in the TE and Transfer-Encoding header fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 uses transfer-coding values in the TE header field (section 14.39) and in the Transfer-Encoding header field (section 14.41). | Only these two headers carry transfer-codings: client may emit TE on requests and must interpret Transfer-Encoding on responses (and emit it on chunked request bodies). |
| `3.5-3.6-15` | MUST | Any transfer-coded message-body must include "chunked" in its set of transfer-codings, unless the message is delimited by connection close. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Whenever a transfer-coding is applied to a message-body, the set of transfer-codings MUST include "chunked", unless the message is terminated by closing the connection. | When emitting a transfer-coded request body always include chunked (requests can never be close-delimited); when receiving, a Transfer-Encoding without final chunked means read-until-close framing. |
| `3.5-3.6-16` | MUST | When chunked is used it must be the last (outermost) transfer-coding applied to the message-body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | When the "chunked" transfer-coding is used, it MUST be the last transfer-coding applied to the message-body. | Emit chunked only as the final coding in Transfer-Encoding; on receive, treat chunked anywhere but last position in the list as a framing violation. |
| `3.5-3.6-17` | MUST NOT | The chunked transfer-coding must not be applied more than once to a message-body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "chunked" transfer-coding MUST NOT be applied more than once to a message-body. | Never emit chunked twice; on receive, reject a Transfer-Encoding list containing "chunked" more than once (request-smuggling vector). |
| `3.5-3.6-18` | informative | The initial IANA transfer-coding registry contains chunked, identity, gzip, compress, and deflate. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Initially, the registry contains the following tokens: "chunked" (section 3.6.1), "identity" (section 3.6.2), "gzip" (section 3.5), "compress" (section 3.5), and "deflate" (section 3.5). | Recognize these five tokens as transfer-codings; note RFC 2616 (unlike RFC 7230) allows "Transfer-Encoding: identity", which the parser should tolerate as a no-op (referenced section 3.6.2 does not exist — known 2616 defect). |
| `3.5-3.6-19` | SHOULD | New transfer-coding value tokens should be registered the same way as content-coding tokens. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | New transfer-coding value tokens SHOULD be registered in the same way as new content-coding value tokens (section 3.5). | Registration duty for spec authors; no client code obligation. |
| `3.5-3.6-20` | SHOULD | A server receiving an entity-body with a transfer-coding it does not understand should return 501 and close the connection. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server which receives an entity-body with a transfer-coding it does not understand SHOULD return 501 (Unimplemented), and close the connection. | Server-only; the client's soft counterpart is to handle a 501 response plus connection close gracefully (no retry of the coding). |
| `3.5-3.6-21` | MUST NOT | A server must not send transfer-codings to an HTTP/1.0 client. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST NOT send transfer-codings to an HTTP/1.0 client. | Server-only; poseidon always sends HTTP/1.1 requests, so no client obligation (a client sending HTTP/1.0 could not rely on chunked responses). |
| `3.5-3.6-22` | OPTIONAL | The trailer of a chunked body, containing entity-header fields, is optional. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | followed by an OPTIONAL trailer containing entity-header fields. | Chunked decoder must accept both an empty trailer (bare CRLF after last-chunk) and one or more trailer header fields. |
| `3.5-3.6-23` | informative | A chunked body is zero or more chunks, then a last-chunk, then a trailer, then a terminating CRLF. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Chunked-Body = *chunk last-chunk trailer CRLF | Decoder state machine: loop chunks until last-chunk, then parse trailer headers, then require the final empty-line CRLF before the message is complete. |
| `3.5-3.6-24` | informative | Each chunk is a chunk-size with optional chunk-extension, CRLF, exactly chunk-size octets of data, then CRLF. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | chunk = chunk-size [ chunk-extension ] CRLF chunk-data CRLF | After reading chunk-size octets of data, require the trailing CRLF exactly; anything else is a framing error. |
| `3.5-3.6-25` | informative | chunk-size is one or more hexadecimal digits. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | chunk-size = 1*HEX | Parse chunk-size as unbounded-length hex (accept upper/lowercase digits, leading zeros); enforce an implementation overflow cap with a clean error, and reject an empty or non-hex size. |
| `3.5-3.6-26` | informative | The chunk-size field is a string of hex digits giving the size of the chunk's data. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The chunk-size field is a string of hex digits indicating the size of the chunk. | chunk-size counts octets of chunk-data only — it excludes the CRLFs and any chunk-extension bytes. |
| `3.5-3.6-27` | informative | The last-chunk is one or more "0" digits with optional chunk-extension, then CRLF. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | last-chunk = 1*("0") [ chunk-extension ] CRLF | Decoder must accept multi-zero last chunks ("00", "000") and extensions on the last chunk; emit exactly "0\r\n" when encoding. |
| `3.5-3.6-28` | informative | A chunk-extension is zero or more ";" name[=value] pairs, name a token, value a token or quoted-string. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | chunk-extension= *( ";" chunk-ext-name [ "=" chunk-ext-val ] ) chunk-ext-name = token chunk-ext-val = token \| quoted-string | Parse (or at minimum skip to CRLF safely, respecting quoted-string quoting) any ";name" and ";name=value" extensions on every chunk line, with a length bound against abuse. |
| `3.5-3.6-29` | informative | chunk-data is exactly chunk-size octets of opaque data. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | chunk-data = chunk-size(OCTET) | Read exactly chunk-size raw octets — data is binary and must not be scanned for CRLF or otherwise interpreted. |
| `3.5-3.6-30` | informative | The trailer is zero or more entity-header fields each followed by CRLF. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | trailer = *(entity-header CRLF) | After last-chunk, parse header lines with the normal header-field parser until the empty line; zero trailer fields is valid. |
| `3.5-3.6-31` | informative | Chunked encoding ends at any zero-size chunk, followed by the trailer, terminated by an empty line. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The chunked encoding is ended by any chunk whose size is zero, followed by the trailer, which is terminated by an empty line. | Treat any chunk parsing to size 0 as end-of-body (never as an empty data chunk mid-stream); body is complete only after the trailer's terminating empty line. |
| `3.5-3.6-32` | informative | The Trailer header field can announce which header fields will be included in the trailer. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The Trailer header field can be used to indicate which header fields are included in a trailer | Parse Trailer as an ordinary response header; when emitting a chunked request with trailers, optionally set Trailer to name them. |
| `3.5-3.6-33` | MUST NOT | A server may put header fields in a response trailer only if the request's TE indicated "trailers" is acceptable, or the fields are origin-server optional metadata the recipient can do without. <br>_(server,origin [CLIENT])_ | A server using chunked transfer-coding in a response MUST NOT use the trailer for any header fields unless at least one of the following is true | Binds the server, but the client must respect the model: send "TE: trailers" if it wants guaranteed trailers, and never depend on trailer fields it did not opt into — the parser must still accept trailers arriving unsolicited (condition b) and tolerate their absence. |
| `3.5-3.6-34` | MUST | Every HTTP/1.1 application must be able to receive and decode the chunked transfer-coding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All HTTP/1.1 applications MUST be able to receive and decode the "chunked" transfer-coding | Chunked response decoding is unconditionally mandatory in the client — it cannot be feature-gated or advertised away. |
| `3.5-3.6-35` | MUST | Applications must ignore chunk-extensions they do not understand. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MUST ignore chunk-extension extensions they do not understand | Unknown chunk-extensions must be skipped silently — never an error, never surfaced as a decode failure (but still parsed safely and length-bounded). |

### 3.7-3.12 - Media Types, Product Tokens, Quality Values, Language Tags, Entity Tags, Range Units

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `3.7-3.12-1` | informative | A media-type value is a type token, a "/", a subtype token, followed by zero or more ";"-separated parameters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | media-type = type "/" subtype *( ";" parameter ) | Content-Type/Accept parser and emitter must conform to token "/" token *( ";" parameter ), with type=token and subtype=token. |
| `3.7-3.12-2` | MAY | Media-type parameters are optional attribute/value pairs (section 3.6 syntax) that may follow the type/subtype. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Parameters MAY follow the type/subtype in the form of attribute/value pairs (as defined in section 3.6). | Parser must accept zero or more attribute=value parameters after the subtype; emitter may append them. |
| `3.7-3.12-3` | informative | Media type, subtype, and parameter attribute names are case-insensitive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The type, subtype, and parameter attribute names are case-insensitive. | Compare type/subtype and parameter names case-insensitively (e.g. Text/HTML == text/html, CHARSET == charset). |
| `3.7-3.12-4` | informative | Media-type parameter values may or may not be case-sensitive depending on the parameter's own semantics. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Parameter values might or might not be case-sensitive, depending on the semantics of the parameter name. | Do not blindly case-fold parameter values; preserve their original case and compare per-parameter. |
| `3.7-3.12-5` | MUST NOT | No linear white space is allowed between type and subtype, nor between a parameter attribute and its value. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Linear white space (LWS) MUST NOT be used between the type and subtype, nor between an attribute and its value. | Emitter must produce type/subtype and attr=value with no spaces around "/" or "="; check serialized Content-Type/Accept bytes. |
| `3.7-3.12-6` | SHOULD | When sending to older HTTP applications, only use media-type parameters that the type/subtype definition requires. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | implementations SHOULD only use media type parameters when they are required by that type/subtype definition. | Do not add gratuitous media-type parameters to emitted Content-Type; only include ones the media type requires (e.g. multipart boundary). |
| `3.7-3.12-7` | informative | Use of media types not registered with IANA is discouraged. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Use of non-registered media types is discouraged. | Prefer IANA-registered media types in emitted Content-Type; no hard parser action required. |
| `3.7-3.12-8` | MUST | An entity-body must be in the media type's canonical form before transmission, except for "text" types. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An entity-body transferred via HTTP messages MUST be represented in the appropriate canonical form prior to its transmission except for "text" types | Client sending a request body must transmit it in the media type's canonical form (line-break relaxation applies only to text types). |
| `3.7-3.12-9` | informative | Text media may be transported with bare CR or bare LF as the line break if used consistently for the entire entity-body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | allows the transport of text media with plain CR or LF alone representing a line break when it is done consistently for an entire entity-body. | Client may send text bodies with consistent bare CR or LF line breaks; do not normalize/reject such bodies. |
| `3.7-3.12-10` | MUST | Applications must accept CRLF, bare CR, and bare LF as line breaks in text media received via HTTP. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP applications MUST accept CRLF, bare CR, and bare LF as being representative of a line break in text media received via HTTP. | Any text-body handling in the client must treat CR, LF, and CRLF equivalently as line breaks; never reject text bodies for bare CR/LF. |
| `3.7-3.12-11` | informative | For character sets that do not use octets 13/10 for CR/LF, that charset's own octet sequences may represent line breaks in text bodies. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP allows the use of whatever octet sequences are defined by that character set to represent the equivalent of CR and LF for line breaks. | Do not assume octets 13/10 delimit lines in text bodies of multi-byte charsets; the flexibility applies only inside the entity-body. |
| `3.7-3.12-12` | MUST NOT | Bare CR or LF must never replace CRLF within HTTP control structures such as header fields and multipart boundaries. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a bare CR or LF MUST NOT be substituted for CRLF within any of the HTTP control structures (such as header fields and multipart boundaries). | Request serializer must terminate request-line/headers with CRLF only; multipart writer must use CRLF boundaries; never emit bare CR/LF there. |
| `3.7-3.12-13` | MUST | When an entity-body carries a content-coding, the underlying data must already be in the canonical/allowed form before encoding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If an entity-body is encoded with a content-coding, the underlying data MUST be in a form defined above prior to being encoded. | Apply canonicalization (3.7.1 rules) to the body first, then apply gzip/deflate/etc. content-coding when emitting. |
| `3.7-3.12-14` | informative | Text subtypes received via HTTP without an explicit charset parameter default to ISO-8859-1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | media subtypes of the "text" type are defined to have a default charset value of "ISO-8859-1" when received via HTTP. | When the client decodes a text/* response lacking a charset parameter, default to ISO-8859-1 (per RFC 2616; note later specs changed this). |
| `3.7-3.12-15` | MUST | Data in a character set other than ISO-8859-1 or its subsets must carry an appropriate charset label. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Data in character sets other than "ISO-8859-1" or its subsets MUST be labeled with an appropriate charset value. | When the client sends a text body in a non-Latin-1 charset (e.g. UTF-8), it must include charset=... in Content-Type. |
| `3.7-3.12-16` | MUST | Every multipart media type value must include a boundary parameter. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MUST include a boundary parameter as part of the media type value. | When emitting multipart/* (e.g. multipart/form-data) the client must put boundary=... in the Content-Type; parser may treat its absence as malformed. |
| `3.7-3.12-17` | MUST | A multipart message-body is a protocol element and must use only CRLF for line breaks between body-parts. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The message body is itself a protocol element and MUST therefore use only CRLF to represent line breaks between body-parts. | Multipart body writer must delimit body-parts with CRLF exclusively (boundary lines, part-header terminators). |
| `3.7-3.12-18` | MUST | The epilogue of any multipart message transferred via HTTP must be empty. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the epilogue of any multipart message MUST be empty | Multipart writer must end the body at the closing boundary; nothing may follow the final boundary delimiter. |
| `3.7-3.12-19` | MUST NOT | HTTP applications must not transmit a multipart epilogue even if the original multipart content had one. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP applications MUST NOT transmit the epilogue (even if the original multipart contains an epilogue). | When forwarding or re-serializing existing MIME content, strip any epilogue before sending; the body must be self-delimiting via the ending boundary. |
| `3.7-3.12-20` | SHOULD | A user agent receiving a multipart type should behave the same as or similarly to a MIME user agent (except multipart/byteranges in 206 handled per 13.5.4/14.16). <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | an HTTP user agent SHOULD follow the same or similar behavior as a MIME user agent would upon receipt of a multipart type. | If the client interprets multipart response bodies at all, process them per MIME rules; stated twice in 3.7.2. A payload-opaque library may pass them through. |
| `3.7-3.12-21` | informative | MIME header fields inside body-parts of a multipart body have no HTTP significance beyond their MIME semantics. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The MIME header fields within each body-part of a multipart message-body do not have any significance to HTTP beyond that defined by their MIME semantics. | Never treat headers inside multipart body-parts as HTTP message headers (no framing, connection, or transfer semantics from them). |
| `3.7-3.12-22` | MUST | An unrecognized multipart subtype must be treated as multipart/mixed. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If an application receives an unrecognized multipart subtype, the application MUST treat it as being equivalent to "multipart/mixed". | Client-side multipart handling must fall back to multipart/mixed semantics for unknown multipart/* subtypes instead of erroring. |
| `3.7-3.12-23` | informative | By convention, product tokens are listed in order of their significance for identifying the application. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | By convention, the products are listed in order of their significance for identifying the application. | When building User-Agent, put the primary product first, sub-products (e.g. library name/version) after, whitespace-separated. |
| `3.7-3.12-24` | informative | A product token is a token optionally followed by "/" and a product-version token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | product = token ["/" product-version] product-version = token | User-Agent builder/validator must emit each product as token or token"/"token; both parts restricted to token characters. |
| `3.7-3.12-25` | SHOULD | Product tokens should be short and to the point. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Product tokens SHOULD be short and to the point. | Keep the library's default User-Agent product token concise (name/version only). |
| `3.7-3.12-26` | MUST NOT | Product tokens must not be used for advertising or other non-essential information. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | They MUST NOT be used for advertising or other non-essential information. | Do not embed promotional or extraneous strings in the default User-Agent value. |
| `3.7-3.12-27` | MAY | Any token character is permitted in a product-version. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | any token character MAY appear in a product-version | Parser/validator must accept the full token character set in the version part (e.g. "2.17b3"), not just digits and dots. |
| `3.7-3.12-28` | SHOULD | The product-version token should be used only as a version identifier; successive versions should differ only in the product-version portion. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | this token SHOULD only be used for a version identifier | Version bumps of the client should change only the product-version part of its User-Agent product, keeping the product name stable. |
| `3.7-3.12-29` | informative | A quality weight is a real number normalized to the range 0 through 1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A weight is normalized to a real number in the range 0 through 1, where 0 is the minimum and 1 the maximum value. | Reject or clamp q values outside [0,1] when emitting Accept*/TE headers; the grammar makes values above 1 unrepresentable. |
| `3.7-3.12-30` | informative | A quality value of 0 means content with that parameter is not acceptable for the client. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If a parameter has a quality value of 0, then content with this parameter is `not acceptable' for the client. | Treat q=0 in emitted Accept*/TE entries as explicit exclusion of that item, not as low preference. |
| `3.7-3.12-31` | MUST NOT | HTTP/1.1 applications must not generate qvalues with more than three digits after the decimal point. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 applications MUST NOT generate more than three digits after the decimal point. | qvalue formatter must emit at most 3 fractional digits (e.g. q=0.125 ok, q=0.1254 forbidden); check Accept*/TE serialization. |
| `3.7-3.12-32` | SHOULD | User configuration of quality values should also be limited to three digits after the decimal point. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | User configuration of these values SHOULD also be limited in this fashion. | Validate caller-supplied q values in the client API to at most 3 decimal digits (round or reject). |
| `3.7-3.12-33` | informative | qvalue grammar: "0" optionally followed by "." and up to 3 digits, or "1" optionally followed by "." and up to three zeros. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | qvalue = ( "0" [ "." 0*3DIGIT ] ) \| ( "1" [ "." 0*3("0") ] ) | qvalue parser/formatter must match this grammar exactly: leading "0" or "1" required, 1.x only with zeros, no values > 1. |
| `3.7-3.12-34` | informative | HTTP language tag syntax and registry are those defined by RFC 1766. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The syntax and registry of HTTP language tags is the same as that defined by RFC 1766 [1]. | Accept-Language/Content-Language values must follow RFC 1766 tag syntax (summarized by the language-tag production below). |
| `3.7-3.12-35` | informative | A language-tag is a primary-tag of 1-8 ALPHA followed by zero or more "-"-separated subtags of 1-8 ALPHA each. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | language-tag = primary-tag *( "-" subtag ) primary-tag = 1*8ALPHA subtag = 1*8ALPHA | Language-tag emitter/validator: each segment 1-8 alphabetic characters, segments joined by "-" (e.g. en-US). |
| `3.7-3.12-36` | informative | White space is not allowed within a language tag, and all language tags are case-insensitive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | White space is not allowed within the tag and all tags are case-insensitive. | Never emit spaces inside a language tag; compare tags case-insensitively (en-us == en-US). |
| `3.7-3.12-37` | informative | An entity-tag is an opaque quoted-string, optionally prefixed by the weakness indicator "W/". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | entity-tag = [ weak ] opaque-tag weak = "W/" opaque-tag = quoted-string | ETag/If-Match/If-None-Match/If-Range parsing must accept optional "W/" plus a quoted-string, and preserve the opaque-tag verbatim for echo-back. |
| `3.7-3.12-38` | MAY | A strong entity tag may be shared by two entities of a resource only if they are octet-for-octet identical. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | A "strong entity tag" MAY be shared by two entities of a resource only if they are equivalent by octet equality. | Binds the tag issuer (origin server); client takes no action, but this defines what a strong tag guarantees for comparison. |
| `3.7-3.12-39` | MAY | A weak entity tag ("W/" prefix) may be shared by two entities only if they are semantically equivalent and substitutable. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | MAY be shared by two entities of a resource only if the entities are equivalent and could be substituted for each other with no significant change in semantics. | Binds the tag issuer (origin server); no client emission/parsing action beyond recognizing the W/ prefix. |
| `3.7-3.12-40` | informative | A weak entity tag can only be used for weak comparison. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A weak entity tag can only be used for weak comparison. | Client must not use W/-prefixed tags where strong comparison is required (notably If-Range and range-conditional If-Match); check validator selection logic. |
| `3.7-3.12-41` | MUST | An entity tag must be unique across all versions of all entities associated with a particular resource. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | An entity tag MUST be unique across all versions of all entities associated with a particular resource. | Server-side obligation; client may rely on tag uniqueness per resource when caching/validating but has nothing to implement. |
| `3.7-3.12-42` | MAY | The same entity tag value may be used for entities obtained from different URIs. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | A given entity tag value MAY be used for entities obtained by requests on different URIs. | Server-side allowance; no client action. |
| `3.7-3.12-43` | informative | Identical entity tag values on entities from different URIs do not imply those entities are equivalent. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The use of the same entity tag value in conjunction with entities obtained by requests on different URIs does not imply the equivalence of those entities. | Client/cache logic must scope ETag comparison per-URI; never deduplicate or validate across URIs based on matching tags. |
| `3.7-3.12-44` | informative | A range-unit is either the bytes-unit "bytes" or an other-range-unit token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | range-unit = bytes-unit \| other-range-unit bytes-unit = "bytes" other-range-unit = token | Range/Content-Range parsing must accept "bytes" plus any token as a unit name; emitter uses "bytes". |
| `3.7-3.12-45` | informative | "bytes" is the only range unit defined by HTTP/1.1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The only range unit defined by HTTP/1.1 is "bytes". | Client should generate Range requests only with the bytes unit; other units are undefined by the spec. |
| `3.7-3.12-46` | MAY | Implementations may ignore ranges specified using units other than "bytes". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 implementations MAY ignore ranges specified using other units. | Client may ignore non-bytes range units it receives (e.g. in Accept-Ranges/Content-Range) and must tolerate servers ignoring non-bytes Range requests it sends. |
| `3.7-3.12-47` | informative | HTTP/1.1 is designed so implementations need not depend on knowledge of ranges. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 has been designed to allow implementations of applications that do not depend on knowledge of ranges. | Range support is optional in the client; core request/response handling must work correctly with no range logic at all. |

### 4 - HTTP Message: Message Types, Message Headers, Message Body, Message Length, General Header Fields

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `4-1` | informative | An HTTP/1.1 message is either a Request or a Response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP-message = Request \| Response ; HTTP/1.1 messages | Client emits only Request messages and parses only Response messages; no other top-level message form exists. |
| `4-2` | informative | Every message is a start-line, zero or more header fields each ended by CRLF, an empty line (bare CRLF) terminating the headers, then an optional message-body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | generic-message = start-line *(message-header CRLF) CRLF [ message-body ] start-line = Request-Line \| Status-Line | Response parser must locate the header terminator (CRLF on an otherwise empty line) before any body handling; request writer must emit exactly this structure. |
| `4-3` | SHOULD | Servers should skip empty lines received where a Request-Line is expected. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | servers SHOULD ignore any empty line(s) received where a Request-Line is expected. | Server-side robustness rule; no client obligation (client-side prohibition on sending such CRLFs is 4-4). |
| `4-4` | MUST NOT | A client must not send an extra CRLF before or after a request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | an HTTP/1.1 client MUST NOT preface or follow a request with an extra CRLF. | Request serializer must emit no stray CRLF before the Request-Line or after the message end (esp. after bodies); verify pipelined requests are byte-adjacent. |
| `4-5` | informative | A header field is a name, a colon, and a field value; field names are case-insensitive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Each header field consists of a name followed by a colon (":") and the field value. Field names are case-insensitive. | Response header parser must match field names case-insensitively (e.g. content-length == Content-Length) when framing and interpreting. |
| `4-6` | MAY | The field value may be preceded by any amount of LWS; a single SP is preferred. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The field value MAY be preceded by any amount of LWS, though a single SP is preferred. | Parser must accept zero or more SP/HT (and folded LWS) after the colon; writer should emit exactly one SP. |
| `4-7` | informative | A header field may be continued onto following lines when each extra line starts with at least one SP or HT (line folding). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Header fields can be extended over multiple lines by preceding each extra line with at least one SP or HT. | Response parser must recognize obs-fold continuation lines (CRLF followed by SP/HT) as part of the previous field value, not a new header or end of headers. |
| `4-8` | informative | When generating HTTP constructs, applications ought to use the common form since some implementations reject anything else. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Applications ought to follow "common form", where one is known or indicated, when generating HTTP constructs | Request writer should emit canonical forms: 'Name: value' with single SP, no folding, no unusual LWS. |
| `4-9` | informative | Grammar: message-header is field-name ":" optional field-value; field-name is a token; field-value is any mix of field-content and LWS. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | message-header = field-name ":" [ field-value ] field-name = token field-value = *( field-content \| LWS ) | Parser must accept empty field values; field names must be validated as tokens (no separators/CTLs) when emitting; value may contain LWS. |
