| `4-10` | MAY | Leading or trailing LWS around the field-content may be stripped without changing semantics. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Such leading or trailing LWS MAY be removed without changing the semantics of the field value. | Client parser may (and typically should) trim leading/trailing SP/HT from received field values before interpretation. |
| `4-11` | MAY | Any LWS occurring between field-content may be collapsed to a single SP before interpreting or forwarding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Any LWS that occurs between field-content MAY be replaced with a single SP before interpreting the field value | When unfolding continuation lines, client may replace the fold (CRLF + SP/HT runs) with one SP; interpretation must be unaffected. |
| `4-12` | informative | The receive order of header fields with different names carries no meaning. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The order in which header fields with differing field names are received is not significant. | Client must not attach semantics to relative ordering of differently-named response headers. |
| `4-13` | MAY | A message may contain multiple headers with the same field-name only if that header is defined as a comma-separated list. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Multiple message-header fields with the same field-name MAY be present in a message if and only if the entire field-value for that header field is defined as a comma-separated list | Request writer must not emit duplicate non-list headers (e.g. two Content-Length); parser must be prepared to receive duplicates of list-valued headers. |
| `4-14` | MUST | Multiple same-name header fields must be combinable into a single field by comma-joining the values in order, without changing message semantics. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | It MUST be possible to combine the multiple header fields into one "field-name: field-value" pair, without changing the semantics of the message | Client's header API/combining logic must treat 'H: a' + 'H: b' as equivalent to 'H: a,b' preserving order; never split a non-list value across fields when emitting. |
| `4-15` | informative | The receive order of same-name header fields is significant to the interpretation of the combined value. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The order in which header fields with the same field-name are received is therefore significant to the interpretation of the combined field value | Client header storage must preserve arrival order of same-name fields when parsing responses and insertion order when serializing requests. |
| `4-16` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not reorder same-name field values when forwarding a message. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | a proxy MUST NOT change the order of these field values when a message is forwarded. | Proxy-only obligation; no action for a pure client. |
| `4-17` | informative | Grammar: the message-body is the entity-body, possibly encoded per Transfer-Encoding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | message-body = entity-body \| <entity-body encoded as per Transfer-Encoding> | Client must decode transfer-codings (chunked) to recover the entity-body from the message-body on receipt, and encode when sending. |
| `4-18` | MUST | Any transfer-coding applied to a message must be declared via the Transfer-Encoding header. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Transfer-Encoding MUST be used to indicate any transfer-codings applied by an application to ensure safe and proper transfer of the message. | If the client sends a chunked request body it must include 'Transfer-Encoding: chunked'; never apply a coding silently. |
| `4-19` | MAY | Transfer-Encoding is a message property and may be added or removed by any application along the request/response chain. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Transfer-Encoding is a property of the message, not of the entity, and thus MAY be added or removed by any application along the request/response chain. | Client may choose to apply chunked coding to request bodies; must not assume the coding it receives is the one the origin applied. |
| `4-20` | MUST NOT | A request must not carry a message-body if the method's specification does not allow an entity-body in requests. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A message-body MUST NOT be included in a request if the specification of the request method (section 5.1.1) does not allow sending an entity-body in requests. | Client API must reject/omit bodies on methods whose spec disallows request entity-bodies (e.g. TRACE). |
| `4-21` | SHOULD | A server should read and forward a message-body on any request. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server SHOULD read and forward a message-body on any request | Server-only; no client obligation. |
| `4-22` | SHOULD | If the request method has no defined entity-body semantics, the server should ignore the body when handling the request. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | if the request method does not include defined semantics for an entity-body, then the message-body SHOULD be ignored when handling the request. | Server-only; no client obligation. |
| `4-23` | MUST NOT | Responses to HEAD never include a message-body, regardless of entity-header fields present. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | All responses to the HEAD request method MUST NOT include a message-body, even though the presence of entity-header fields might lead one to believe they do. | Client framing must track the request method: after a HEAD, terminate the response at the header terminator even if Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding is present, and keep the connection usable. |
| `4-24` | MUST NOT | 1xx, 204, and 304 responses never include a message-body. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | All 1xx (informational), 204 (no content), and 304 (not modified) responses MUST NOT include a message-body. | Client framing must treat 1xx/204/304 as bodiless: end the message at the empty line after headers, ignore any Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding for length purposes. |
| `4-25` | MAY | All other responses include a message-body, which may be zero length. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | All other responses do include a message-body, although it MAY be of zero length. | Client must handle zero-length bodies (Content-Length: 0, empty chunked body, immediate close) as valid complete responses. |
| `4-26` | informative | Length rule 1: any response that is forbidden a message-body is terminated by the first empty line after the headers, whatever entity-headers say. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | is always terminated by the first empty line after the header fields, regardless of the entity-header fields present in the message. | Framing precedence step 1: for HEAD responses and 1xx/204/304 statuses, message ends at the blank line — check this before consulting Transfer-Encoding or Content-Length. |
| `4-27` | informative | Length rule 2: if Transfer-Encoding is present with any value other than "identity", the transfer-length is determined by chunked coding (unless the message ends by connection close). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If a Transfer-Encoding header field (section 14.41) is present and has any value other than "identity", then the transfer-length is defined by use of the "chunked" transfer-coding (section 3.6) | Framing precedence step 2: on a non-identity Transfer-Encoding, read the body via the chunked decoder; fall back to read-until-close only if the message is close-delimited. |
| `4-28` | informative | Length rule 3: a present Content-Length gives the decimal octet count that is both entity-length and transfer-length. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If a Content-Length header field (section 14.13) is present, its decimal value in OCTETs represents both the entity-length and the transfer-length. | Framing precedence step 3: when rules 1-2 don't apply, read exactly Content-Length octets as the body. |
| `4-29` | MUST NOT | Content-Length must not be sent when entity-length and transfer-length differ, i.e. when a Transfer-Encoding header is present. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The Content-Length header field MUST NOT be sent if these two lengths are different (i.e., if a Transfer-Encoding header field is present). | Client request writer must never emit both Content-Length and Transfer-Encoding on the same request. |
| `4-30` | MUST | A recipient of a message with both Transfer-Encoding and Content-Length must ignore the Content-Length. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If a message is received with both a Transfer-Encoding header field and a Content-Length header field, the latter MUST be ignored. | Response framer must let Transfer-Encoding win: when both headers arrive, frame by chunked/close and disregard Content-Length entirely. |
| `4-31` | informative | Length rule 4: when the media type is multipart/byteranges and no other transfer-length is specified, that self-delimiting media type defines the transfer-length. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the message uses the media type "multipart/byteranges", and the ransfer-length is not otherwise specified, then this self-elimiting media type defines the transfer-length. | Source text is corrupted [sic: 'ransfer-length'='transfer-length', 'elimiting'='delimiting']. Client must either parse multipart/byteranges framing or avoid triggering it (see 4-33); note RFC 7230 later dropped this rule. |
| `4-32` | MUST NOT | multipart/byteranges must not be used as the delimiter unless the sender knows the recipient can parse it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This media type UST NOT be used unless the sender knows that the recipient can arse it | Source text is corrupted [sic: 'UST NOT'='MUST NOT', 'arse'='parse']. For the client: only advertise multi-range Range requests if the parser supports multipart/byteranges-delimited responses. |
| `4-33` | informative | A Range header with multiple byte-range specifiers sent by a 1.1 client implies the client can parse multipart/byteranges responses. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the presence in a request of a Range header with ultiple byte-range specifiers from a 1.1 client implies that the lient can parse multipart/byteranges responses. | Source text is corrupted [sic: 'ultiple'='multiple', 'lient'='client']. If the client API allows multi-range Range headers, it must implement multipart/byteranges body parsing; otherwise restrict to single ranges. |
| `4-34` | MUST | If a Range header was forwarded by a 1.0 proxy that does not understand multipart/byteranges, the server must delimit the message using items 1, 3, or 5. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | in this case the server MUST delimit the message using methods defined in items 1,3 or 5 of this section. | Server-only obligation; the client-side framings it falls back to (empty-line, Content-Length, close) are already required of the client. |
| `4-35` | informative | Length rule 5: the server closing the connection delimits the response; connection close cannot delimit a request body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | By the server closing the connection. (Closing the connection cannot be used to indicate the end of a request body | Client must support read-until-EOF response framing as the last-resort rule, and must never rely on half-close to terminate a request body it sends — always use Content-Length or chunked. |
| `4-36` | MUST | An HTTP/1.1 request containing a message-body must include a valid Content-Length unless the server is known to be HTTP/1.1 compliant. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 requests containing a message-body MUST include a valid Content-Length header field unless the server is known to be HTTP/1.1 compliant. | Client must default request bodies to Content-Length framing; only use chunked request bodies against servers known (e.g. from a prior 1.1 response on the connection) to be HTTP/1.1. |
| `4-37` | SHOULD | A server receiving a body without Content-Length should respond 400 if it cannot determine the length, or 411 to insist on Content-Length. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | the server SHOULD respond with 400 (bad request) if it cannot determine the length of the message, or with 411 (length required) | Server-only; a robust client may retry with Content-Length on 411, but no conformance obligation here. |
| `4-38` | MUST | Every HTTP/1.1 application that receives entities must accept the chunked transfer-coding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All HTTP/1.1 applications that receive entities MUST accept the "chunked" transfer-coding (section 3.6) | Client must implement a full chunked decoder (chunk-size line with extensions, chunk data, last-chunk, trailers, final CRLF) for response bodies. |
| `4-39` | MUST NOT | A message must not include both a Content-Length header and a non-identity transfer-coding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Messages MUST NOT include both a Content-Length header field and a non-identity transfer-coding. | Request writer must enforce mutual exclusion: setting Transfer-Encoding: chunked forbids emitting Content-Length (duplicate guard with 4-29). |
| `4-40` | MUST | If a message includes a non-identity transfer-coding, the Content-Length must be ignored. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the message does include a non-identity transfer-coding, the Content-Length MUST be ignored. | Response framer must discard Content-Length whenever a non-identity Transfer-Encoding is present (same behavior as 4-30; verify a single code path handles both). |
| `4-41` | MUST | When Content-Length is given in a message where a body is allowed, its value must exactly equal the number of octets in the message-body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | its field value MUST exactly match the number of OCTETs in the message-body. | Client emitting a request body must send exactly Content-Length octets; on receive, detect short reads (connection closed early) as invalid-length errors. |
| `4-42` | MUST | An HTTP/1.1 user agent must notify the user when it receives and detects an invalid length. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 user agents MUST notify the user when an invalid length is received and detected. | Client library must surface a distinct error (not silent truncation) to the caller when the body's actual octet count mismatches Content-Length. |
| `4-43` | informative | Grammar: the general-header fields are Cache-Control, Connection, Date, Pragma, Trailer, Transfer-Encoding, Upgrade, Via, and Warning. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | general-header = Cache-Control \| Connection \| Date \| Pragma \| Trailer \| Transfer-Encoding \| Upgrade \| Via \| Warning | **VERIFY** Classification only (quote joins the alternation lines, per-line '; Section 14.x' comments omitted); client needs no special dispatch but these headers apply to the message, not the entity. -- The quote deletes the nine inline ABNF section-reference comments that are present on every alternation line of the source (lines 1913-1921), which is a content alteration, not a line-wrap/whitespace/page-break artifact. The verbatim source reads: 'general-header = Cache-Control ; Section 14.9 \| Connection ; Section 14.10 \| Date ; Section 14.18 \| Pragma ; Section 14.32 \| Trailer ; Section 14.40 \| Transfer-Encoding ; Section 14.41 \| Upgrade ; Section 14.42 \| Via ; Section 14.45 \| Warning ; Section 14.46'. The requirement/classification (the nine general-header field names) is factually correct, but the quote itself is not verbatim. |
| `4-44` | informative | General-header field names can only be reliably extended with a protocol version change; experimental fields may act as general-headers if all parties recognize them. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | General-header field names can be extended reliably only in combination with a change in the protocol version. | **VERIFY** Protocol-evolution guidance; no client code obligation. -- only 1 verifier(s) |
| `4-45` | informative | Header fields not recognized as general/request/response/entity headers are treated as entity-header fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Unrecognized header fields are treated as entity-header fields. | **VERIFY** Client parser must accept unknown response headers without error and pass them through as ordinary (entity) headers — never reject a message over an unrecognized field name. -- only 1 verifier(s) |

### 5 - Request: Request-Line, Method, Request-URI, Host requirement (RFC 2616, June 1999)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `5-1` | informative | A Request consists of a Request-Line, zero or more general/request/entity headers each terminated by CRLF, an empty CRLF line, and an optional message body. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Request = Request-Line *(( general-header \| request-header \| entity-header ) CRLF) CRLF [ message-body ] | http1 request writer must emit exactly: request-line, each header + CRLF, one bare CRLF, then optional body — nothing else between. |
| `5-2` | informative | The three Request-Line elements (Method, Request-URI, HTTP-Version) are separated by SP characters. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | The elements are separated by SP characters. | Join method, URI, and version with exactly one SP each when serializing the request-line. |
| `5-3` | informative | No CR or LF may appear anywhere in the Request-Line except in the terminating CRLF sequence. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | No CR or LF is allowed except in the final CRLF sequence. | Reject any method or Request-URI containing CR or LF before writing the request-line (request-splitting/injection guard). |
| `5-4` | informative | Grammar: the Request-Line is Method SP Request-URI SP HTTP-Version terminated by CRLF, in that order. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Request-Line = Method SP Request-URI SP HTTP-Version CRLF | Emit exactly `METHOD SP uri SP HTTP/1.1 CRLF` with no leading/trailing whitespace or extra separators. |
| `5-5` | informative | The request method token is case-sensitive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The method is case-sensitive. | Never case-fold the method; send standard methods in their exact canonical uppercase form (GET, not get). |
| `5-6` | informative | Grammar: Method is one of the eight standard method names or any extension-method, where extension-method is a token. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Method = "OPTIONS" \| "GET" \| "HEAD" \| "POST" \| "PUT" \| "DELETE" \| "TRACE" \| "CONNECT" \| extension-method extension-method = token | Validate caller-supplied methods against the RFC 2616 token character set; arbitrary extension tokens are legal. |
| `5-7` | SHOULD | An origin server SHOULD return 405 (Method Not Allowed) when the method is known but not allowed for the requested resource. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD return the status code 405 (Method Not Allowed) if the method is known by the origin server but not allowed for the requested resource | No client implementation; ensure 405 is handled as a normal final response, never a transport error or retry trigger. |
| `5-8` | SHOULD | An origin server SHOULD return 501 (Not Implemented) when the method is unrecognized or not implemented by it. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | and 501 (Not Implemented) if the method is unrecognized or not implemented by the origin server | No client implementation; surface 501 to the caller without automatic retry. |
| `5-9` | MUST | All general-purpose servers MUST support the GET and HEAD methods. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The methods GET and HEAD MUST be supported by all general-purpose servers. | No client obligation; the client may assume GET/HEAD are available on general-purpose servers. |
| `5-10` | OPTIONAL | All methods other than GET and HEAD are OPTIONAL to implement. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All other methods are OPTIONAL | Client may support any subset of methods but must not assume server support for anything beyond GET/HEAD (expect 405/501). |
| `5-11` | MUST | If the standard methods are implemented, they MUST be implemented with the semantics specified in section 9. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | if the above methods are implemented, they MUST be implemented with the same semantics as those specified in section 9 | Any standard method the client exposes must carry section 9 semantics (e.g., HEAD expects no response body, GET carries no side-effect intent). |
| `5-12` | informative | Grammar: the Request-URI takes exactly one of four forms — "*", absoluteURI, abs_path, or authority. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Request-URI = "*" \| absoluteURI \| abs_path \| authority | Request-target serializer must produce exactly one of the four forms and pick the correct one for the situation (facts 5-13, 5-14, 5-19, 5-20). |
| `5-13` | informative | The "*" request-URI form is only allowed when the method does not necessarily apply to a specific resource (e.g., OPTIONS *). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | and is only allowed when the method used does not necessarily apply to a resource | Only emit "*" for server-wide requests such as `OPTIONS * HTTP/1.1`; reject "*" combined with resource-targeted methods. |
| `5-14` | REQUIRED | The absoluteURI request form is REQUIRED when the request is being sent to a proxy. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The absoluteURI form is REQUIRED when the request is being made to a proxy. | If/when the client gains forward-proxy support, the request-line URI must be the full absoluteURI (scheme://host/path), not abs_path. |
| `5-15` | MAY | A proxy MAY forward the request on to another proxy or directly to the server named by the absoluteURI. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | the proxy MAY forward the request on to another proxy or directly to the server | No client action; proxy routing behavior only. |
| `5-16` | MUST | To avoid request loops, a proxy MUST recognize all of its own server names, including aliases, local variations, and its numeric IP address. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | In order to avoid request loops, a proxy MUST be able to recognize all of its server names, including any aliases, local variations, and the numeric IP address. | No client action; proxy-only loop-prevention rule. |
| `5-17` | MUST | All HTTP/1.1 servers MUST accept the absoluteURI form in requests, to allow future transition to absolute URIs everywhere. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | all HTTP/1.1 servers MUST accept the absoluteURI form in requests | No client obligation; do not rely on it either — keep emitting abs_path to origin servers per 5-18/5-20. |
| `5-18` | informative | HTTP/1.1 clients only generate the absoluteURI form in requests directed to proxies, never to origin servers. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 clients will only generate them in requests to proxies | Check the request-line builder never emits scheme://host targets on direct origin connections — abs_path form only. |
| `5-19` | informative | The authority (host:port) request-URI form is used only with the CONNECT method. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The authority form is only used by the CONNECT method (section 9.9). | Emit a bare host:port request-target only when the method is CONNECT; reject authority form for all other methods. |
| `5-20` | MUST | For requests to an origin server or gateway, the absolute path of the URI MUST be transmitted as the Request-URI. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the absolute path of the URI MUST be transmitted (see section 3.2.1, abs_path) as the Request-URI | On direct origin/gateway requests the request-line target must be path (+ optional query) only — verify http1 writer strips scheme and authority. |
| `5-21` | MUST | For requests to an origin server or gateway, the network location (authority) of the URI MUST be transmitted in a Host header field. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the network location of the URI (authority) MUST be transmitted in a Host header field | Every request must carry a Host header set to the target URI's authority (host[:port]); verify http1 always injects it. |
| `5-22` | MUST | The absolute path in the Request-URI cannot be empty; if the original URI has no path, it MUST be sent as "/". <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the absolute path cannot be empty; if none is present in the original URI, it MUST be given as "/" (the server root) | Normalize an empty URL path to "/" before writing the request-line (e.g., `GET / HTTP/1.1` for http://example.com). |
| `5-23` | informative | The Request-URI is transmitted in the URI format specified in section 3.2.1 (RFC 2396 syntax with %-escaping). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The Request-URI is transmitted in the format specified in section 3.2.1. | Serialize/percent-encode the request target per section 3.2.1 URI rules; never emit raw unsafe characters (esp. SP, CTLs) in the URI. |
| `5-24` | MUST | If the Request-URI uses "% HEX HEX" encoding, the origin server MUST decode it in order to interpret the request properly. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If the Request-URI is encoded using the "% HEX HEX" encoding [42], the origin server MUST decode the Request-URI in order to properly interpret the request. | No client action; server-side decoding. Client may safely percent-encode reserved characters knowing the origin must decode. |
| `5-25` | SHOULD | Servers SHOULD respond to invalid Request-URIs with an appropriate status code. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers SHOULD respond to invalid Request-URIs with an appropriate status code. | No client implementation; expect an error status (not necessarily connection close) if a malformed URI slips through. |
| `5-26` | MUST NOT | A transparent proxy MUST NOT rewrite the abs_path part of a received Request-URI when forwarding, except to replace a null abs_path with "/". <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A transparent proxy MUST NOT rewrite the "abs_path" part of the received Request-URI when forwarding it to the next inbound server, except as noted above to replace a null abs_path with "/" | No client action; proxy-only forwarding constraint. |
| `5-27` | informative | The exact resource identified by a request is determined by examining both the Request-URI and the Host header field. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | The exact resource identified by an Internet request is determined by examining both the Request-URI and the Host header field. | Server-side determination; client hygiene follow-through is to keep the request-line target and Host header referring to the same authority. |
| `5-28` | MAY | An origin server that does not allow resources to differ by requested host MAY ignore the Host header value when identifying the resource. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server that does not allow resources to differ by the requested host MAY ignore the Host header field value | No client action; Host must still be sent regardless (5-21, section 14.23). |
| `5-29` | MUST | An origin server that differentiates resources by host (virtual hosting) MUST use rules 1-3 of section 5.2 to determine the requested resource. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | MUST use the following rules for determining the requested resource on an HTTP/1.1 request | No client action; virtual-host resolution algorithm binds the origin server. |
| `5-30` | MUST | Rule 1: if the Request-URI is an absoluteURI, the host comes from the Request-URI and any Host header value MUST be ignored by the server. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If Request-URI is an absoluteURI, the host is part of the Request-URI. Any Host header field value in the request MUST be ignored. | No client obligation here, but when sending absoluteURI (proxy mode) still include Host per 14.23 and keep it consistent — the server ignores it anyway. |
| `5-31` | informative | Rule 2: if the Request-URI is not an absoluteURI and the request includes a Host header, the host is determined by the Host header value. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If the Request-URI is not an absoluteURI, and the request includes a Host header field, the host is determined by the Host header field value. | No client action; this is why the Host value the client sends (5-21) must be the intended target authority. |
| `5-32` | MUST | Rule 3: if the host determined by rules 1 or 2 is not valid on the server, the server MUST respond with 400 (Bad Request). <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If the host as determined by rule 1 or 2 is not a valid host on the server, the response MUST be a 400 (Bad Request) error message. | No client implementation; a 400 can indicate the client derived a Host the server doesn't serve — useful diagnostic for Host-derivation bugs. |
| `5-33` | MAY | Recipients of an HTTP/1.0 request lacking a Host header MAY use heuristics (e.g., URI path examination) to determine the requested resource. <br>_(server,proxy [not-client])_ | Recipients of an HTTP/1.0 request that lacks a Host header field MAY attempt to use heuristics | No action for an HTTP/1.1 client — always sending Host (5-21) means the heuristic path never applies. |
| `5-34` | informative | Grammar: the request-header class comprises exactly these 19 fields: Accept, Accept-Charset, Accept-Encoding, Accept-Language, Authorization, Expect, From, Host, If-Match, If-Modified-Since, If-None-Match, If-Range, If-Unmodified-Since, Max-Forwards, Proxy-Authorization, Range, Referer, TE, User-Agent. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | request-header = Accept \| Accept-Charset \| Accept-Encoding \| Accept-Language \| Authorization \| Expect \| From \| Host \| If-Match \| If-Modified-Since \| If-None-Match \| If-Range \| If-Unmodified-Since \| Max-Forwards \| Proxy-Authorization \| Range \| Referer \| TE \| User-Agent | Treat these 19 names as request-header class when composing requests; all are legal for the client to emit alongside general- and entity-headers. |
| `5-35` | informative | Request-header field names can be extended reliably only in combination with a change in the protocol version. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Request-header field names can be extended reliably only in combination with a change in the protocol version. | No code obligation; design note — do not invent headers expecting request-header semantics without out-of-band agreement (see 5-36). |
| `5-36` | MAY | New or experimental header fields MAY be given request-header semantics if all parties in the communication recognize them as such. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | new or experimental header fields MAY be given the semantics of request-header fields if all parties in the communication recognize them to be request-header fields | Client may send arbitrary extension headers; only assume request-header (as opposed to entity-header) semantics when the peer is known to recognize them. |
| `5-37` | informative | Unrecognized header fields in a request are treated by recipients as entity-header fields. <br>_(server,proxy [not-client])_ | Unrecognized header fields are treated as entity-header fields. | Recipient-side classification rule; when the client emits custom headers, expect servers/proxies to handle them as entity-headers (no special hop-by-hop processing). |

### 6 - Response: Status-Line, Status Code and Reason Phrase

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `6-1` | informative | A server answers a received and interpreted request with exactly one HTTP response message, which the client must be prepared to receive and parse. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | After receiving and interpreting a request message, a server responds with an HTTP response message. | Client must expect one HTTP response message per request sent and parse it according to the Response grammar. |
| `6-2` | informative | A Response message is a Status-Line, followed by zero or more general/response/entity header fields each terminated by CRLF, then a bare CRLF, then an optional message-body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Response = Status-Line *(( general-header \| response-header \| entity-header ) CRLF) CRLF [ message-body ] | Response parser: read Status-Line, then header lines each ending CRLF, stop at the empty CRLF line, then read optional message-body per framing rules. |
| `6-3` | informative | The first line of a Response is the Status-Line: protocol version, then numeric status code, then textual reason phrase, each element separated by SP characters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | consisting of the protocol version followed by a numeric status code and its associated textual phrase, with each element separated by SP characters. | Parse the first response line by splitting on SP: version token, status code, remainder is the reason phrase. |
| `6-4` | informative | No CR or LF may appear anywhere inside the Status-Line except in the terminating CRLF sequence. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | No CR or LF is allowed except in the final CRLF sequence. | Treat any bare CR or LF inside the Status-Line before the terminating CRLF as a malformed response. |
| `6-5` | informative | Grammar: Status-Line = HTTP-Version SP Status-Code SP Reason-Phrase CRLF. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Status-Line = HTTP-Version SP Status-Code SP Reason-Phrase CRLF | Status-Line parser must require exactly this shape: version, single SP, 3-digit code, single SP, reason phrase (possibly empty), CRLF. |
| `6-6` | informative | The Status-Code element is a 3-digit integer result code describing the outcome of the attempt to understand and satisfy the request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The Status-Code element is a 3-digit integer result code of the attempt to understand and satisfy the request. | Parse Status-Code as exactly three ASCII digits; anything else in that position is a malformed Status-Line. |
| `6-7` | informative | The Status-Code is for automata and the Reason-Phrase for humans; the client is not required to examine or display the Reason-Phrase. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client is not required to examine or display the Reason-Phrase. | Client may capture the reason phrase as opaque text or ignore it; no logic may depend on examining it. |
| `6-8` | informative | The first digit of the Status-Code defines the response class; the last two digits carry no categorization role. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The first digit of the Status-Code defines the class of response. The last two digits do not have any categorization role. | Derive response class solely from the first digit (code/100); never infer class meaning from the last two digits. |
| `6-9` | informative | There are exactly five defined first-digit classes: 1xx Informational, 2xx Success, 3xx Redirection, 4xx Client Error, 5xx Server Error. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | There are 5 values for the first digit: | Client must recognize classes 1xx-5xx; first digits 0 and 6-9 fall outside any defined class and warrant malformed/undefined handling. |
| `6-10` | MAY | The listed reason phrases are only recommendations and MAY be replaced by local equivalents without affecting the protocol. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The reason phrases listed here are only recommendations -- they MAY be replaced by local equivalents without affecting the protocol. | Client must never compare the reason phrase against expected strings; accept any legal TEXT (including localized or arbitrary phrases). |
| `6-11` | informative | Grammar: Status-Code is one of the defined codes 100, 101, 200-206, 300-305, 307, 400-417, 500-505, or an extension-code. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Status-Code = "100" ; Section 10.1.1: Continue \| "101" ; Section 10.1.2: Switching Protocols | Client should know the defined codes (100, 101, 200-206, 300-305, 307, 400-417, 500-505); any other 3-digit value is an extension-code, not a parse error. |
| `6-12` | informative | Grammar: extension-code = 3DIGIT — any three-digit value is a syntactically valid status code. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | extension-code = 3DIGIT | Parser must accept any 3-digit status code as syntactically valid, including unassigned values like 431 or 599. |
| `6-13` | informative | Grammar: Reason-Phrase is zero or more TEXT octets excluding CR and LF; an empty reason phrase is legal. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Reason-Phrase = *<TEXT, excluding CR, LF> | Accept an empty reason phrase (Status-Line ending 'code SP CRLF') and any TEXT content; reject embedded CR or LF. |
| `6-14` | informative | HTTP status codes are extensible, and applications are not required to understand the meaning of all registered status codes. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP applications are not required to understand the meaning of all registered status codes, though such understanding is obviously desirable. | Client must not fail or error on registered-but-unimplemented status codes; only class-level handling (6-15) is mandatory. |
| `6-15` | MUST | Applications MUST understand the class of any status code from its first digit and MUST treat any unrecognized response as equivalent to the x00 code of that class. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | applications MUST understand the class of any status code, as indicated by the first digit, and treat any unrecognized response as being equivalent to the x00 status code of that class | For an unrecognized code (e.g. 431), client must apply the semantics of the class base code (400): check fallback-to-x00 logic exists for all five classes. |
| `6-16` | MUST NOT | A response with an unrecognized status code MUST NOT be cached. <br>_(cache,user-agent,proxy [CLIENT])_ | an unrecognized response MUST NOT be cached | If the client ever implements a response cache, it must never store responses with unrecognized status codes; vacuously satisfied while the client has no cache. |
| `6-17` | SHOULD | For an unrecognized status code, user agents SHOULD present the returned entity to the user, since it likely contains human-readable explanation. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | user agents SHOULD present to the user the entity returned with the response | Client library must still read and deliver the response body to the caller for unrecognized status codes, not discard it. |
| `6-18` | informative | Grammar: the response-header fields are Accept-Ranges, Age, ETag, Location, Proxy-Authenticate, Retry-After, Server, Vary, and WWW-Authenticate. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | response-header = Accept-Ranges \| Age \| ETag \| Location \| Proxy-Authenticate \| Retry-After \| Server \| Vary \| WWW-Authenticate | Client header handling may classify these nine fields as response-headers; all other unrecognized fields fall through to entity-header treatment (6-21). |
| `6-19` | informative | Response-header field names can be extended reliably only in combination with a change in the protocol version. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Response-header field names can be extended reliably only in combination with a change in the protocol version. | Client must not assume response-header semantics for unknown fields on an unchanged protocol version; treat them per 6-21 unless out-of-band agreement exists. |
| `6-20` | MAY | New or experimental header fields MAY be given response-header semantics if all parties in the communication recognize them as response-header fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | new or experimental header fields MAY be given the semantics of response-header fields if all parties in the communication recognize them to be response-header fields. | Optional: client may special-case mutually-recognized experimental fields as response-headers; default behavior must remain entity-header treatment. |
| `6-21` | informative | Unrecognized header fields in a response are treated as entity-header fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Unrecognized header fields are treated as entity-header fields. | Parser must accept unknown response header fields without error and preserve them (as entity-header/metadata), never reject or drop the response because of them. |

### 7 - Entity: entity-header fields, entity body, type, entity length

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `7-1` | MAY | Request and response messages may carry an entity unless the request method or response status code forbids one. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Request and Response messages MAY transfer an entity if not otherwise restricted by the request method or response status code. | Allow a body on any request whose method permits it, and be prepared to receive an entity on any response unless status/method rules (sec 4.3/4.4) forbid one. |
| `7-2` | informative | An entity is composed of entity-header fields plus an entity-body, and some responses carry only the entity-headers with no body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An entity consists of entity-header fields and an entity-body, although some responses will only include the entity-headers. | Response parser must accept entity-headers (Content-Type, Content-Length, etc.) on bodiless responses such as HEAD replies and 304 without error. |
| `7-3` | informative | Throughout section 7, 'sender' and 'recipient' each mean either the client or the server depending on the direction of the entity. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | both sender and recipient refer to either the client or the server, depending on who sends and who receives the entity. | Apply every section-7 recipient rule to the client's response handling and every sender rule to the client's request emission. |
| `7-4` | informative | Entity-header metainformation is generally optional, but individual headers may be made mandatory by other parts of the specification. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Some of this metainformation is OPTIONAL; some might be REQUIRED by portions of this specification. | Section 7 itself mandates no specific entity header; enforce per-header requirements from section 14 where they apply. |
| `7-5` | informative | Grammar: entity-header is one of Allow, Content-Encoding, Content-Language, Content-Length, Content-Location, Content-MD5, Content-Range, Content-Type, Expires, Last-Modified, or an extension-header. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | entity-header = Allow ; Section 14.7 \| Content-Encoding ; Section 14.11 ... \| Last-Modified ; Section 14.29 \| extension-header | Header classification must recognize the ten enumerated entity headers; anything else parses as an extension-header rather than an error. |
| `7-6` | informative | Grammar: an extension-header uses the generic message-header syntax. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | extension-header = message-header | Parse unknown entity headers with the same generic field-name/field-value (message-header, sec 4.2) rules as any other header. |
| `7-7` | informative | Extension entity-header fields cannot be assumed to be recognized by the recipient. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | but these fields cannot be assumed to be recognizable by the recipient. | Never make request correctness depend on the server understanding a non-standard entity header the client emits. |
| `7-8` | SHOULD | A recipient should ignore header fields it does not recognize. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Unrecognized header fields SHOULD be ignored by the recipient | Unknown response headers must never cause a parse failure; store or skip them without acting on them. |
| `7-9` | MUST | Transparent proxies must forward unrecognized header fields. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | MUST be forwarded by transparent proxies. | No client obligation — proxy-only forwarding rule; nothing to implement in a pure client. |
| `7-10` | informative | The format and encoding of any entity-body sent in a request or response is defined by the accompanying entity-header fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The entity-body (if any) sent with an HTTP request or response is in a format and encoding defined by the entity-header fields. | Interpret received bodies per their entity headers; when sending, emit entity headers that accurately describe the body bytes actually sent. |
| `7-11` | informative | Grammar: the entity-body is an arbitrary sequence of octets. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | entity-body = *OCTET | Treat the body as opaque binary at the framing layer — no charset, line-ending, or textual assumptions. |
| `7-12` | informative | An entity-body exists in a message only when a message-body is present per the section 4.3 rules. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An entity-body is only present in a message when a message-body is present, as described in section 4.3. | Decide body presence from sec 4.3 framing rules (method and status code), never from the mere presence of entity headers. |
| `7-13` | informative | The recipient obtains the entity-body by decoding whatever Transfer-Encoding was applied to the message-body. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | The entity-body is obtained from the message-body by decoding any Transfer-Encoding that might have been applied to ensure safe and proper transfer of the message. | Client must strip transfer-codings (e.g. chunked) before exposing the body; entity headers describe the transfer-decoded body, not the wire bytes. |
| `7-14` | informative | The data type of an included entity-body is determined by the Content-Type and Content-Encoding header fields together. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the data type of that body is determined via the header fields Content-Type and Content-Encoding. These define a two-layer, ordered encoding model | Characterize a received entity using both Content-Type and Content-Encoding, treating them as an ordered two-layer model. |
| `7-15` | informative | Grammar/model: the entity-body equals Content-Encoding applied over Content-Type applied over the data — content-coding is the outer layer. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | entity-body := Content-Encoding( Content-Type( data ) ) | If the client decodes content-codings, remove Content-Encoding first (outermost), then interpret the media type per Content-Type. |
| `7-16` | informative | Content-Type specifies the media type of the underlying data. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Type specifies the media type of the underlying data. | Map Content-Type to the media type of the (content-decoded) data when surfacing the response to the caller. |
| `7-17` | informative | Content-Encoding indicates additional content codings applied to the data (usually compression) that are a property of the requested resource. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Encoding may be used to indicate any additional content codings applied to the data, usually for the purpose of data compression, that are a property of the requested resource. | Treat Content-Encoding as an end-to-end property of the entity: if the client does not decode it, pass the header and coded bytes through together. |
| `7-18` | informative | There is no default content encoding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | There is no default encoding. | Absent Content-Encoding, treat the body as identity-coded; never assume any coding by default. |
| `7-19` | SHOULD | Any HTTP/1.1 message that contains an entity-body should carry a Content-Type header field giving the body's media type. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Any HTTP/1.1 message containing an entity-body SHOULD include a Content-Type header field defining the media type of that body. | Client should set Content-Type on every request it sends with a body (or expose an API making omission a deliberate caller choice). |
| `7-20` | MAY | Only when no Content-Type field is present may the recipient guess the media type by inspecting the content and/or the URI's name extension. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | If and only if the media type is not given by a Content-Type field, the recipient MAY attempt to guess the media type via inspection of its content | Content sniffing is permitted only when Content-Type is absent; an explicit Content-Type must never be overridden by sniffing. |
| `7-21` | SHOULD | If the media type cannot be determined, the recipient should treat the entity as application/octet-stream. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | If the media type remains unknown, the recipient SHOULD treat it as type "application/octet-stream". | When surfacing a typed body with no determinable media type, default it to application/octet-stream. |
| `7-22` | informative | The entity-length of a message is the length of the message-body before any transfer-codings were applied. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The entity-length of a message is the length of the message-body before any transfer-codings have been applied. | Keep entity-length (post-transfer-decode size) conceptually distinct from transfer-length (wire size); they differ whenever a transfer-coding like chunked is applied. |
| `7-23` | informative | How much message-body to read (the transfer-length) is determined by the rules of section 4.4, not by section 7. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Section 4.4 defines how the transfer-length of a message-body is determined. | The client's body-framing logic (how many octets to consume) must implement sec 4.4; section 7 only defines what the decoded entity is. |

### 8 - Connections: persistent connections, 100-continue, premature close (RFC 2616 §8, lines 2415-2813)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `8-1` | SHOULD | HTTP implementations should support persistent connections. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP implementations SHOULD implement persistent connections. | http1 transport must default to connection reuse (keep-alive) rather than one-connection-per-request. |
| `8-2` | SHOULD | Absent contrary indication, a client should assume the server keeps the connection persistent, even after error responses. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | unless otherwise indicated, the client SHOULD assume that the server will maintain a persistent connection, even after error responses from the server. | Default to persistent on HTTP/1.1 responses (including 4xx/5xx) unless Connection: close or missing self-delimiting length. |
| `8-3` | MUST NOT | After close is signaled via Connection header, the client must not send further requests on that connection. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Once a close has been signaled, the client MUST NOT send any more requests on that connection. | On seeing close token (sent or received), mark conn dead in pool; never dispatch another request on it. |
| `8-4` | MAY | A server may assume an HTTP/1.1 client wants persistence unless the request carried Connection: close. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | An HTTP/1.1 server MAY assume that a HTTP/1.1 client intends to maintain a persistent connection unless a Connection header including the connection-token "close" was sent in the request. | Omitting Connection: close in a request signals persistence intent — client must only omit it when it will actually reuse the conn. |
| `8-5` | SHOULD | A server closing immediately after the response should include Connection: close in that response. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | If the server chooses to close the connection immediately after sending the response, it SHOULD send a Connection header including the connection-token close. | SHOULD not MUST: client must also tolerate close WITHOUT the token (async close recovery, 8-26). |
| `8-6` | MAY | An HTTP/1.1 client may expect persistence but decides based on whether the response contains Connection: close. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | An HTTP/1.1 client MAY expect a connection to remain open, but would decide to keep it open based on whether the response from a server contains a Connection header with the connection-token close. | Parse response Connection header; if close token present, do not return conn to pool. |
| `8-7` | SHOULD | A client not wanting persistence beyond this request should send Connection: close in the request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | In case the client does not want to maintain a connection for more than that request, it SHOULD send a Connection header including the connection-token close. | Expose/emit Connection: close on requests when caller disables keep-alive or conn is being retired. |
| `8-8` | informative | Whichever side sends the close token, that request is the last one on the connection. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | If either the client or the server sends the close token in the Connection header, that request becomes the last one for the connection. | Close-token semantics are symmetric: after a request sent with Connection: close, tear down after its response completes. |
| `8-9` | SHOULD NOT | Do not assume persistence for HTTP versions below 1.1 unless explicitly signaled. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Clients and servers SHOULD NOT assume that a persistent connection is maintained for HTTP versions less than 1.1 unless it is explicitly signaled. | On an HTTP/1.0 response, default to non-persistent unless it carries an explicit keep-alive signal. |
| `8-10` | MUST | To remain persistent, every message on the connection must have a self-defined length (not delimited by connection close). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In order to remain persistent, all messages on the connection MUST have a self-defined message length (i.e., one not defined by closure of the connection), as described in section 4.4. | If a response has neither Content-Length nor chunked framing (read-to-EOF body), the conn must not be reused; requests the client emits with bodies must always be self-delimited. |
| `8-11` | MAY | A client supporting persistent connections may pipeline requests without waiting for each response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that supports persistent connections MAY "pipeline" its requests (i.e., send multiple requests without waiting for each response). | Pipelining is optional; if implemented, all pipelining constraints 8-12..8-17 apply. |
| `8-12` | MUST | A server must send responses to pipelined requests in the order the requests were received. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MUST send its responses to those requests in the same order that the requests were received. | Client-side pipelining may correlate responses to requests strictly FIFO — this ordering guarantee is the basis of correlation. |
| `8-13` | SHOULD | Clients that pipeline immediately after connection establishment should be prepared to retry if the first pipelined attempt fails. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients which assume persistent connections and pipeline immediately after connection establishment SHOULD be prepared to retry their connection if the first pipelined attempt fails. | If pipelining on a fresh conn, implement reconnect-and-retry for the failed batch. |
| `8-14` | MUST NOT | On such a retry the client must not pipeline until it knows the connection is persistent. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If a client does such a retry, it MUST NOT pipeline before it knows the connection is persistent. | Retry path must send one request, confirm persistence from its response, only then resume pipelining. |
| `8-15` | MUST | Clients must be prepared to resend requests if the server closes before delivering all corresponding responses. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients MUST also be prepared to resend their requests if the server closes the connection before sending all of the corresponding responses. | Track which pipelined requests got responses; unanswered ones must be re-issuable (subject to idempotency rules 8-27). |
| `8-16` | SHOULD NOT | Clients should not pipeline non-idempotent methods or non-idempotent sequences of methods. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients SHOULD NOT pipeline requests using non-idempotent methods or non-idempotent sequences of methods (see section 9.1.2). | Pipelining implementation must gate on method idempotency (GET/HEAD/PUT/DELETE ok; POST not). |
| `8-17` | SHOULD | A client sending a non-idempotent request should wait until it has the response status for the previous request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client wishing to send a non-idempotent request SHOULD wait to send that request until it has received the response status for the previous request. | Non-idempotent requests serialize: do not write one until prior response's status line has arrived. |
| `8-18` | MUST | A proxy must signal persistence separately on each side (client-facing and origin/next-proxy-facing). <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | The proxy server MUST signal persistent connections separately with its clients and the origin servers (or other proxy servers) that it connects to. | Proxy-only; no client code path. |
| `8-19` | informative | Persistent-connection signaling is hop-by-hop: each persistent connection applies to a single transport link. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Each persistent connection applies to only one transport link. | When connecting through a proxy, Connection semantics apply only to the client-proxy hop; never treat them end-to-end. |
| `8-20` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not establish an HTTP/1.1 persistent connection with an HTTP/1.0 client. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy server MUST NOT establish a HTTP/1.1 persistent connection with an HTTP/1.0 client | Proxy-only; irrelevant to a 1.1 client implementation. |
| `8-21` | informative | Persistent connections impose no requirement on the length or existence of idle time-outs on either side. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | The use of persistent connections places no requirements on the length (or existence) of this time-out for either the client or the server. | Pool idle-timeout is free to be any value (or none); no conformance constraint on the number. |
| `8-22` | SHOULD | When a client or server times out a connection it should close it gracefully. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | When a client or server wishes to time-out it SHOULD issue a graceful close on the transport connection. | Idle-eviction path should do orderly TCP close (not abortive RST) on pooled conns. |
| `8-23` | SHOULD | Clients and servers should constantly watch for the peer's transport close and respond appropriately. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Clients and servers SHOULD both constantly watch for the other side of the transport close, and respond to it as appropriate. | Pooled idle conns need a read-watch (or pre-use liveness check) so server-side close is detected promptly, not on next write. |
| `8-24` | MAY | Any party may close the transport connection at any time. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A client, server, or proxy MAY close the transport connection at any time. | Never treat an unexpected close as protocol violation; it can race with a request the client just started writing. |
| `8-25` | MUST | Clients, servers, and proxies must be able to recover from asynchronous close events. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This means that clients, servers, and proxies MUST be able to recover from asynchronous close events. | A write/read error on a reused pooled conn must be recoverable (surface a retryable error or retry internally), not fatal. |
| `8-26` | SHOULD | Client software should transparently reopen the connection and retransmit an aborted request sequence when the sequence is idempotent. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Client software SHOULD reopen the transport connection and retransmit the aborted sequence of requests without user interaction so long as the request sequence is idempotent | Retry layer should auto-retry idempotent requests that died on a stale pooled conn before any response arrived. |
| `8-27` | MUST NOT | Non-idempotent methods or sequences must not be automatically retried. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Non-idempotent methods or sequences MUST NOT be automatically retried | Retry policy must exclude non-idempotent methods (e.g. POST) from automatic replay after asynchronous close — verify client/retry gate. |
| `8-28` | MAY | User agents may offer a human operator the choice of retrying non-idempotent requests. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | user agents MAY offer a human operator the choice of retrying the request(s). | For a library: surface the failure so the CALLER can decide to retry; equivalent of operator choice. |
| `8-29` | MAY | Confirmation by user-agent software with semantic understanding of the application may substitute for user confirmation. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Confirmation by user-agent software with semantic understanding of the application MAY substitute for user confirmation. | An explicit caller-provided retry predicate (opt-in retry of non-idempotent requests) is permitted as the semantic confirmation. |
| `8-30` | SHOULD NOT | An automatic retry should not be repeated if the second attempt also fails. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The automatic retry SHOULD NOT be repeated if the second sequence of requests fails. | Async-close auto-retry is one-shot by default; further retries only via explicit caller policy. |
| `8-31` | SHOULD | Servers should respond to at least one request per connection if at all possible. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers SHOULD always respond to at least one request per connection, if at all possible. | Server-only; client just benefits. |
| `8-32` | SHOULD NOT | Servers should not close a connection in the middle of transmitting a response unless failure is suspected. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers SHOULD NOT close a connection in the middle of transmitting a response, unless a network or client failure is suspected. | Server-only; client must still detect truncated bodies (Content-Length short-read → error) since this is only SHOULD NOT. |
| `8-33` | SHOULD | Clients using persistent connections should limit simultaneous connections to a given server. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients that use persistent connections SHOULD limit the number of simultaneous connections that they maintain to a given server. | Pool must have a per-host max-connections cap (configurable). |
| `8-34` | SHOULD NOT | A single-user client should not maintain more than 2 connections to any server or proxy. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A single-user client SHOULD NOT maintain more than 2 connections with any server or proxy. | Historic guidance (relaxed by RFC 7230); a load generator intentionally exceeds it — document the deliberate deviation, keep cap configurable. |
| `8-35` | SHOULD | A proxy should use at most 2*N connections to another server or proxy, N being simultaneously active users. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy SHOULD use up to 2*N connections to another server or proxy, where N is the number of simultaneously active users. | Proxy-only. |
| `8-36` | SHOULD | Servers should keep connections open and rely on TCP flow control for overload instead of closing and expecting client retry. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | HTTP/1.1 servers SHOULD maintain persistent connections and use TCP's flow control mechanisms to resolve temporary overloads, rather than terminating connections with the expectation that clients will retry. | Server-only; client write path must nonetheless tolerate TCP backpressure (blocking writes) as the normal overload mechanism. |
| `8-37` | SHOULD | A client transmitting a request body should monitor the connection for an error status during transmission. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | An HTTP/1.1 (or later) client sending a message-body SHOULD monitor the network connection for an error status while it is transmitting the request. | While streaming a request body, concurrently read for an early response (error status) instead of write-then-read strictly. |
| `8-38` | SHOULD | On seeing an error status mid-upload, the client should immediately stop transmitting the body. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the client sees an error status, it SHOULD immediately cease transmitting the body. | Early-response detection must abort the body writer promptly. |
| `8-39` | MAY | If the body is chunked, a zero-length chunk with empty trailer may be used to end the message prematurely. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the body is being sent using a "chunked" encoding (section 3.6), a zero length chunk and empty trailer MAY be used to prematurely mark the end of the message. | Abort path for chunked uploads: emit 0\r\n\r\n to terminate cleanly and keep the conn reusable. |
| `8-40` | MUST | If the aborted body was framed with Content-Length, the client must close the connection. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the body was preceded by a Content-Length header, the client MUST close the connection. | Aborting a Content-Length upload mid-body poisons framing: conn must be closed, never returned to pool. |
| `8-41` | MUST | A client that will wait for 100 (Continue) before sending the body must send Expect: 100-continue. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If a client will wait for a 100 (Continue) response before sending the request body, it MUST send an Expect request-header field (section 14.20) with the "100-continue" expectation. | Any wait-before-body behavior must be coupled to actually emitting Expect: 100-continue; never wait without sending it. |
| `8-42` | MUST NOT | A client must not send Expect: 100-continue if it does not intend to send a request body. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST NOT send an Expect request-header field (section 14.20) with the "100-continue" expectation if it does not intend to send a request body. | Validate/strip Expect: 100-continue on bodyless requests (GET/HEAD or nil body). |
| `8-43` | informative | The protocol permits ambiguous cases where a client sends Expect: 100-continue yet never receives either a 100 or a 417. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the protocol allows ambiguous situations in which a client may send "Expect: 100-continue" without receiving either a 417 (Expectation Failed) status or a 100 (Continue) status. | Design premise: 100-continue handling cannot block on a guaranteed interim response; a timeout fallback is required (8-44). |
| `8-44` | SHOULD NOT | When sending Expect: 100-continue to a server never observed to emit 100, the client should not wait indefinitely before sending the body. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client SHOULD NOT wait for an indefinite period before sending the request body. | Bound the 100-continue wait with a timeout, after which the body is transmitted anyway. |
| `8-45` | MUST | An origin server receiving Expect: 100-continue must either respond 100 and keep reading, or respond with a final status code. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | an origin server MUST either respond with 100 (Continue) status and continue to read from the input stream, or respond with a final status code. | Client state machine after sending Expect must handle both shapes: 100 then final response, or an immediate final response with no 100. |
| `8-46` | MUST NOT | The origin server must not wait for the request body before sending the 100 (Continue) response. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | The origin server MUST NOT wait for the request body before sending the 100 (Continue) response. | Server-only; underpins why the client can legitimately hold the body while waiting. |
| `8-47` | MAY | A server answering the expectation with a final status may close the connection or may read and discard the rest of the request. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | If it responds with a final status code, it MAY close the transport connection or it MAY continue to read and discard the rest of the request. | After a final status arrives instead of 100, client must tolerate either server close or a conn that remains readable; don't assume reuse. |
| `8-48` | MUST NOT | A server returning a final status code to an Expect request must not perform the requested method. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | It MUST NOT perform the requested method if it returns a final status code. | Server-only semantic guarantee; client may treat pre-body final error as method-not-executed. |
| `8-49` | SHOULD NOT | An origin server should not send 100 (Continue) when the request lacks the 100-continue expectation. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | An origin server SHOULD NOT send a 100 (Continue) response if the request message does not include an Expect request-header field with the "100-continue" expectation | Only SHOULD NOT — client response parser must tolerate an unsolicited 100 (skip it and read the next response); see 8-51. |
| `8-50` | MUST NOT | An origin server must not send 100 (Continue) in response to a request from an HTTP/1.0 (or earlier) client. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | MUST NOT send a 100 (Continue) response if such a request comes from an HTTP/1.0 (or earlier) client. | Binds servers; only matters if the client emits HTTP/1.0 requests (poseidon emits 1.1). |
| `8-51` | MAY | For RFC 2068 compatibility a server may send an unsolicited 100 (Continue) to an HTTP/1.1 PUT or POST without the Expect header. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | a server MAY send a 100 (Continue) status in response to an HTTP/1.1 PUT or POST request that does not include an Expect request-header field with the "100-continue" expectation. | Client MUST tolerate a stray 100 before the real response on any PUT/POST even when it never sent Expect — skip 1xx and keep reading. |
| `8-52` | MAY | An origin server may omit the 100 (Continue) response if it has already received some or all of the request body. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | An origin server MAY omit a 100 (Continue) response if it has already received some or all of the request body for the corresponding request. | Once the client starts sending the body (e.g. after timeout 8-44), it must not still require a 100 to arrive. |
| `8-53` | MUST | A server that sent 100 (Continue) must ultimately send a final status code unless it prematurely terminates the connection. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | An origin server that sends a 100 (Continue) response MUST ultimately send a final status code, once the request body is received and processed, unless it terminates the transport connection prematurely. | After receiving 100, client keeps the transaction open awaiting a final status; a close instead of final status is a premature-close error. |
| `8-54` | SHOULD NOT | A server responding early (no Expect, body present) should not close the connection until it has read the entire request or the client closes. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | the server SHOULD NOT close the transport connection until it has read the entire request, or until the client closes the connection. | Server-only; client should still be robust to reset-before-response-read (retry logic 8-60) since it is only SHOULD NOT. |
| `8-55` | MUST | A proxy receiving Expect: 100-continue must forward the request including the Expect header when the next hop is HTTP/1.1+ or of unknown version. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | it MUST forward the request, including the Expect header field. | Proxy-only. |
| `8-56` | MUST NOT | If the proxy knows the next-hop server is HTTP/1.0 or lower, it must not forward the request. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | If the proxy knows that the version of the next-hop server is HTTP/1.0 or lower, it MUST NOT forward the request | Proxy-only. |
| `8-57` | MUST | In that case the proxy must respond with 417 (Expectation Failed). <br>_(proxy [CLIENT])_ | it MUST respond with a 417 (Expectation Failed) status. | Client sending Expect: 100-continue must handle a 417 final response (typically: retry the request without the Expect header). |
| `8-58` | SHOULD | Proxies should cache the HTTP version numbers of recently-referenced next-hop servers. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Proxies SHOULD maintain a cache recording the HTTP version numbers received from recently-referenced next-hop servers. | Proxy-only. |
| `8-59` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not forward a 100 (Continue) response to an HTTP/1.0-or-earlier client whose request lacked the 100-continue expectation. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST NOT forward a 100 (Continue) response if the request message was received from an HTTP/1.0 (or earlier) client and did not include an Expect request-header field | Proxy-only; overrides the general 1xx-forwarding rule of section 10.1. |
| `8-60` | SHOULD | A client that sent a body without Expect: 100-continue, not directly connected to an HTTP/1.1 origin, and saw close before any status, should retry the request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | if the client sees the connection close before receiving any status from the server, the client SHOULD retry the request. | Premature-close-before-any-status on a request with a body (no Expect sent, possibly via proxy) triggers a retry path. |
| `8-61` | MAY | The retrying client may use the binary exponential backoff algorithm to obtain a reliable response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the client does retry this request, it MAY use the following "binary exponential backoff" algorithm to be assured of obtaining a reliable response: | Optional: reconnect, send headers, wait T before body, loop until accepted/error/caller-abort — exponential backoff in the retry layer suffices. |
| `8-62` | informative | In the backoff algorithm the wait before transmitting the body is T = R * 2^N, R being estimated RTT (or 5 seconds if unknown), N the number of previous retries. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Compute T = R * (2**N), where N is the number of previous retries of this request. | If implementing 8-61, base R on connect RTT with a 5s fallback and double per retry. |
| `8-63` | SHOULD NOT | If an error status is received at any point during the retry procedure, the client should not continue. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If at any point an error status is received, the client - SHOULD NOT continue and | An error response terminates the retry loop; do not transmit the (remaining) body or re-attempt. |
| `8-64` | SHOULD | On receiving that error status, the client should close the connection if it has not finished sending the request message. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | SHOULD close the connection if it has not completed sending the request message. | Error mid-request-transmission: close the conn (framing is unsalvageable), consistent with 8-40. |

### 9 - Method Definitions

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9-1` | informative | Extension methods beyond the defined set cannot be assumed to carry the same semantics for independently extended clients and servers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Although this set can be expanded, additional methods cannot be assumed to share the same semantics for separately extended clients and servers. | Treat unknown/extension methods as neither safe nor idempotent: never auto-retry them and make no framing assumptions beyond generic rules. |
| `9-2` | MUST | Every HTTP/1.1 request must carry a Host request-header field. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The Host request-header field (section 14.23) MUST accompany all HTTP/1.1 requests. | Verify the http1 request writer emits Host on every request, all methods included (OPTIONS *, CONNECT authority form too). |
| `9-3` | SHOULD NOT | GET and HEAD should not be used to take actions other than retrieval; they are conventionally 'safe'. <br>_(user-agent,origin [CLIENT])_ | the convention has been established that the GET and HEAD methods SHOULD NOT have the significance of taking an action other than retrieval. | Classify GET/HEAD as safe in the client's retry/redirect policy; do not silently rewrite unsafe methods into safe ones or vice versa. |
| `9-4` | informative | GET, HEAD, PUT and DELETE are idempotent: N > 0 identical requests have the same side-effects as one request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the side-effects of N > 0 identical requests is the same as for a single request. The methods GET, HEAD, PUT and DELETE share this property. | Retry policy: GET/HEAD/PUT/DELETE are eligible for automatic replay after transport failure; POST is not. |
| `9-5` | SHOULD NOT | OPTIONS and TRACE should have no side effects and are therefore inherently idempotent. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | the methods OPTIONS and TRACE SHOULD NOT have side effects, and so are inherently idempotent. | Add OPTIONS and TRACE to the idempotent set in the client retry classifier. |
| `9-6` | informative | A sequence of requests may be non-idempotent even when each individual method in it is idempotent. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | it is possible that a sequence of several requests is non-idempotent, even if all of the methods executed in that sequence are idempotent. | Make retry decisions per individual request only; never blindly replay a whole pipelined/batched sequence after a mid-sequence failure. |
| `9-7` | informative | Responses to OPTIONS are not cacheable. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to this method are not cacheable. | No client obligation (poseidon has no cache); if a cache layer is ever added, never store OPTIONS responses. |
| `9-8` | MUST | An OPTIONS request that carries an entity-body (signalled by Content-Length or Transfer-Encoding) must declare the media type with Content-Type. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the OPTIONS request includes an entity-body (as indicated by the presence of Content-Length or Transfer-Encoding), then the media type MUST be indicated by a Content-Type field. | When emitting OPTIONS with a body, require or auto-set Content-Type before writing the request; validate in the request builder. |
| `9-9` | MAY | A server not supporting an OPTIONS-body extension may discard the request body. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that does not support such an extension MAY discard the request body. | No client code obligation; callers must not depend on an OPTIONS request body being interpreted. |
| `9-10` | informative | OPTIONS with Request-URI "*" applies to the server as a whole, not a specific resource (usable as a ping/no-op). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the Request-URI is an asterisk ("*"), the OPTIONS request is intended to apply to the server in general rather than to a specific resource. | Request-line writer must support the literal asterisk-form target "OPTIONS * HTTP/1.1" without path normalization or escaping. |
| `9-11` | SHOULD | A 200 response to OPTIONS should include header fields indicating optional features the server implements for that resource (e.g. Allow). <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A 200 response SHOULD include any header fields that indicate optional features implemented by the server and applicable to that resource (e.g., Allow), possibly including extensions not defined by this specification. | Server-side; client may optionally parse Allow but has no obligation. |
| `9-12` | SHOULD | An OPTIONS response body, if present, should describe the communication options. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The response body, if any, SHOULD also include information about the communication options. | Server-side; client just delivers any body bytes to the caller. |
| `9-13` | MAY | Content negotiation may be used to select the OPTIONS response body format. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Content negotiation MAY be used to select the appropriate response format. | Server-side; no client action. |
| `9-14` | MUST | An OPTIONS response without a body must carry Content-Length: 0. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | If no response body is included, the response MUST include a Content-Length field with a field-value of "0". | Binds server emission; client response parser already handles Content-Length: 0 with empty body via generic framing — nothing extra. |
| `9-15` | MAY | The client may use Max-Forwards on OPTIONS to target a specific proxy in the request chain. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The Max-Forwards request-header field MAY be used to target a specific proxy in the request chain. | Allow callers to set Max-Forwards on OPTIONS requests; the client must pass it through unmodified. |
| `9-16` | MUST | A proxy receiving a forwardable OPTIONS on an absoluteURI must check for a Max-Forwards field. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | When a proxy receives an OPTIONS request on an absoluteURI for which request forwarding is permitted, the proxy MUST check for a Max-Forwards field. | Proxy-only; irrelevant unless a proxy mode is ever built. |
| `9-17` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not forward an OPTIONS request whose Max-Forwards value is zero. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | If the Max-Forwards field-value is zero ("0"), the proxy MUST NOT forward the message; | Proxy-only; no client obligation. |
| `9-18` | SHOULD | Instead of forwarding a Max-Forwards: 0 OPTIONS, the proxy should answer with its own communication options. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | instead, the proxy SHOULD respond with its own communication options. | Proxy-only; no client obligation. |
