| `9-19` | MUST | A proxy forwarding OPTIONS with Max-Forwards greater than zero must decrement the field-value. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | If the Max-Forwards field-value is an integer greater than zero, the proxy MUST decrement the field-value when it forwards the request. | Proxy-only; no client obligation. |
| `9-20` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not add a Max-Forwards field when forwarding a request that had none. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | If no Max-Forwards field is present in the request, then the forwarded request MUST NOT include a Max-Forwards field. | Proxy-only; no client obligation. |
| `9-21` | informative | GET means retrieve whatever information (as an entity) is identified by the Request-URI. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The GET method means retrieve whatever information (in the form of an entity) is identified by the Request-URI. | Semantics only; client emits GET with no body expectations beyond generic framing. |
| `9-22` | informative | Including If-Modified-Since, If-Unmodified-Since, If-Match, If-None-Match or If-Range turns a GET into a conditional GET (entity transferred only under those conditions). <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | The semantics of the GET method change to a "conditional GET" if the request message includes an If-Modified-Since, If-Unmodified-Since, If-Match, If-None-Match, or If-Range header field. | Pass conditional headers through untouched; the caller must handle 304 (no-body) responses — parser must frame 304 correctly. |
| `9-23` | informative | Including a Range header turns a GET into a partial GET requesting only part of the entity. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | The semantics of the GET method change to a "partial GET" if the request message includes a Range header field. | Pass Range through untouched; caller must be prepared for 206 partial-content responses. |
| `9-24` | informative | A GET response is cacheable if and only if it meets the section 13 caching requirements. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | The response to a GET request is cacheable if and only if it meets the requirements for HTTP caching described in section 13. | Cache-only; no obligation for a cacheless client. |
| `9-25` | MUST NOT | HEAD is identical to GET except the server must not return a message-body in the response. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The HEAD method is identical to GET except that the server MUST NOT return a message-body in the response. | CRITICAL framing rule: the http1 response reader must key framing off the request method — after HEAD, never read a body even when Content-Length or Transfer-Encoding is present. |
| `9-26` | SHOULD | Headers in a HEAD response should be identical to those that a GET would return. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The metainformation contained in the HTTP headers in response to a HEAD request SHOULD be identical to the information sent in response to a GET request. | Server-side; client may expose HEAD headers to callers but must not rely on GET-equivalence. |
| `9-27` | MAY | A HEAD response may be cacheable in the sense that its header information may update a previously cached entity. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | The response to a HEAD request MAY be cacheable in the sense that the information contained in the response MAY be used to update a previously cached entity from that resource. | Cache-only; no obligation. |
| `9-28` | MUST | If HEAD response field values (Content-Length, Content-MD5, ETag, Last-Modified) show the cached entity differs from the current one, the cache must treat the entry as stale. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | then the cache MUST treat the cache entry as stale. | Cache-only; no obligation for a cacheless client. |
| `9-29` | informative | POST requests that the origin server accept the enclosed entity as a new subordinate of the Request-URI resource. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The POST method is used to request that the origin server accept the entity enclosed in the request as a new subordinate of the resource identified by the Request-URI in the Request-Line. | Semantics: POST is non-idempotent — exclude from automatic retry unless the caller explicitly opts in. |
| `9-30` | informative | When a POST does not result in a URI-identifiable resource, 200 or 204 is the appropriate response status. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | In this case, either 200 (OK) or 204 (No Content) is the appropriate response status, depending on whether or not the response includes an entity that describes the result. | Origin-side; client accepts either — 204 body-less framing is handled by generic rules. |
| `9-31` | SHOULD | If a POST created a resource, the response should be 201 with a describing entity and a Location header. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the response SHOULD be 201 (Created) and contain an entity which describes the status of the request and refers to the new resource, and a Location header | Origin-side; client may surface Location to callers, no obligation. |
| `9-32` | informative | POST responses are not cacheable unless they include appropriate Cache-Control or Expires headers. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to this method are not cacheable, unless the response includes appropriate Cache-Control or Expires header fields. | Cache-only; no obligation. |
| `9-33` | informative | A 303 (See Other) response can be used to direct the user agent to retrieve a cacheable resource after POST. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the 303 (See Other) response can be used to direct the user agent to retrieve a cacheable resource. | If redirect-following is implemented, 303 after POST must be followed with GET; otherwise surface 303 to the caller unchanged. |
| `9-34` | MUST | POST requests must obey the message transmission requirements of section 8.2. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | POST requests MUST obey the message transmission requirements set out in section 8.2. | Apply section 8.2 rules (Expect: 100-continue handling, no automatic retransmit of non-idempotent sends, connection-error handling) when transmitting POST bodies. |
| `9-35` | informative | PUT requests that the enclosed entity be stored under the supplied Request-URI. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The PUT method requests that the enclosed entity be stored under the supplied Request-URI. | Semantics only; PUT is idempotent (see 9-4) so retriable. |
| `9-36` | SHOULD | If the Request-URI names an existing resource, the enclosed entity should be treated as a modified version of it. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If the Request-URI refers to an already existing resource, the enclosed entity SHOULD be considered as a modified version of the one residing on the origin server. | Origin-side; no client obligation. |
| `9-37` | MUST | If PUT creates a new resource, the origin server must respond 201 (Created). <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If a new resource is created, the origin server MUST inform the user agent via the 201 (Created) response. | Origin-side; client may treat 201 as creation signal, no obligation. |
| `9-38` | SHOULD | If PUT modified an existing resource, the origin should send 200 or 204 to indicate success. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If an existing resource is modified, either the 200 (OK) or 204 (No Content) response codes SHOULD be sent to indicate successful completion of the request. | Origin-side; client accepts either as success. |
| `9-39` | SHOULD | If the resource could not be created or modified, an appropriate error response reflecting the problem should be given. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If the resource could not be created or modified with the Request-URI, an appropriate error response SHOULD be given that reflects the nature of the problem. | Origin-side; no client obligation. |
| `9-40` | MUST NOT | The recipient of a PUT entity must not ignore Content-* headers it does not understand or implement. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | The recipient of the entity MUST NOT ignore any Content-* (e.g. Content-Range) headers that it does not understand or implement | Server-side; awareness only — exotic Content-* headers on a PUT may legitimately draw a 501. |
| `9-41` | MUST | A recipient that cannot honor a Content-* header on a PUT must return 501 (Not Implemented). <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | and MUST return a 501 (Not Implemented) response in such cases. | Server-side; client should expect 501 when sending unrecognized Content-* headers with PUT. |
| `9-42` | SHOULD | Cache entries matching the Request-URI of a PUT passing through a cache should be treated as stale. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | If the request passes through a cache and the Request-URI identifies one or more currently cached entities, those entries SHOULD be treated as stale. | Cache-only; no obligation. |
| `9-43` | informative | Responses to PUT are not cacheable. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to this method are not cacheable. | Cache-only; if a cache is ever added, never store PUT responses. |
| `9-44` | MUST NOT | The server must not apply a PUT request to a resource other than the one identified by the Request-URI. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | the server MUST NOT attempt to apply the request to some other resource. | Server-side; no client obligation. |
| `9-45` | MUST | If the server wants a PUT applied to a different URI, it must send a 301 (Moved Permanently) response. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | If the server desires that the request be applied to a different URI, it MUST send a 301 (Moved Permanently) response; | Server-side; sets up the client-side MAY in 9-46. |
| `9-46` | MAY | On a 301 to a PUT, the user agent may decide for itself whether to redirect the request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent MAY then make its own decision regarding whether or not to redirect the request. | Never auto-redirect a PUT on 301; surface it to the caller (or an explicit policy hook) for the decision. |
| `9-47` | MAY | A single resource may be identified by many different URIs. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | A single resource MAY be identified by many different URIs. | Definitional; no client code obligation. |
| `9-48` | MUST | PUT requests must obey the message transmission requirements of section 8.2. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | PUT requests MUST obey the message transmission requirements set out in section 8.2. | Apply section 8.2 rules (Expect: 100-continue, retransmission constraints) when transmitting PUT bodies, same path as 9-34. |
| `9-49` | SHOULD | Entity-headers in a PUT request should be applied to the resource created or modified, unless otherwise specified. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the entity-headers in the PUT request SHOULD be applied to the resource created or modified by the PUT. | Origin-side; client just transmits caller-provided entity-headers unmodified. |
| `9-50` | informative | DELETE requests that the origin server delete the resource identified by the Request-URI. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The DELETE method requests that the origin server delete the resource identified by the Request-URI. | Semantics only; DELETE is idempotent (see 9-4) so retriable. |
| `9-51` | MAY | The DELETE action may be overridden by human intervention or other means on the origin server. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | This method MAY be overridden by human intervention (or other means) on the origin server. | Origin-side; underpins 9-52 for the client. |
| `9-52` | informative | The client cannot be guaranteed the DELETE was carried out even if the returned status code indicates success. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client cannot be guaranteed that the operation has been carried out, even if the status code returned from the origin server indicates that the action has been completed successfully. | Do not treat a 2xx DELETE response as proof of deletion in any client-side bookkeeping or API guarantees. |
| `9-53` | SHOULD NOT | The server should not indicate DELETE success unless, at response time, it intends to delete or make the resource inaccessible. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | the server SHOULD NOT indicate success unless, at the time the response is given, it intends to delete the resource or move it to an inaccessible location. | Server-side; no client obligation. |
| `9-54` | SHOULD | A successful DELETE response should be 200 (with status entity), 202 (not yet enacted), or 204 (enacted, no entity). <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | A successful response SHOULD be 200 (OK) if the response includes an entity describing the status, 202 (Accepted) if the action has not yet been enacted, or 204 (No Content) | Origin-side; client should treat 200/202/204 all as DELETE success in any status-classification helper. |
| `9-55` | SHOULD | Cache entries matching the Request-URI of a DELETE passing through a cache should be treated as stale. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | If the request passes through a cache and the Request-URI identifies one or more currently cached entities, those entries SHOULD be treated as stale. | Cache-only; no obligation. |
| `9-56` | informative | Responses to DELETE are not cacheable. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to this method are not cacheable. | Cache-only; if a cache is ever added, never store DELETE responses. |
| `9-57` | SHOULD | The final recipient of a TRACE should reflect the received request message back as the entity-body of a 200 response. <br>_(origin,proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | The final recipient of the request SHOULD reflect the message received back to the client as the entity-body of a 200 (OK) response. | Recipient-side; client just delivers the reflected body to the caller. |
| `9-58` | informative | The final recipient of a TRACE is the origin server or the first proxy/gateway receiving Max-Forwards of zero. <br>_(origin,proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | The final recipient is either the origin server or the first proxy or gateway to receive a Max-Forwards value of zero (0) in the request | Chain-topology definition; no client code obligation. |
| `9-59` | MUST NOT | A TRACE request must not include an entity. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A TRACE request MUST NOT include an entity. | Request builder must reject (or refuse to send) any TRACE request carrying a body, Content-Length, or Transfer-Encoding. |
| `9-60` | informative | Max-Forwards on TRACE lets the client limit the length of the request chain (e.g. to test proxy loops). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Use of the Max-Forwards header field allows the client to limit the length of the request chain, | Allow callers to set Max-Forwards on TRACE requests and pass it through unmodified. |
| `9-61` | SHOULD | A valid TRACE should be answered with the entire request message in the entity-body with Content-Type message/http. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | If the request is valid, the response SHOULD contain the entire request message in the entity-body, with a Content-Type of "message/http". | Server-side; client may hand the raw message/http body to the caller, no parsing obligation. |
| `9-62` | MUST NOT | Responses to TRACE must not be cached. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to this method MUST NOT be cached. | Cache-only; if a cache is ever added, TRACE responses are categorically uncacheable (MUST NOT, stronger than OPTIONS/PUT/DELETE). |
| `9-63` | informative | The method name CONNECT is reserved for use with a proxy that can dynamically switch to being a tunnel. <br>_(client,proxy,tunnel [CLIENT])_ | This specification reserves the method name CONNECT for use with a proxy that can dynamically switch to being a tunnel (e.g. SSL tunneling [44]). | Emit CONNECT only for establishing proxy tunnels; do not treat it as a general-purpose request method with normal response-body framing. |

### 10.1-10.2 - Status Codes: Informational 1xx, Successful 2xx

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `10.1-10.2-1` | informative | A 1xx response is provisional, consists only of the Status-Line and optional headers, and is terminated by an empty line (it has no message body). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a provisional response, consisting only of the Status-Line and optional headers, and is terminated by an empty line | http1 response reader must frame every 1xx as headers-only — stop at the empty line and never attempt to read a body for a 1xx status. |
| `10.1-10.2-2` | informative | No header fields are required in a 1xx response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | There are no required headers for this class of status code. | Accept a 1xx response whose header block is empty; do not error on any missing header. |
| `10.1-10.2-3` | MUST NOT | Servers must not send a 1xx response to an HTTP/1.0 client except under experimental conditions. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | servers MUST NOT send a 1xx response to an HTTP/1.0 client except under experimental conditions. | No client obligation; if the client ever emits HTTP/1.0 requests it should still tolerate a stray 1xx per 10.1-10.2-4. |
| `10.1-10.2-4` | MUST | A client must be prepared to accept one or more 1xx responses before the regular (final) response, even when it did not expect a 100 (Continue). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST be prepared to accept one or more 1xx status responses prior to a regular response, even if the client does not expect a 100 (Continue) status message. | Response read loop must consume an arbitrary number of consecutive 1xx header blocks before the final status — a single-shot 100 check is insufficient; verify http1 reader loops. |
| `10.1-10.2-5` | MAY | A user agent may ignore unexpected 1xx responses. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Unexpected 1xx status responses MAY be ignored by a user agent. | Silently discard unsolicited 1xx responses (optionally surface via hook); never fail the request because of one. |
| `10.1-10.2-6` | MUST | Proxies must forward 1xx responses unless the proxy-client connection is closed or the proxy itself requested the 1xx generation. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Proxies MUST forward 1xx responses, unless the connection between the proxy and its client has been closed, or unless the proxy itself requested the generation of the 1xx response. | No action — poseidon is not a proxy. |
| `10.1-10.2-7` | SHOULD | On receiving 100 (Continue), the client should continue with its request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client SHOULD continue with its request. | When Expect: 100-continue was sent, receipt of 100 must trigger transmission of the deferred request body. |
| `10.1-10.2-8` | SHOULD | The client should continue by sending the remainder of the request, or ignore the 100 response if the request was already completed. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client SHOULD continue by sending the remainder of the request or, if the request has already been completed, ignore this response. | If the body was already fully sent when 100 arrives, discard the 100 without error; otherwise send the remaining body bytes. |
| `10.1-10.2-9` | MUST | The server must send a final response after the request has been completed, even when a 100 (Continue) was sent. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server MUST send a final response after the request has been completed. | Client may rely on this: after any 100, keep reading — a final non-1xx response always follows; never complete the call on a 1xx. |
| `10.1-10.2-10` | informative | After a 101 response, the server switches to the protocol(s) named in the response's Upgrade header immediately after the empty line terminating the 101 response. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | The server will switch protocols to those defined by the response's Upgrade header field immediately after the empty line which terminates the 101 response. | On 101, stop HTTP/1.1 parsing exactly at the terminating empty line and hand the raw connection to the new protocol — every byte after it belongs to the upgraded protocol. |
| `10.1-10.2-11` | SHOULD | The protocol should be switched only when it is advantageous to do so. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The protocol SHOULD be switched only when it is advantageous to do so. | Advisory policy only — no parser change; callers decide when requesting Upgrade is beneficial. |
| `10.1-10.2-12` | informative | The 2xx class indicates the client's request was successfully received, understood, and accepted. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This class of status code indicates that the client's request was successfully received, understood, and accepted. | Classify every 2xx status (including unrecognized ones, per §6.1.1 class fallback) as success. |
| `10.1-10.2-13` | informative | For 200 OK, the information returned depends on the request method (GET: entity of the resource; POST: entity describing the result; TRACE: the request as received). <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The information returned with the response is dependent on the method used in the request | No client code change; interpretation of the 200 entity is the caller's concern (HEAD framing handled separately in 10.1-10.2-14). |
| `10.1-10.2-14` | informative | A 200 response to HEAD carries the entity-header fields of the resource but no message body. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | the entity-header fields corresponding to the requested resource are sent in the response without any message-body | Frame HEAD responses as bodyless regardless of Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding values; verify the http1 reader special-cases HEAD. |
| `10.1-10.2-15` | informative | For 201 Created, the most specific URI for the newly created resource is given by a Location header field. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | with the most specific URI for the resource given by a Location header field | Surface the Location header on 201 responses so callers can reference the created resource; no parsing change. |
| `10.1-10.2-16` | SHOULD | A 201 response should include an entity listing resource characteristics and location(s) for the user or user agent to choose from. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The response SHOULD include an entity containing a list of resource characteristics and location(s) from which the user or user agent can choose the one most appropriate. | No client obligation; tolerate both empty and non-empty 201 bodies. |
| `10.1-10.2-17` | MUST | The origin server must create the resource before returning 201. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | The origin server MUST create the resource before returning the 201 status code. | No client action; callers may rely on the resource existing once 201 is received. |
| `10.1-10.2-18` | SHOULD | If the action cannot be carried out immediately, the server should respond with 202 (Accepted) instead of 201. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | If the action cannot be carried out immediately, the server SHOULD respond with 202 (Accepted) response instead. | No client action. |
| `10.1-10.2-19` | MAY | A 201 response may contain an ETag header indicating the current entity tag of the variant just created. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A 201 response MAY contain an ETag response header field indicating the current value of the entity tag for the requested variant just created | Ensure the ETag header is surfaced unfiltered on 201 responses (generic header passthrough suffices). |
| `10.1-10.2-20` | informative | 202 Accepted means the request was accepted for processing but processing has not completed and may never be acted upon. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The request has been accepted for processing, but the processing has not been completed. | Treat 202 as success without a completed result; no special framing. |
| `10.1-10.2-21` | SHOULD | A 202 entity should include an indication of the request's current status plus a pointer to a status monitor or a completion estimate. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The entity returned with this response SHOULD include an indication of the request's current status and either a pointer to a status monitor or some estimate | No client action. |
| `10.1-10.2-22` | informative | 203 means the returned entity-header metainformation is not the definitive set from the origin server but gathered from a local or third-party copy. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The returned metainformation in the entity-header is not the definitive set as available from the origin server, but is gathered from a local or a third-party copy. | Semantics only; no code change — headers on 203 may be non-authoritative. |
| `10.1-10.2-23` | MAY | The metainformation set presented in a 203 may be a subset or superset of the original version. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The set presented MAY be a subset or superset of the original version. | No client action; do not assume 203 headers are complete or exact. |
| `10.1-10.2-24` | informative | Use of 203 is not required and is only appropriate when the response would otherwise be 200 (OK). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Use of this response code is not required and is only appropriate when the response would otherwise be 200 (OK). | Handle 203 identically to 200 on the client side: same body framing, same success classification. |
| `10.1-10.2-25` | MAY | A 204 response may include new or updated metainformation in the form of entity-headers. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The response MAY include new or updated metainformation in the form of entity-headers | Parse headers on 204 normally; presence of entity-headers must not cause a body read (see 10.1-10.2-29). |
| `10.1-10.2-26` | SHOULD | Entity-headers present on a 204, if any, should be associated with the requested variant. <br>_(server,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | which if present SHOULD be associated with the requested variant | Interpret 204 entity-headers as describing the requested variant; no framing impact — surfacing headers unmodified satisfies this. |
| `10.1-10.2-27` | SHOULD NOT | On 204, a user agent should not change its document view from the one that caused the request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | If the client is a user agent, it SHOULD NOT change its document view from that which caused the request to be sent. | N/A for a protocol library — no document view exists; nothing to implement. |
| `10.1-10.2-28` | SHOULD | New or updated metainformation from a 204 should be applied to the document currently in the user agent's active view. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | any new or updated metainformation SHOULD be applied to the document currently in the user agent's active view | N/A for a protocol library — no document view; surfacing 204 headers to the caller suffices. |
| `10.1-10.2-29` | MUST NOT | A 204 response must not include a message body and is always terminated by the first empty line after the header fields. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | The 204 response MUST NOT include a message-body, and thus is always terminated by the first empty line after the header fields. | Reader must treat 204 as unconditionally bodyless: terminate the message at end of headers and never read a body even if Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding is present; verify http1 reader. |
| `10.1-10.2-30` | SHOULD | On 205 Reset Content, the user agent should reset the document view that caused the request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent SHOULD reset the document view which caused the request to be sent | N/A for a protocol library — no document view; nothing to implement. |
| `10.1-10.2-31` | MUST NOT | A 205 response must not include an entity. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The response MUST NOT include an entity. | Expect a zero-length body on 205; note RFC 2616 §4.4 does not exempt 205 from normal framing, so frame via Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding as usual but the body must be empty. |
| `10.1-10.2-32` | MUST | A 206 response is only valid for a request that included a Range header field indicating the desired range. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The request MUST have included a Range header field (section 14.35) indicating the desired range | Client must support emitting Range for partial GETs; a 206 to a request sent without Range is a peer violation — surface it, do not crash. |
| `10.1-10.2-33` | MAY | The request preceding a 206 may have included an If-Range header field to make it conditional. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | MAY have included an If-Range header field (section 14.27) to make the request conditional | Support sending If-Range alongside Range for conditional partial GETs (generic header emission suffices). |
| `10.1-10.2-34` | MUST | A 206 response must include either a Content-Range header for the range sent, or a multipart/byteranges Content-Type with Content-Range fields for each part. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Either a Content-Range header field (section 14.16) indicating the range included with this response, or a multipart/byteranges Content-Type including Content-Range fields for each part. | On 206, expose Content-Range to callers and recognize multipart/byteranges Content-Type (per-part Content-Range lives inside the multipart body). |
| `10.1-10.2-35` | MUST | If a Content-Length header is present in a 206 response, its value must match the actual number of octets transmitted in the message body. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | If a Content-Length header field is present in the response, its value MUST match the actual number of OCTETs transmitted in the message-body. | Client frames by Content-Length; treat a short read before Content-Length octets as a truncated/invalid response, not EOF success. |
| `10.1-10.2-36` | MUST | A 206 response must include a Date header field (it is in the mandatory header list). <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The response MUST include the following header fields: | No client obligation; a conformance validator may check Date presence on 206. |
| `10.1-10.2-37` | MUST | A 206 response must include ETag and/or Content-Location if that header would have been sent in a 200 response to the same request. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | ETag and/or Content-Location, if the header would have been sent in a 200 response to the same request | No client action; server-side obligation. |
| `10.1-10.2-38` | MUST | A 206 response must include Expires, Cache-Control, and/or Vary if the field-value might differ from that sent in any previous response for the same variant. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Expires, Cache-Control, and/or Vary, if the field-value might differ from that sent in any previous response for the same variant | No client action; relevant only if a caching layer is added. |
| `10.1-10.2-39` | SHOULD NOT | If the 206 resulted from an If-Range request using a strong cache validator, the response should not include other entity-headers. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | If the 206 response is the result of an If-Range request that used a strong cache validator (see section 13.3.3), the response SHOULD NOT include other entity-headers. | Client consequence: do not require a full entity-header set on 206 responses to If-Range requests. |
| `10.1-10.2-40` | MUST NOT | If the 206 resulted from an If-Range request using a weak validator, the response must not include other entity-headers. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | If the response is the result of an If-Range request that used a weak validator, the response MUST NOT include other entity-headers | No client action; prevents inconsistency between cached bodies and updated headers. |
| `10.1-10.2-41` | MUST | Otherwise (no If-Range), a 206 response must include all entity-headers that a 200 response to the same request would have carried. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Otherwise, the response MUST include all of the entity-headers that would have been returned with a 200 (OK) response to the same request. | No client action. |
| `10.1-10.2-42` | MUST NOT | A cache must not combine a 206 response with other previously cached content if the ETag or Last-Modified headers do not match exactly. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A cache MUST NOT combine a 206 response with other previously cached content if the ETag or Last-Modified headers do not match exactly | N/A — poseidon has no response cache; binding only if range-assembly caching is ever added. |
| `10.1-10.2-43` | MUST NOT | A cache that does not support the Range and Content-Range headers must not cache 206 responses. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A cache that does not support the Range and Content-Range headers MUST NOT cache 206 (Partial) responses. | N/A — no cache layer in poseidon. |

### 10.3 - Redirection 3xx

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `10.3-1` | informative | A 3xx status code signals that the user agent must take further action to fulfill the request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | This class of status code indicates that further action needs to be taken by the user agent in order to fulfill the request. | Treat any 3xx response as a non-final redirect signal requiring follow-up action, not a terminal success. |
| `10.3-2` | MAY | The user agent may carry out the redirect without user interaction only if the second request's method is GET or HEAD. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The action required MAY be carried out by the user agent without interaction with the user if and only if the method used in the second request is GET or HEAD. | Auto-follow redirects without user/caller interaction only when the redirected (second) request method is GET or HEAD. |
| `10.3-3` | SHOULD | A client should detect infinite redirection loops. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client SHOULD detect infinite redirection loops, since such loops generate network traffic for each redirection. | Implement a redirect-loop detector (visited-URI set or hop counter) and abort with a distinct error on loop detection. |
| `10.3-4` | informative | Earlier specs recommended a maximum of five redirections, and some clients implement that fixed limit. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | previous versions of this specification recommended a maximum of five redirections. | A fixed cap on redirect-chain length (e.g. 5-10 hops) is acceptable and historically expected; make it configurable. |
| `10.3-5` | SHOULD | Unless the request was HEAD, a 300 response should include an entity listing resource characteristics and locations to choose from. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Unless it was a HEAD request, the response SHOULD include an entity containing a list of resource characteristics and location(s) | No client obligation; just be prepared for a 300 response to carry an entity body and drain it correctly. |
| `10.3-6` | informative | The format of the 300 choice-list entity is defined by the response's Content-Type media type, not by HTTP. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The entity format is specified by the media type given in the Content-Type header field. | If exposing 300 bodies to callers, interpret them per the response Content-Type; no fixed wire format is mandated. |
| `10.3-7` | MAY | The user agent may automatically select the most appropriate choice from a 300 response, but no standard algorithm is defined. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | selection of the most appropriate choice MAY be performed automatically. However, this specification does not define any standard for such automatic selection. | Automatic selection among 300 alternatives is optional; not implementing it (surfacing 300 to the caller) is conforming. |
| `10.3-8` | SHOULD | A server with a preferred representation should put its URI in the Location field of the 300 response. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | If the server has a preferred choice of representation, it SHOULD include the specific URI for that representation in the Location field | No client obligation; Location on a 300 is an optional server hint — do not require its presence. |
| `10.3-9` | MAY | User agents may use the Location field value of a 300 response for automatic redirection. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | user agents MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection. | Optionally auto-follow Location on a 300 when present, subject to the GET/HEAD auto-follow rule (10.3-2). |
| `10.3-10` | informative | A 300 response is cacheable by default unless headers indicate otherwise. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | This response is cacheable unless indicated otherwise. | N/A without a response cache; if caching is added, 300 is cacheable by default absent contrary headers. |
| `10.3-11` | SHOULD | After a 301, future references to the resource should use one of the returned URIs (permanent move). <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The requested resource has been assigned a new permanent URI and any future references to this resource SHOULD use one of the returned URIs. | Treat 301 as permanent: subsequent requests for that resource should target the returned URI (or surface permanence to the caller). |
| `10.3-12` | informative | A 301 response is cacheable by default unless headers indicate otherwise. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | This response is cacheable unless indicated otherwise. | N/A without a cache; if caching, 301 is cacheable by default absent contrary headers. |
| `10.3-13` | SHOULD | The new permanent URI of a 301 should be given in the Location header field. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The new permanent URI SHOULD be given by the Location field in the response. | Read the 301 redirect target from the Location header; tolerate its absence (SHOULD, not MUST) without crashing. |
| `10.3-14` | SHOULD | Unless the request was HEAD, the 301 response entity should contain a short hypertext note linking to the new URI(s). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Unless the request method was HEAD, the entity of the response SHOULD contain a short hypertext note with a hyperlink to the new URI(s). | Expect a 301 to carry an entity body; drain/discard it correctly (per its framing) before reusing the connection. |
| `10.3-15` | MUST NOT | On a 301 to a request other than GET or HEAD, the user agent must not automatically redirect without user confirmation. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | If the 301 status code is received in response to a request other than GET or HEAD, the user agent MUST NOT automatically redirect the request unless it can be confirmed by the user | Never auto-follow 301 for non-GET/HEAD methods unless the caller explicitly opted in (confirmation policy); default must be no-follow. |
| `10.3-16` | informative | Some HTTP/1.0 user agents erroneously change a POST into a GET when auto-redirecting after a 301; this is flagged as wrong. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | When automatically redirecting a POST request after receiving a 301 status code, some existing HTTP/1.0 user agents will erroneously change it into a GET request. | Per RFC 2616 semantics, do not rewrite POST to GET on 301; if compatibility rewriting is offered, make it explicit opt-in. |
| `10.3-17` | SHOULD | Because a 302 redirection is temporary, the client should continue to use the original Request-URI for future requests. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Since the redirection might be altered on occasion, the client SHOULD continue to use the Request-URI for future requests. | Do not permanently rewrite the target URI on 302; future requests keep using the original Request-URI. |
| `10.3-18` | informative | A 302 response is only cacheable if a Cache-Control or Expires header field indicates so. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | This response is only cacheable if indicated by a Cache-Control or Expires header field. | N/A without a cache; if caching, store 302 only with explicit Cache-Control/Expires permission. |
| `10.3-19` | SHOULD | The temporary URI of a 302 should be given in the Location header field. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The temporary URI SHOULD be given by the Location field in the response. | Read the 302 redirect target from Location; tolerate its absence without crashing. |
| `10.3-20` | SHOULD | Unless the request was HEAD, the 302 response entity should contain a short hypertext note linking to the new URI(s). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Unless the request method was HEAD, the entity of the response SHOULD contain a short hypertext note with a hyperlink to the new URI(s). | Expect a 302 to carry an entity body; drain it correctly before connection reuse. |
| `10.3-21` | MUST NOT | On a 302 to a request other than GET or HEAD, the user agent must not automatically redirect without user confirmation. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | If the 302 status code is received in response to a request other than GET or HEAD, the user agent MUST NOT automatically redirect the request unless it can be confirmed by the user | Never auto-follow 302 for non-GET/HEAD methods without explicit opt-in confirmation from the caller. |
| `10.3-22` | informative | De-facto, most user agents treat 302 like 303 (GET on Location regardless of original method), though RFC 1945/2068 forbade changing the method; 303 and 307 exist to disambiguate. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | most existing user agent implementations treat 302 as if it were a 303 response, performing a GET on the Location field-value regardless of the original request method. | Pick and document a 302 policy: strict RFC 2616 (preserve method) vs de-facto 303-like (rewrite to GET); make it an explicit client option. |
| `10.3-23` | SHOULD | The resource identified by a 303 response should be retrieved with a GET on the Location URI, regardless of the original method. <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The response to the request can be found under a different URI and SHOULD be retrieved using a GET method on that resource. | When following a 303, issue GET on the Location URI (drop the request body) irrespective of the original method. |
| `10.3-24` | informative | The URI in a 303 is not a substitute reference for the originally requested resource. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The new URI is not a substitute reference for the originally requested resource. | Do not rewrite future requests to the 303 Location URI; it only locates the response to this one request. |
| `10.3-25` | MUST NOT | A 303 response must not be cached (the redirected second response might be cacheable). <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | The 303 response MUST NOT be cached, but the response to the second (redirected) request might be cacheable. | N/A without a cache; if caching is added, never store 303 responses. |
| `10.3-26` | SHOULD | The different URI of a 303 should be given in the Location header field. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The different URI SHOULD be given by the Location field in the response. | Read the 303 target from Location; tolerate its absence without crashing. |
| `10.3-27` | SHOULD | Unless the request was HEAD, the 303 response entity should contain a short hypertext note linking to the new URI(s). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Unless the request method was HEAD, the entity of the response SHOULD contain a short hypertext note with a hyperlink to the new URI(s). | Expect a 303 to carry an entity body; drain it correctly before connection reuse. |
| `10.3-28` | informative | Many pre-HTTP/1.1 user agents do not understand 303; servers may use 302 instead for interoperability. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Many pre-HTTP/1.1 user agents do not understand the 303 status. When interoperability with such clients is a concern, the 302 status code may be used instead | No client action; historical interop note for servers choosing between 302 and 303. |
| `10.3-29` | SHOULD | A server should answer a conditional GET with 304 when access is allowed but the document is unmodified. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | If the client has performed a conditional GET request and access is allowed, but the document has not been modified, the server SHOULD respond with this status code. | Be prepared to receive 304 on any request carrying conditional headers (If-Modified-Since / If-None-Match) and surface it to the caller. |
| `10.3-30` | MUST NOT | A 304 response must not contain a message-body and is always terminated by the first empty line after the headers. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | The 304 response MUST NOT contain a message-body, and thus is always terminated by the first empty line after the header fields. | CRITICAL framing: parse 304 as bodiless — never read a body after 304 headers, even if Content-Length or Transfer-Encoding headers are present. |
| `10.3-31` | MUST | A 304 response must include a Date header unless its omission is permitted by section 14.18.1 (clockless servers). <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The response MUST include the following header fields: - Date, unless its omission is required by section 14.18.1 | No client obligation; do not reject a 304 based on Date presence or absence. |
| `10.3-32` | informative | Proxies and clients add their own Date to any response received without one (per RFC 2068 §14.19) so caches operate correctly with clockless origins. <br>_(proxy,client,cache [not-client])_ | proxies and clients add their own Date to any response received without one (as already specified by [RFC 2068], section 14.19) | N/A unless responses feed a cache; a caching recipient stamps its own Date on responses lacking one. |
| `10.3-33` | MUST | A 304 must include ETag and/or Content-Location if that header would have been sent in a 200 response to the same request. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | ETag and/or Content-Location, if the header would have been sent in a 200 response to the same request | No client obligation; if consuming validators, take ETag/Content-Location from the 304 when present. |
| `10.3-34` | MUST | A 304 must include Expires, Cache-Control, and/or Vary if their field-values might differ from those in any previous response for the same variant. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Expires, Cache-Control, and/or Vary, if the field-value might differ from that sent in any previous response for the same variant | No client obligation; these headers on 304 matter only to caching recipients. |
| `10.3-35` | SHOULD NOT | If the conditional GET used a strong cache validator, the 304 response should not include other entity-headers. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | If the conditional GET used a strong cache validator (see section 13.3.3), the response SHOULD NOT include other entity-headers. | No client action; tolerate 304 responses both with and without extra entity-headers. |
| `10.3-36` | MUST NOT | If the conditional GET used a weak validator, the 304 response must not include other entity-headers. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Otherwise (i.e., the conditional GET used a weak validator), the response MUST NOT include other entity-headers | No client action; server-side rule preventing inconsistency between cached bodies and updated headers. |
| `10.3-37` | MUST | If a 304 refers to an entity not currently cached, the cache must disregard the response and repeat the request unconditionally. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | If a 304 response indicates an entity not currently cached, then the cache MUST disregard the response and repeat the request without the conditional. | N/A without a cache; a caching client receiving 304 for an entity it no longer holds must retry without conditional headers. |
| `10.3-38` | MUST | A cache using a 304 to update an entry must update that entry with any new field values from the response. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | If a cache uses a received 304 response to update a cache entry, the cache MUST update the entry to reflect any new field values given in the response. | N/A without a cache; if caching, merge 304 header updates into the stored entry. |
| `10.3-39` | MUST | On 305, the requested resource must be accessed through the proxy whose URI is given in the Location field. <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The requested resource MUST be accessed through the proxy given by the Location field. The Location field gives the URI of the proxy. | If honoring 305, resend the same request via the proxy URI from Location; given the security note (10.3-42), refusing to auto-follow 305 is the safer policy. |
| `10.3-40` | informative | A 305 applies to that single request only; the recipient repeats just this one request via the proxy. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The recipient is expected to repeat this single request via the proxy. | Never persist a 305 proxy beyond the one retried request; do not treat it as connection- or origin-wide proxy configuration. |
| `10.3-41` | MUST | 305 responses must only be generated by origin servers. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | 305 responses MUST only be generated by origin servers. | Honor a 305 only if it plausibly came from the origin server; ignore 305s injected by intermediaries (security). |
| `10.3-42` | informative | Failing to observe the 305 limitations (single request, origin-only) has significant security consequences. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Not observing these limitations has significant security consequences. | Treat 305 as security-sensitive; safest client default is to not auto-follow 305 and surface it to the caller. |
| `10.3-43` | informative | Status code 306 is no longer used and is reserved. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 306 status code was used in a previous version of the specification, is no longer used, and the code is reserved. | Implement no special 306 handling; treat it as an unrecognized 3xx (per §6.1.1, equivalent to 300 of its class). |
| `10.3-44` | SHOULD | Because a 307 redirection may be altered, the client should continue to use the original Request-URI for future requests. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Since the redirection MAY be altered on occasion, the client SHOULD continue to use the Request-URI for future requests. | Do not permanently rewrite the target URI on 307; future requests keep using the original Request-URI. |
| `10.3-45` | informative | A 307 response is only cacheable if a Cache-Control or Expires header field indicates so. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | This response is only cacheable if indicated by a Cache-Control or Expires header field. | N/A without a cache; if caching, store 307 only with explicit Cache-Control/Expires permission. |
| `10.3-46` | SHOULD | The temporary URI of a 307 should be given in the Location header field. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The temporary URI SHOULD be given by the Location field in the response. | Read the 307 redirect target from Location; tolerate its absence without crashing. |
| `10.3-47` | SHOULD | Unless the request was HEAD, the 307 response entity should contain a short hypertext note linking to the new URI(s), because pre-HTTP/1.1 agents do not understand 307. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Unless the request method was HEAD, the entity of the response SHOULD contain a short hypertext note with a hyperlink to the new URI(s) | Expect a 307 to carry an entity body; drain it correctly before connection reuse. |
| `10.3-48` | SHOULD | The hypertext note in a 307 should contain the information necessary for a user to repeat the original request on the new URI. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Therefore, the note SHOULD contain the information necessary for a user to repeat the original request on the new URI. | No client action; note content is a server obligation. |
| `10.3-49` | MUST NOT | On a 307 to a request other than GET or HEAD, the user agent must not automatically redirect without user confirmation. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | If the 307 status code is received in response to a request other than GET or HEAD, the user agent MUST NOT automatically redirect the request unless it can be confirmed by the user | Never auto-follow 307 for non-GET/HEAD without explicit opt-in; when followed, preserve the original method and body (unlike 303). |

### 10.4 - Client Error 4xx

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `10.4-1` | informative | The 4xx status class denotes cases where the client appears to have erred. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 4xx class of status code is intended for cases in which the client seems to have erred. | Classify all 4xx codes as client-error class in response handling; no blanket automatic retry. |
| `10.4-2` | SHOULD | Except for HEAD responses, the server should include an entity explaining the error and whether it is temporary or permanent. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Except when responding to a HEAD request, the server SHOULD include an entity containing an explanation of the error situation, and whether it is a temporary or permanent condition. | Client must be prepared to read/drain an entity body on any 4xx response (none follows a HEAD). |
| `10.4-3` | informative | 4xx status codes may be returned for any request method. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | These status codes are applicable to any request method. | Accept any 4xx status regardless of the request method sent; do not treat method/status combinations as protocol errors. |
| `10.4-4` | SHOULD | User agents should display any entity included with a 4xx response to the user. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | User agents SHOULD display any included entity to the user. | Surface the 4xx entity body to the caller instead of discarding it (display itself is N/A for a non-interactive library). |
| `10.4-5` | SHOULD | A server over TCP should ensure the client has acknowledged the response packets before closing the input connection, to avoid a reset erasing unread data. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a server implementation using TCP SHOULD be careful to ensure that the client acknowledges receipt of the packet(s) containing the response, before the server closes the input connection. | Server-only; but be aware a non-conforming server's early close can RST-erase the response — surface as connection error, do not reuse the conn. |
| `10.4-6` | informative | 400 means the server could not understand the request due to malformed syntax. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The request could not be understood by the server due to malformed syntax. | Map 400 to not-retryable-without-modification in the retry classifier. |
| `10.4-7` | SHOULD NOT | On 400, the client should not repeat the request without modifying it. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client SHOULD NOT repeat the request without modifications. | Retry logic must not auto-resubmit an identical request after a 400. |
| `10.4-8` | informative | 401 means the request requires user authentication. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The request requires user authentication. | Treat 401 as an auth-required signal, distinct from 403. |
| `10.4-9` | MUST | A 401 response must include a WWW-Authenticate header with a challenge applicable to the requested resource. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The response MUST include a WWW-Authenticate header field (section 14.47) containing a challenge applicable to the requested resource. | Parse and expose WWW-Authenticate on 401; remain robust (no crash/reject) if a non-conforming server omits it. |
| `10.4-10` | MAY | After a 401, the client may repeat the request with a suitable Authorization header. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client MAY repeat the request with a suitable Authorization header field (section 14.8). | Retrying with Authorization is optional; if implemented, retry at most with new/valid credentials. |
| `10.4-11` | informative | A 401 to a request that already carried Authorization means those credentials were refused. <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | If the request already included Authorization credentials, then the 401 response indicates that authorization has been refused for those credentials. | If the request carried Authorization, treat 401 as credential rejection — never blind-retry the same credentials. |
| `10.4-12` | SHOULD | If a 401 repeats the same challenge and the user agent already attempted authentication, the response entity should be presented to the user. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | then the user SHOULD be presented the entity that was given in the response, since that entity might include relevant diagnostic information. | On an identical repeated challenge after an auth attempt, stop the auth-retry loop and surface the response entity to the caller. |
| `10.4-13` | informative | 402 Payment Required is reserved for future use. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This code is reserved for future use. | Treat 402 as a generic 4xx with no special semantics. |
| `10.4-14` | informative | 403 means the server understood the request but refuses to fulfill it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server understood the request, but is refusing to fulfill it. | Distinguish 403 (understood, refused, auth will not help) from 401 in error classification. |
| `10.4-15` | SHOULD NOT | A 403 request should not be repeated, and authorization will not help. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Authorization will not help and the request SHOULD NOT be repeated. | Retry policy: never auto-repeat a request that got 403, and do not attempt credential retry. |
| `10.4-16` | SHOULD | If the method was not HEAD and the server wants to publicize the refusal reason, it should describe it in the entity. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | it SHOULD describe the reason for the refusal in the entity. | Server-only; client needs no action beyond normal optional-body handling on 403. |
| `10.4-17` | informative | 404 means nothing matched the Request-URI, with no indication whether the condition is temporary or permanent. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server has not found anything matching the Request-URI. No indication is given of whether the condition is temporary or permanent. | Do not classify 404 as permanent in retry/caching logic; permanence is signaled by 410, not 404. |
| `10.4-18` | SHOULD | Servers that know a resource is permanently gone with no forwarding address should use 410 rather than 404. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | The 410 (Gone) status code SHOULD be used if the server knows, through some internally configurable mechanism, that an old resource is permanently unavailable and has no forwarding address. | Server-only; no client obligation. |
| `10.4-19` | MUST | A 405 response must include an Allow header listing valid methods for the resource. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The response MUST include an Allow header containing a list of valid methods for the requested resource. | Parse the Allow header on 405 and expose the permitted method list; stay robust if a non-conforming server omits it. |
| `10.4-20` | informative | 406 means the resource can only generate entities with content characteristics unacceptable per the request's accept headers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The resource identified by the request is only capable of generating response entities which have content characteristics not acceptable according to the accept headers sent in the request. | Map 406 to content-negotiation failure for the Accept* headers the client sent. |
| `10.4-21` | SHOULD | Unless the request was HEAD, a 406 response should include an entity listing available entity characteristics and locations to choose from. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Unless it was a HEAD request, the response SHOULD include an entity containing a list of available entity characteristics and location(s) | Server-only; a client implementing negotiation may optionally parse the alternatives entity. |
| `10.4-22` | MAY | A user agent may automatically select the most appropriate variant from a 406 entity, but no standard selection format is defined. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | selection of the most appropriate choice MAY be performed automatically. However, this specification does not define any standard for such automatic selection. | Automatic variant selection is optional and unstandardized — safe to omit entirely. |
| `10.4-23` | informative | Servers are permitted to return responses that are not acceptable per the request's accept headers; user agents are encouraged to inspect response headers for acceptability. <br>_(server,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 servers are allowed to return responses which are not acceptable according to the accept headers sent in the request. | Never assume the server honored Accept; check the response Content-Type when the caller requires a specific media type. |
| `10.4-24` | SHOULD | If a response could be unacceptable, the user agent should temporarily stop receiving data and ask the user how to proceed. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | a user agent SHOULD temporarily stop receipt of more data and query the user for a decision on further actions. | Interactive-UA rule; for a library, optionally allow the caller to abort body receipt early (DoStream) on unacceptable Content-Type — document as N/A otherwise. |
| `10.4-25` | informative | 407 is like 401 but means the client must first authenticate itself with the proxy. <br>_(client,proxy [CLIENT])_ | This code is similar to 401 (Unauthorized), but indicates that the client must first authenticate itself with the proxy. | Distinguish 407 (proxy auth) from 401 (origin auth); route credentials to the proxy layer, not the origin. |
| `10.4-26` | MUST | A 407 response from a proxy must include a Proxy-Authenticate header with a challenge applicable to the proxy. <br>_(proxy [CLIENT])_ | The proxy MUST return a Proxy-Authenticate header field (section 14.33) containing a challenge applicable to the proxy for the requested resource. | Parse and expose Proxy-Authenticate on 407; remain robust if absent. |
| `10.4-27` | MAY | After a 407, the client may repeat the request with a suitable Proxy-Authorization header. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client MAY repeat the request with a suitable Proxy-Authorization header field (section 14.34). | Retrying with Proxy-Authorization is optional; if implemented, attach it to the proxy hop only. |
| `10.4-28` | informative | 408 means the client did not produce a request within the time the server was prepared to wait. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The client did not produce a request within the time that the server was prepared to wait. | Classify 408 as a server-side idle timeout on the request — a candidate for retry. |
| `10.4-29` | MAY | After a 408, the client may repeat the request unmodified at any later time. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client MAY repeat the request without modifications at any later time. | Retry policy may auto-retry on 408 without modification (subject to the caller's idempotency constraints). |
| `10.4-30` | informative | 409 means the request could not be completed due to a conflict with the current state of the resource. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The request could not be completed due to a conflict with the current state of the resource. | Map 409 to resolvable-conflict: retryable only after the caller modifies the request. |
| `10.4-31` | informative | 409 is only allowed where the user is expected to be able to resolve the conflict and resubmit. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | This code is only allowed in situations where it is expected that the user might be able to resolve the conflict and resubmit the request. | Server usage constraint; no direct client obligation. |
| `10.4-32` | SHOULD | A 409 response body should include enough information for the user to recognize the source of the conflict. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The response body SHOULD include enough information for the user to recognize the source of the conflict. | Server-only; client just surfaces the body. |
| `10.4-33` | informative | 410 means the resource is gone with no forwarding address, and the condition is expected to be permanent. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The requested resource is no longer available at the server and no forwarding address is known. This condition is expected to be considered permanent. | Classify 410 as permanent failure — never auto-retry, unlike 404. |
| `10.4-34` | SHOULD | Clients with link editing capabilities should delete references to the Request-URI after user approval on a 410. <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Clients with link editing capabilities SHOULD delete references to the Request-URI after user approval. | Applies only to link-editing clients (bookmark managers, crawlers); N/A for a plain request library — document the exemption. |
| `10.4-35` | SHOULD | If the server cannot determine whether the gone condition is permanent, it should use 404 instead of 410. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | If the server does not know, or has no facility to determine, whether or not the condition is permanent, the status code 404 (Not Found) SHOULD be used instead. | Server-only; no client obligation. |
| `10.4-36` | informative | A 410 response is cacheable by default unless indicated otherwise. <br>_(cache [CLIENT])_ | This response is cacheable unless indicated otherwise. | If the client has a cache layer, 410 is cacheable by default absent Cache-Control/Expires overrides; no obligation for a cache-less client. |
| `10.4-37` | informative | 411 means the server refuses the request because it lacks a defined Content-Length. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | The server refuses to accept the request without a defined Content-Length. | Map 411 to missing-length framing rejection (e.g. server refused a chunked request body). |
| `10.4-38` | MAY | After a 411, the client may repeat the request if it adds a valid Content-Length for the message body. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client MAY repeat the request if it adds a valid Content-Length header field containing the length of the message-body in the request message. | Optional: on 411, retry only after adding a valid Content-Length (requires buffering a streamed body to learn its length). |
| `10.4-39` | informative | 412 means a precondition in the request headers evaluated to false on the server. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The precondition given in one or more of the request-header fields evaluated to false when it was tested on the server. | Map 412 to conditional-request failure (If-Match / If-Unmodified-Since etc.); not retryable unchanged. |
| `10.4-40` | informative | 413 means the request entity is larger than the server is willing or able to process. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server is refusing to process a request because the request entity is larger than the server is willing or able to process. | Map 413 to request-too-large; never retry with the same-size body. |
| `10.4-41` | MAY | On 413, the server may close the connection to stop the client from continuing to send the request. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server MAY close the connection to prevent the client from continuing the request. | Client must tolerate connection close mid-request-body: still attempt to read the 413 response, surface the write error otherwise, and never return the conn to the pool. |
| `10.4-42` | SHOULD | If the 413 condition is temporary, the server should include Retry-After, after which the client may try again. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | If the condition is temporary, the server SHOULD include a Retry-After header field to indicate that it is temporary and after what time the client MAY try again. | On 413, parse Retry-After if present and honor it for retry scheduling; treat its absence as a permanent condition. |
| `10.4-43` | informative | 414 means the server refuses because the Request-URI is longer than it is willing to interpret. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server is refusing to service the request because the Request-URI is longer than the server is willing to interpret. | Map 414 to URI-too-long; guard redirect-following against URI growth loops (redirected prefix pointing to a suffix of itself). |
| `10.4-44` | informative | 415 means the request entity is in a format not supported by the resource for the requested method. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server is refusing to service the request because the entity of the request is in a format not supported by the requested resource for the requested method. | Map 415 to unsupported request media type; not retryable with the same Content-Type. |
| `10.4-45` | SHOULD | A server should return 416 when the request had a Range header whose range-specifiers do not overlap the resource extent and no If-Range was included. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server SHOULD return a response with this status code if a request included a Range request-header field (section 14.35), and none of the range-specifier values in this field overlap the current extent of the selected resource | A client sending Range without If-Range must expect and handle 416 when its ranges fall outside the resource. |
| `10.4-46` | SHOULD | A 416 for a byte-range request should include a Content-Range header giving the current length of the selected resource. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the response SHOULD include a Content-Range entity-header field specifying the current length of the selected resource | Parse Content-Range (bytes */N form) on 416 to learn the current resource length; remain robust if absent. |
| `10.4-47` | MUST NOT | A 416 response must not use the multipart/byteranges content-type. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | This response MUST NOT use the multipart/byteranges content-type. | Server-only; client need not support multipart/byteranges parsing for 416 bodies. |
| `10.4-48` | informative | 417 means the Expect header's expectation could not be met by the server, or a proxy has unambiguous evidence the next-hop server cannot meet it. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | The expectation given in an Expect request-header field (see section 14.20) could not be met by this server | A client sending Expect: 100-continue must handle 417 — abort the body send and optionally retry the request without the Expect header. |

### 10.5 - Server Error 5xx

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `10.5-1` | informative | Any response status code beginning with digit 5 signals that the server knows it erred or cannot perform the request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Response status codes beginning with the digit "5" indicate cases in which the server is aware that it has erred or is incapable of performing the request. | Classify every 500-599 status as a server-side error class, never as a defect in the client's request. |
| `10.5-2` | SHOULD | Except for HEAD responses, the server should include an entity explaining the error and whether it is temporary or permanent. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Except when responding to a HEAD request, the server SHOULD include an entity containing an explanation of the error situation, and whether it is a temporary or permanent condition. | Client must be prepared to receive and drain an entity body on any 5xx response, and expect no body when the request was HEAD. |
| `10.5-3` | SHOULD | User agents should display any entity included with a 5xx response to the user. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | User agents SHOULD display any included entity to the user. | Surface the 5xx response body to the caller (library equivalent of displaying to the user) rather than silently discarding it. |
| `10.5-4` | informative | 5xx response codes can appear in response to any request method. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | These response codes are applicable to any request method. | Accept and process any 5xx code on responses to every request method; do not restrict handling by method. |
| `10.5-5` | informative | 500 Internal Server Error means the server hit an unexpected condition that prevented fulfilling the request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server encountered an unexpected condition which prevented it from fulfilling the request. | Map 500 to a generic server failure; it also serves as the fallback handling path for 503-without-Retry-After (see 10.5-12). |
| `10.5-6` | informative | 501 Not Implemented means the server lacks the functionality required to fulfill the request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server does not support the functionality required to fulfill the request. | Map 501 to 'server lacks required functionality'; do not automatically retry the identical request against the same server. |
| `10.5-7` | informative | 501 is the appropriate response when the server does not recognize the request method and cannot support it for any resource. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | This is the appropriate response when the server does not recognize the request method and is not capable of supporting it for any resource. | No client obligation; informational only — a 501 may specifically indicate the request method was not recognized. |
| `10.5-8` | informative | 502 Bad Gateway means a gateway/proxy received an invalid response from the upstream server it accessed. <br>_(gateway,proxy [CLIENT])_ | The server, while acting as a gateway or proxy, received an invalid response from the upstream server it accessed in attempting to fulfill the request. | Map 502 to an upstream/gateway failure distinct from origin failure; treat as potentially transient in retry classification. |
| `10.5-9` | informative | 503 Service Unavailable means the server is currently unable to handle the request due to temporary overloading or maintenance. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server is currently unable to handle the request due to a temporary overloading or maintenance of the server. | Map 503 to temporary overload/maintenance; classify it as retry-eligible rather than a permanent failure. |
| `10.5-10` | informative | 503 implies a temporary condition that will be alleviated after some delay. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The implication is that this is a temporary condition which will be alleviated after some delay. | Retry/backoff logic may re-issue the request after a delay on 503; do not cache or persist it as a permanent error. |
| `10.5-11` | MAY | The server may indicate the length of the 503 delay in a Retry-After header. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | If known, the length of the delay MAY be indicated in a Retry-After header. | Parse the Retry-After header on 503 responses and honor the indicated delay before any retry. |
| `10.5-12` | SHOULD | When a 503 carries no Retry-After header, the client should handle the response exactly as it would a 500. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If no Retry-After is given, the client SHOULD handle the response as it would for a 500 response. | Verify the code path for 503-without-Retry-After falls through to the same handling as 500 (no assumed retry delay). |
| `10.5-13` | informative | A server becoming overloaded is not required to use 503; it may simply refuse the connection. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The existence of the 503 status code does not imply that a server must use it when becoming overloaded. Some servers may wish to simply refuse the connection. | No client conformance action; design note — do not assume overload always surfaces as 503, connection refusal is also a legitimate overload signal. |
| `10.5-14` | informative | 504 Gateway Timeout means a gateway/proxy did not receive a timely response from the upstream or auxiliary server it needed. <br>_(gateway,proxy [CLIENT])_ | The server, while acting as a gateway or proxy, did not receive a timely response from the upstream server specified by the URI | Map 504 to an upstream timeout at a gateway/proxy; treat as potentially transient in retry classification. |
| `10.5-15` | informative | Some deployed proxies return 400 or 500 instead of 504 when DNS lookups time out. <br>_(proxy [CLIENT])_ | some deployed proxies are known to return 400 or 500 when DNS lookups time out. | Robustness: do not key upstream-timeout handling exclusively on 504; real proxies may report DNS timeouts as 400 or 500. |
| `10.5-16` | informative | 505 HTTP Version Not Supported means the server does not support, or refuses to support, the HTTP protocol version used in the request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server does not support, or refuses to support, the HTTP protocol version that was used in the request message. | Map 505 to protocol-version rejection; do not blindly retry the request with the same HTTP version. |
| `10.5-17` | informative | A 505 signals the server is unable or unwilling to complete the request using the same major HTTP version as the client (per section 3.1). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The server is indicating that it is unable or unwilling to complete the request using the same major version as the client | On 505, any retry must use a different HTTP major version; treat the current major version as rejected by that server. |
| `10.5-18` | SHOULD | A 505 response should contain an entity describing why the version is unsupported and which other protocols the server supports. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The response SHOULD contain an entity describing why that version is not supported and what other protocols are supported by that server. | No client obligation; optionally expose the 505 entity body to callers so they can learn which protocols the server supports. |

### 11-12 - Access Authentication; Content Negotiation

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `11-12-1` | OPTIONAL | Challenge-response authentication is an optional mechanism: servers may challenge a client request and clients may supply authentication information. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | HTTP provides several OPTIONAL challenge-response authentication mechanisms which can be used by a server to challenge a client request and by a client to provide authentication information. | Auth support is optional; if implemented, client must be able to receive a challenge (401/407) and resend the request with credentials. |
| `11-12-2` | informative | The access-authentication framework and Basic/Digest schemes are not defined here but delegated to RFC 2617 [43]. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The general framework for access authentication, and the specification of "basic" and "digest" authentication, are specified in "HTTP Authentication: Basic and Digest Access Authentication" [43]. | Any Basic/Digest support must conform to RFC 2617, not to RFC 2616 itself; conformance tests for auth syntax belong against RFC 2617. |
| `11-12-3` | informative | RFC 2616 uses the terms "challenge" and "credentials" exactly as defined in RFC 2617. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This specification adopts the definitions of "challenge" and "credentials" from that specification. | Parse WWW-Authenticate/Proxy-Authenticate values (challenge) and build Authorization/Proxy-Authorization values (credentials) per RFC 2617 grammar. |
| `11-12-4` | MAY | Any response that carries an entity-body, including error responses, may be subject to content negotiation. <br>_(server,cache [CLIENT])_ | Any response containing an entity-body MAY be subject to negotiation, including error responses. | Do not assume error responses have a fixed representation; treat any entity-bearing response (including 4xx/5xx) as potentially varying by request headers. |
| `11-12-5` | informative | HTTP defines two orthogonal kinds of content negotiation, server-driven and agent-driven, usable separately or in combination. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | There are two kinds of content negotiation which are possible in HTTP: server-driven and agent-driven negotiation. | Taxonomy only; no client code required. |
| `11-12-6` | informative | Server-driven negotiation is defined as representation selection performed by an algorithm at the server based on request header fields or other request information. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | If the selection of the best representation for a response is made by an algorithm located at the server, it is called server-driven negotiation. | Definition only; selection happens server-side, nothing for the client to implement. |
