| `15-16` | SHOULD | General-purpose user agents providing a high degree of header configurability should warn users about the associated loss of privacy. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | General purpose user agents which provide a high degree of header configurability SHOULD warn users about the loss of privacy which can be involved. | For a library: note in docs (e.g., CLIENT_GUIDE) that elaborate custom accept/quality-value headers act as long-lived user fingerprints. |
| `15-17` | SHOULD | Origin server implementations should restrict documents returned by HTTP requests to only those intended by the server administrators. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | Implementations of HTTP origin servers SHOULD be careful to restrict the documents returned by HTTP requests to be only those that were intended by the server administrators. | N/A — origin-server document-scoping duty. |
| `15-18` | MUST | A server translating HTTP URIs directly into file system calls must take special care not to serve files unintended for HTTP delivery. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | the server MUST take special care not to serve files that were not intended to be delivered to HTTP clients | N/A — server-side file-mapping rule; no client obligation. |
| `15-19` | MUST | A server must disallow ".." path constructs in the Request-URI where they would allow access to resources outside the intended set. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | an HTTP server MUST disallow any such construct in the Request-URI if it would otherwise allow access to a resource outside those intended to be accessible | N/A — path-traversal defense binds the server; the client transmits the URI the caller supplies. |
| `15-20` | MUST | Files intended only for internal server reference (access control files, configuration, script code) must be protected from retrieval via HTTP. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | MUST be protected from inappropriate retrieval, since they might contain sensitive information | N/A — server-side file protection. |
| `15-21` | SHOULD | HTTP clients should rely on their name resolver to confirm IP/DNS-name associations rather than caching results of previous host name lookups themselves. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | HTTP clients SHOULD rely on their name resolver for confirmation of an IP number/DNS name association, rather than caching the result of previous host name lookups | Check the client resolver (client/ DNS discovery): do not pin resolved IPs indefinitely; re-consult the resolver rather than keeping a private永-lived IP cache. |
| `15-22` | SHOULD | Platforms that can cache host name lookups locally when appropriate should be configured to do so. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Many platforms already can cache host name lookups locally when appropriate, and they SHOULD be configured to do so | Prefer delegating DNS caching to the platform resolver; if the client layers its own cache, make it configurable. |
| `15-23` | informative | Host name lookup results are properly cached only when the TTL reported by the name server makes it likely the cached information remains useful. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | only when the TTL (Time To Live) information reported by the name server makes it likely that the cached information will remain useful | Any client-side DNS cache must key entry lifetime to the record TTL, not a fixed arbitrary duration. |
| `15-24` | MUST | HTTP clients that cache host name lookup results for performance must observe the TTL information reported by DNS. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If HTTP clients cache the results of host name lookups in order to achieve a performance improvement, they MUST observe the TTL information reported by DNS. | Hard requirement: verify the poseidon DNS resolver's refresh/eviction honors record TTL (or re-resolves each connect); add a conformance test if a cache exists. |
| `15-25` | MUST | A server hosting multiple mutually-untrusting organizations must check Location and Content-Location values in responses those organizations generate, so they cannot invalidate resources they have no authority over. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | it MUST check the values of Location and Content-Location headers in responses that are generated under control of said organizations | N/A — binds multi-tenant servers; (related client-cache hygiene lives in section 13, not here). |
| `15-26` | informative | Content-Disposition is not part of the HTTP standard but is widely implemented; its serious security considerations (RFC 1806/RFC 2183) apply to implementors. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Disposition is not part of the HTTP standard, but since it is widely implemented, we are documenting its use and risks for implementors | If the client ever interprets Content-Disposition (e.g., suggested filenames), apply RFC 2183 precautions; passing the header through opaquely is fine. |
| `15-27` | informative | Use of password-protected screen savers, idle time-outs, and similar methods is encouraged to mitigate risks of indefinitely retained authentication credentials. <br>_(user-agent [not-client])_ | we encourage the use of password protection in screen savers, idle time-outs, and other methods which mitigate the security problems inherent in this problem | Environmental/user-level mitigation; no obligation on HTTP library code (relevant only if the client persists credentials — see 15-28). |
| `15-28` | informative | User agents that cache authentication credentials are encouraged to provide a readily accessible, user-controlled mechanism for discarding them. <br>_(user-agent,client [CLIENT])_ | user agents which cache credentials are encouraged to provide a readily accessible mechanism for discarding cached credentials under user control | If the client retains auth state (stored Authorization values, tokens) across requests, expose an API to clear it; if no credential cache exists, nothing to do. |
| `15-29` | informative | Proxy operators should protect proxy systems as they would any system containing or transporting sensitive information. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Proxy operators should protect the systems on which proxies run as they would protect any system that contains or transports sensitive information | N/A — proxy operational guidance. |
| `15-30` | informative | Proxy log information should be carefully guarded with appropriate usage guidelines, since it contains highly sensitive personal information. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Log information should be carefully guarded, and appropriate guidelines for use developed and followed | N/A — proxy log-handling guidance. |
| `15-31` | informative | Cache contents persist after requests complete and should be protected as sensitive information. <br>_(proxy,cache [not-client])_ | cache contents should be protected as sensitive information | N/A for a non-caching client; would apply only if a response cache were added. |
| `15-32` | informative | Proxy implementors should consider the privacy and security implications of design, coding decisions, and configuration options — especially defaults. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Proxy implementors should consider the privacy and security implications of their design and coding decisions, and of the configuration options they provide to proxy operators | N/A — proxy implementor guidance. |
| `15-33` | informative | Users of a proxy must understand the proxy is no more trustworthy than its operators; HTTP itself cannot solve this. <br>_(user-agent [not-client])_ | Users of a proxy need to be aware that they are no trustworthier than the people who run the proxy | N/A — user-awareness note; no code behavior to check. |
| `15-34` | informative | Denial-of-service attacks on proxies exist, are hard to defend against, and implementors should beware. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | They exist. They are hard to defend against. Research continues. Beware. | N/A — proxy DoS advisory. |

### 19.3-19.6 - RFC 2616: Tolerant Applications; Differences from RFC 2045; Additional Features; Compatibility with Previous Versions

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `19.3-19.6-1` | RECOMMENDED | Operational applications should tolerate deviations from the spec whenever the deviation can be interpreted unambiguously. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | We therefore recommend that operational applications be tolerant of deviations whenever those deviations can be interpreted unambiguously. | Response parser should accept unambiguous deviations rather than hard-failing; document each leniency. |
| `19.3-19.6-2` | SHOULD | Clients should be tolerant when parsing the Status-Line (and servers when parsing the Request-Line). <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Clients SHOULD be tolerant in parsing the Status-Line and servers tolerant when parsing the Request-Line. | Status-line parser must be lenient about recoverable malformations instead of rejecting the response outright. |
| `19.3-19.6-3` | SHOULD | Parsers of Status-Line/Request-Line should accept any run of SP or HT characters between fields, even though the grammar requires a single SP. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | they SHOULD accept any amount of SP or HT characters between fields, even though only a single SP is required. | Status-line parser: skip 1*(SP\|HT) between HTTP-Version, Status-Code, and Reason-Phrase; emit exactly one SP. |
| `19.3-19.6-4` | informative | The line terminator for message-header fields is the CRLF sequence. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The line terminator for message-header fields is the sequence CRLF. | Emit CRLF after the request line and every header field; CRLF is the canonical parse terminator. |
| `19.3-19.6-5` | RECOMMENDED | When parsing headers, applications should recognize a bare LF as a line terminator and ignore a leading CR. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | we recommend that applications, when parsing such headers, recognize a single LF as a line terminator and ignore the leading CR. | Header parser: treat lone LF as end-of-line and strip an immediately preceding CR. |
| `19.3-19.6-6` | SHOULD | The charset of an entity-body should be labeled as the lowest common denominator of the character codes actually used in it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The character set of an entity-body SHOULD be labeled as the lowest common denominator of the character codes used within that body | If the client labels request-body charset, pick the minimal charset covering the content. |
| `19.3-19.6-7` | informative | Not labeling the entity charset is preferred over labeling it US-ASCII or ISO-8859-1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | not labeling the entity is preferred over labeling the entity with the labels US-ASCII or ISO-8859-1 | Omit the charset parameter rather than emitting charset=US-ASCII or charset=ISO-8859-1. |
| `19.3-19.6-8` | SHOULD | Clients and caches should treat an RFC-850 date apparently more than 50 years in the future as actually in the past. <br>_(client,cache [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 clients and caches SHOULD assume that an RFC-850 date which appears to be more than 50 years in the future is in fact in the past | If parsing RFC-850 two-digit-year dates, resolve years >50y in the future to the previous century. |
| `19.3-19.6-9` | MAY | An implementation may internally represent a parsed Expires date as earlier than its proper value. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An HTTP/1.1 implementation MAY internally represent a parsed Expires date as earlier than the proper value | If Expires is parsed, any rounding/approximation may only shift the value earlier. |
| `19.3-19.6-10` | MUST NOT | An implementation must not internally represent a parsed Expires date as later than its proper value. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MUST NOT internally represent a parsed Expires date as later than the proper value | If Expires is parsed/exposed, never round or coerce it to a later instant than the header states. |
| `19.3-19.6-11` | MUST | All expiration-related calculations must be performed in GMT. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | All expiration-related calculations MUST be done in GMT. | N/A unless client-side caching is added; then compute age/expiration exclusively in GMT. |
| `19.3-19.6-12` | MUST NOT | The local time zone must not influence any age or expiration calculation or comparison. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | The local time zone MUST NOT influence the calculation or comparison of an age or expiration time. | N/A unless caching is added; then all date math must be locale/TZ-independent (use UTC epoch). |
| `19.3-19.6-13` | MUST | A date value incorrectly carrying a non-GMT time zone must be converted to GMT using the most conservative possible conversion. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If an HTTP header incorrectly carries a date value with a time zone other than GMT, it MUST be converted into GMT using the most conservative possible conversion. | Date parser: on a non-GMT zone token, convert to GMT choosing the interpretation least favorable to freshness. |
| `19.3-19.6-14` | SHOULD | Proxies and gateways to strict MIME environments should be aware of HTTP/MIME differences and perform appropriate conversions. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Proxies and gateways to strict MIME environments SHOULD be aware of these differences and provide the appropriate conversions where necessary. | N/A — proxy/gateway obligation only. |
| `19.3-19.6-15` | MAY | HTTP/1.1 messages may include a single MIME-Version general-header field. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 messages MAY include a single MIME-Version general-header field to indicate what version of the MIME protocol was used to construct the message. | Client may emit at most one MIME-Version header and must tolerate receiving one in responses. |
| `19.3-19.6-16` | informative | Emitting MIME-Version asserts the message is in full compliance with the MIME protocol. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Use of the MIME-Version header field indicates that the message is in full compliance with the MIME protocol (as defined in RFC 2045[7]). | Do not emit MIME-Version unless the request is genuinely fully MIME-compliant; in practice, never emit it. |
| `19.3-19.6-17` | informative | Proxies/gateways are responsible for ensuring full MIME compliance when exporting HTTP messages to strict MIME environments. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Proxies/gateways are responsible for ensuring full compliance (where possible) when exporting HTTP messages to strict MIME environments. | N/A — proxy/gateway obligation only. |
| `19.3-19.6-18` | informative | Grammar: MIME-Version = "MIME-Version" ":" 1*DIGIT "." 1*DIGIT. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MIME-Version = "MIME-Version" ":" 1*DIGIT "." 1*DIGIT | If MIME-Version is emitted or validated, value must match 1*DIGIT "." 1*DIGIT. |
| `19.3-19.6-19` | informative | MIME version "1.0" is the default MIME-Version value for HTTP/1.1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MIME version "1.0" is the default for use in HTTP/1.1. | If ever emitting MIME-Version, use "1.0". |
| `19.3-19.6-20` | informative | HTTP/1.1 message parsing and semantics are governed by RFC 2616, not the MIME specification, even when MIME-Version is present. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP/1.1 message parsing and semantics are defined by this document and not the MIME specification. | Never switch to MIME parsing rules (folding, CTE, line limits) based on MIME-Version presence. |
| `19.3-19.6-21` | informative | HTTP permits CRLF, bare CR, and bare LF as line breaks inside text-type entity content. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP allows CRLF, bare CR, and bare LF to indicate a line break within text content when a message is transmitted over HTTP. | Do not reject or normalize bare CR/LF inside received text bodies; deliver body bytes as-is. |
| `19.3-19.6-22` | SHOULD | A proxy or gateway from HTTP to a strict MIME environment should translate text-media-type line breaks to canonical CRLF. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | SHOULD translate all line breaks within the text media types described in section 3.7.1 of this document to the RFC 2049 canonical form of CRLF | N/A — proxy/gateway obligation only. |
| `19.3-19.6-23` | RECOMMENDED | Canonical form (CRLF line breaks) is recommended for any HTTP content protected by cryptographic checksums, since conversion breaks checksums. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the canonical form is recommended for any content that uses such checksums in HTTP | When sending checksummed text bodies (e.g. Content-MD5-style integrity), emit CRLF canonical form. |
| `19.3-19.6-24` | SHOULD | Proxies and gateways from other protocols should ensure any Date header conforms to an HTTP/1.1 date format, rewriting if necessary. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Proxies and gateways from other protocols SHOULD ensure that any Date header field present in a message conforms to one of the HTTP/1.1 formats and rewrite the date if necessary. | N/A — proxy/gateway obligation only. |
| `19.3-19.6-25` | MUST | Proxies/gateways from HTTP to MIME-compliant protocols must either change Content-Type or decode the entity-body before forwarding, because MIME lacks Content-Encoding. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | proxies and gateways from HTTP to MIME-compliant protocols MUST either change the value of the Content-Type header field or decode the entity-body before forwarding the message. | N/A — proxy/gateway obligation only. |
| `19.3-19.6-26` | MUST | Proxies/gateways from MIME protocols to HTTP must remove any non-identity Content-Transfer-Encoding before delivering the response to an HTTP client. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Proxies and gateways from MIME-compliant protocols to HTTP MUST remove any non-identity CTE ("quoted-printable" or "base64") encoding prior to delivering the response message to an HTTP client. | Client need not implement quoted-printable/base64 CTE decoding; gateways must strip it first. |
| `19.3-19.6-27` | SHOULD | A proxy or gateway from HTTP to a MIME protocol should label data with an appropriate Content-Transfer-Encoding when that improves safe transport. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Such a proxy or gateway SHOULD label the data with an appropriate Content-Transfer-Encoding if doing so will improve the likelihood of safe transport over the destination protocol. | N/A — proxy/gateway obligation only. |
| `19.3-19.6-28` | MUST | Proxies/gateways must remove any transfer-coding before forwarding a message via a MIME-compliant protocol. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Proxies/gateways MUST remove any transfer-coding prior to forwarding a message via a MIME-compliant protocol. | N/A — proxy/gateway obligation only. |
| `19.3-19.6-29` | informative | Chunked decoding process: read chunk-size (with optional chunk-extension) and CRLF, then repeatedly read chunk-data plus CRLF, accumulate length, until a zero chunk-size. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | read chunk-size, chunk-extension (if any) and CRLF while (chunk-size > 0) { read chunk-data and CRLF append chunk-data to entity-body length := length + chunk-size read chunk-size and CRLF } | Chunked body decoder must follow this loop: size line (ignore extensions), exact chunk-data, trailing CRLF, until size 0. |
| `19.3-19.6-30` | informative | After the last chunk, read trailer entity-headers until an empty line, append them to the existing headers, set Content-Length to the accumulated length, and remove "chunked" from Transfer-Encoding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | read entity-header while (entity-header not empty) { append entity-header to existing header fields read entity-header } Content-Length := length Remove "chunked" from Transfer-Encoding | After final chunk: parse trailers to empty line, merge into header set, record decoded length, drop "chunked" from Transfer-Encoding when re-representing. |
| `19.3-19.6-31` | informative | HTTP has no MIME line length limitation and does not fold long lines. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Since HTTP does not have this limitation, HTTP does not fold long lines. | Never fold emitted header lines; accept arbitrarily long received header lines (bounded only by explicit local limits). |
| `19.3-19.6-32` | informative | HTTP transports all message-bodies as opaque payload and does not interpret body content or MIME header lines contained within it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP transports all message-bodies as payload (see section 3.7.2) and does not interpret the content or any MIME header lines that might be contained therein. | Treat received bodies as opaque bytes; never parse MIME structure inside the body at the protocol layer. |
| `19.3-19.6-33` | informative | Implementations cannot rely on the presence of, or interoperability with, the additional features (RFC 1945/2068 extras) in other HTTP/1.1 applications. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Implementors are advised to be aware of these features, but cannot rely upon their presence in, or interoperability with, other HTTP/1.1 applications. | Do not depend on peers supporting Keep-Alive, Content-Disposition, or other 19.5 extras; degrade gracefully. |
| `19.3-19.6-34` | informative | Grammar: content-disposition = "Content-Disposition" ":" disposition-type *( ";" disposition-parm ). <br>_(origin,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | content-disposition = "Content-Disposition" ":" disposition-type *( ";" disposition-parm ) | If Content-Disposition is parsed, split as disposition-type followed by semicolon-separated parameters. |
| `19.3-19.6-35` | informative | Grammar: disposition-type = "attachment" \| disp-extension-token. <br>_(origin,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | disposition-type = "attachment" \| disp-extension-token | Recognize "attachment" specifically; treat unknown disposition types as extension tokens (token grammar). |
| `19.3-19.6-36` | informative | Grammar: disposition-parm = filename-parm \| disp-extension-parm; filename-parm = "filename" "=" quoted-string; disp-extension-parm = token "=" (token \| quoted-string). <br>_(origin,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | disposition-parm = filename-parm \| disp-extension-parm filename-parm = "filename" "=" quoted-string | Parse filename as a quoted-string; accept extension params as token "=" (token \| quoted-string). |
| `19.3-19.6-37` | SHOULD NOT | The receiving user agent should not respect any directory path information present in the filename-parm parameter. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The receiving user agent SHOULD NOT respect any directory path information present in the filename-parm parameter | If exposing/using Content-Disposition filename, strip all directory components (path traversal defense). |
| `19.3-19.6-38` | SHOULD | The filename from Content-Disposition should be treated as a terminal path component only. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The filename SHOULD be treated as a terminal component only. | Use only the final path component of the filename parameter when saving content. |
| `19.3-19.6-39` | informative | With application/octet-stream plus Content-Disposition, the implied suggestion is the user agent should not display the response but offer save-as. <br>_(user-agent [not-client])_ | the implied suggestion is that the user agent should not display the response, but directly enter a `save response as...' dialog | N/A for a library client — interactive UA display guidance only. |
| `19.3-19.6-40` | informative | HTTP/1.1 servers are expected to recognize the Request-Line format of HTTP/0.9, 1.0, and 1.1 requests. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | recognize the format of the Request-Line for HTTP/0.9, 1.0, and 1.1 requests | N/A — server expectation. |
| `19.3-19.6-41` | informative | HTTP/1.1 servers are expected to understand any valid request in HTTP/0.9, 1.0, or 1.1 format. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | understand any valid request in the format of HTTP/0.9, 1.0, or 1.1 | N/A — server expectation. |
| `19.3-19.6-42` | informative | HTTP/1.1 servers are expected to respond with a message in the same major version the client used. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | respond appropriately with a message in the same major version used by the client | Client may expect the response major version to match its request's major version, but must not hard-require it. |
| `19.3-19.6-43` | informative | HTTP/1.1 clients are expected to recognize the Status-Line format of both HTTP/1.0 and HTTP/1.1 responses. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | recognize the format of the Status-Line for HTTP/1.0 and 1.1 responses | Status-line parser must accept HTTP/1.0 as well as HTTP/1.1 version tokens. |
| `19.3-19.6-44` | informative | HTTP/1.1 clients are expected to understand any valid response in HTTP/0.9, 1.0, or 1.1 format. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | understand any valid response in the format of HTTP/0.9, 1.0, or 1.1 | Response parser must handle 1.0 responses (and decide policy for status-line-less 0.9 bodies) without failing. |
| `19.3-19.6-45` | informative | In most HTTP/1.0 implementations, the connection is opened by the client per request and closed by the server after sending the response. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | each connection is established by the client prior to the request and closed by the server after sending the response | For HTTP/1.0 responses without explicit length, frame the body by connection close and assume non-persistence. |
| `19.3-19.6-46` | MUST | Both clients and servers must support the Host request-header. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Both clients and servers MUST support the Host request-header. | Client must implement the Host header (generation, correct host[:port] value). |
| `19.3-19.6-47` | MUST | A client sending an HTTP/1.1 request must include a Host header. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that sends an HTTP/1.1 request MUST send a Host header. | Emit Host in every HTTP/1.1 request without exception; reject/auto-fill requests lacking it. |
| `19.3-19.6-48` | MUST | Servers must respond 400 (Bad Request) to an HTTP/1.1 request lacking a Host request-header. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers MUST report a 400 (Bad Request) error if an HTTP/1.1 request does not include a Host request-header. | Server-side rule; client-side consequence is covered by always sending Host (fact 47). |
| `19.3-19.6-49` | MUST | Servers must accept absolute URIs in the Request-Line. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers MUST accept absolute URIs. | Server obligation; client may rely on it when emitting absoluteURI form (e.g. via proxies). |
| `19.3-19.6-50` | informative | Persistent connections in HTTP/1.0 are not the default and must be explicitly negotiated. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Persistent connections in HTTP/1.0 are explicitly negotiated as they are not the default behavior. | Assume close-after-response for HTTP/1.0 peers unless persistence was explicitly negotiated. |
| `19.3-19.6-51` | informative | HTTP/1.0 clients must be prevented from using Keep-Alive when talking to proxies, because old proxies forward it erroneously and hang. <br>_(client,proxy [CLIENT])_ | The result is that HTTP/1.0 clients must be prevented from using Keep-Alive when talking to proxies. | Never send Connection: Keep-Alive on requests routed through a proxy. |
| `19.3-19.6-52` | informative | Persistent connections are the default for HTTP/1.1 messages; Connection: close is the keyword for declaring non-persistence. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Persistent connections are the default for HTTP/1.1 messages; we introduce a new keyword (Connection: close) for declaring non-persistence. | Keep connections open by default for HTTP/1.1; emit/honor Connection: close to end persistence. |
| `19.3-19.6-53` | informative | A quality value of zero indicates the client refuses ("does not want") that representation. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Quality Values of zero should indicate that "I don't want something" to allow clients to refuse a representation. | When emitting Accept*/TE headers, q=0 means the coding/type is unacceptable; treat received q=0 likewise. |
| `19.3-19.6-54` | informative | Proxies are required to upgrade requests to the highest protocol version they support. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Require proxies to upgrade requests to highest protocol version they support | N/A — proxy obligation (changelog restatement of RFC 2145/section 3.1). |
| `19.3-19.6-55` | informative | Request retry after failure is a user-agent behavior, not a requirement on clients in general. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Made it clear that user-agents should retry requests, not "clients" in general. | Automatic retry policy belongs at the user-agent/application layer (client/retry), not the raw transport. |
| `19.3-19.6-56` | informative | The requirement for clients to ignore unexpected 100 (Continue) responses was generalized to all 1xx responses. <br>_(client,proxy [CLIENT])_ | Converted requirements for clients to ignore unexpected 100 (Continue) responses, and for proxies to forward 100 responses, into a general requirement for 1xx responses. | Response parser must consume and ignore any unexpected 1xx interim response and keep waiting for the final response. |
| `19.3-19.6-57` | MUST NOT | An origin server must not wait for the request body before sending a required 100 (Continue) response. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the origin server MUST NOT wait for the request body before it sends a required 100 (Continue) response | N/A — origin-server obligation (changelog restatement of section 8.2). |
| `19.3-19.6-58` | informative | A server is allowed (not required) to omit the 100 (Continue) response if it has already seen some of the request body. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Allow, rather than require, a server to omit 100 (Continue) if it has already seen some of the request body. | When using Expect: 100-continue, do not block indefinitely on a 100 — proceed after timeout/partial-body send. |
| `19.3-19.6-59` | informative | Proxies should be able to add Content-Length when appropriate. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Proxies should be able to add Content-Length when appropriate. | N/A — proxy capability (changelog restatement of section 13.5.2). |
| `19.3-19.6-60` | informative | The PATCH, LINK, and UNLINK methods were defined in prior versions but not commonly implemented and are dropped from this specification. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The PATCH, LINK, UNLINK methods were defined but not commonly implemented in previous versions of this specification. | Do not assume peer support if callers emit PATCH/LINK/UNLINK; they are extension methods under RFC 2616. |
| `19.3-19.6-61` | informative | The Alternates, Content-Version, Derived-From, Link, URI, Public, and Content-Base header fields from prior versions were not commonly implemented and are dropped. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The Alternates, Content-Version, Derived-From, Link, URI, Public and Content-Base header fields were defined in previous versions of this specification, but not commonly implemented. | Treat these headers as unknown extension headers; attach no protocol semantics and never rely on them. |
